Cardinal invariants of idealized Miller null sets
Aleksander Cieślak, Takehiko Gappo, Arturo Martínez-Celis, Takashi Yamazoe

Abstract
. This paper provides an extensive study of the ℐ \mathscr{I} -Miller null ideals M ℐ M_{\mathscr{I}} , σ \sigma -ideals on the Baire space parametrized by ideals ℐ \mathscr{I} on countable sets. These σ \sigma -ideals are associated to the idealized versions of Miller forcing in the same way that the meager ideal is associated to Cohen forcing. We compute the cardinal invariants of M ℐ M_{\mathscr{I}} for typical examples of Borel ideals ℐ \mathscr{I} and show that Cichoń’s Maximum can be extended by adding the uniformity and covering numbers of M ℐ M_{\mathscr{I}} for different ideals ℐ \mathscr{I} .



2020 Mathematics Subject Classification
03E05, 03E17, 03E35

1.
Introduction

Cichoń’s diagram (
Figure

1
) illustrates the relationship of well-known cardinal invariants of the continuum, which we call
Cichoń characteristics
.

\add(\mathcal{N})
\cov(\mathcal{N})
\add(\mathcal{M})
\mathfrak{b}
\non(\mathcal{M})
\cov(\mathcal{M})
\mathfrak{d}
\cof(\mathcal{M})
\non(\mathcal{N})
\cof(\mathcal{N})
\aleph_{1}
\mathfrak{c}

Figure 1.
Cichoń’s diagram. An arrow
\mathfrak{x}\to\mathfrak{y}
denotes that
\mathfrak{x}\leq\mathfrak{y}
holds.

All of them are cardinal invariants associated with
\sigma
-ideals on Polish spaces; eight of them are defined for the Lebesgue null ideal
\mathcal{N}
or the meager ideal
\mathcal{M}
. The bounding number
\mathfrak{b}
and the dominating number
\mathfrak{d}
are related to the ideal
K_{\sigma}
of
\sigma
-compact subsets of the Baire space:

\mathfrak{b}=\add(K_{\sigma})=\non(K_{\sigma})\text{ and }\mathfrak{d}=\cov(K_{\sigma})=\cof(K_{\sigma}).

In addition,
\aleph_{1}
and
\mathfrak{c}
correspond to the
\sigma
-ideal
[\mathbb{R}]^{\leq{\aleph_{0}}}
of countable sets of the reals:

\aleph_{1}=\add([\mathbb{R}]^{\leq{\aleph_{0}}})=\non([\mathbb{R}]^{\leq{\aleph_{0}}})\text{ and }\mathfrak{c}=\cov([\mathbb{R}]^{\leq{\aleph_{0}}})=\cof([\mathbb{R}]^{\leq{\aleph_{0}}}).

Thus, Cichoń characteristics are the cardinal invariants of the four
\sigma
-ideals
\mathcal{N},\mathcal{M},K_{\sigma}
, and
[\mathbb{R}]^{\leq{\aleph_{0}}}
, which we call
Cichoń
\sigma
-ideals
.

Cichoń’s maximum
is a maximal separation constellation of Cichoń’s diagram, where all Cichoń characteristics have distinct values except for the two dependent numbers

\add(\mathcal{M})=\min\{\mathfrak{b},\cov(\mathcal{M})\}\text{ and }\cof(\mathcal{M})=\max\{\mathfrak{d},\non(\mathcal{M})\}.

Goldstern, Kellner and Shelah
[
GKS19
]
constructed a model of Cichoń’s maximum assuming four strongly compact cardinals. Later, with Mejía
[
GKMS22
]
, the large cardinal assumption was eliminated.
From the perspective of
\sigma
-ideals, their result would mean that the four Cichoń
\sigma
-ideals are so different that their cardinal invariants can have distinct values at the same time.
Moreover, it is possible to force Cichoń’s maximum together with other cardinal invariants taking pairwise different values. Several such examples are known: Goldstern, Kellner, Mejía, and Shelah added to Cichoń’s maximum the cardinal invariants
\mathfrak{m},\mathfrak{p},\mathfrak{h}
in
[
GKMS21
]
and
\mathfrak{s},\mathfrak{r}
in
[
GKMS21a
]
. The fourth author forced Cichoń’s maximum with the evasion and prediction numbers in
[
Yam25
]
. Cardona, Repický, and Shelah
[
CRS25
]
forced Cichoń’s maximum together with the constant evasion and constant prediction numbers. The fourth author forced Cichoń’s maximum together with the covering and uniformity number of the closed null ideal
\mathcal{E}
in
[
Yam24
]
.

Our main aim is to systematically extend Cichoń’s maximum to include cardinal invariants associated with other
\sigma
-ideals. In this paper, we focus on the following family of
\sigma
-ideals on Polish spaces parametrized with ideals on countable sets:

Definition 1.1
.

Let
\mathscr{I}
be an ideal on a countable set
X
. We define the
\mathscr{I}
-Miller null ideal

M_{\mathscr{I}}
as the
\sigma
-ideal on
X^{\omega}
generated by sets of the form

M_{\phi}=\{x\in X^{\omega}:\forall^{\infty}n<\omega~x(n)\in\phi(x\upharpoonright n)\},

where
\phi\colon X^{<\omega}\to\mathscr{I}
.

Definition 1.2
.

Let
\mathscr{I}
be an ideal on a coutable set
X
. We define
K_{\mathscr{I}}
as the
\sigma
-ideal on
X^{\omega}
generated by sets of the form

K_{\phi}=\{x\in X^{\omega}:\forall^{\infty}n<\omega~x(n)\in\phi(n)\},

where
\phi\colon\omega\to\mathscr{I}
.

Through the study of cardinal invariants of
M_{\mathscr{I}}
and
K_{\mathscr{I}}
for various Borel ideals
\mathscr{I}
, we will see how much these
\sigma
-ideals
M_{\mathscr{I}}
and
K_{\mathscr{I}}
are similar to/different from Cichoń
\sigma
-ideals.
It turns out that for some ideals
\mathscr{I}
, the cardinal invariants of
M_{\mathscr{I}}
and
K_{\mathscr{I}}
are totally characterized using Cichoń characteristics, while for some other ideals
\mathscr{I}
, it is possible to extend Cichoń’s maximum to include some of the cardinal invariants of
M_{\mathscr{I}}
and
K_{\mathscr{I}}
.

Recall that the poset of all nonmeager Borel sets is forcing equivalent to Cohen forcing and that the poset of all Borel sets with a positive Lebesgue measure is forcing equivalent to Random forcing.
The ideal
M_{\mathscr{I}}
is related to some natural tree forcing in the same way: Given an ideal
\mathscr{I}
on a countable set
X
, we say that a tree
T\subseteq X^{<\omega}
is an
\mathscr{I}
-Miller tree
if for every
\sigma\in T
there exists
\tau\in T
with
\sigma\subseteq\tau
and

\mathrm{succ}_{T}(\tau):=\{i\in X:\tau^{\frown}\langle i\rangle\in T\}\in\mathscr{I}^{+}.

The
\mathscr{I}
-Miller forcing
, denoted by
\mathbb{M}_{\mathscr{I}}
, is the forcing poset of all
\mathscr{I}
-Miller trees ordered by inclusion. Note that
\mathbb{M}_{\Fin}=\mathbb{M}
is the standard Miller forcing, where
\Fin=[\omega]^{<\omega}
denotes the Fréchet ideal. The forcings
\mathbb{M}_{\mathscr{I}}
were studied by Sabok and Zapletal
[
SZ11
]
, where they showed the relationship between
\mathscr{I}
-Miller trees and the
\mathscr{I}
-Miller null ideal.

Lemma 1.3

(Sabok–Zapletal
[
SZ11
]
)
.

Let
X
be a countable set. For every analytic subset
A\subseteq X^{\omega}
, either
A\in M_{\mathscr{I}}
or there is
T\in\mathbb{M}_{\mathscr{I}}
such that
[T]\subseteq A
.

This lemma justifies our terminology. It also implies that the poset of all
M_{\mathscr{I}}
-positive Borel subsets of
\omega^{\omega}
is forcing equivalent to
\mathbb{M}_{\mathscr{I}}
. The ideal
K_{\mathscr{I}}
does not seem to correspond to any standard forcing notion. We introduce a tree forcing
\mathbb{K}_{\mathscr{I}}
that is forcing equivalent to the poset of all
K_{\mathscr{I}}
-positive Borel sets, and this forcing notion seems new. This is why we did not give a particular name to
K_{\mathscr{I}}
; the letter
K
was chosen as it is a direct generalization of the ideal
K_{\sigma}
.
It appears harder to study forcing properties of
\mathbb{K}_{\mathscr{I}}
compared to
\mathbb{M}_{\mathscr{I}}
, and this fact sometimes prevents us to compute the exact value of
K_{\mathscr{I}}
, even when we could get the exact value of
M_{\mathscr{I}}
.

A systematic study of the cardinal invariants of
M_{\mathscr{I}}
was initiated in
[
CM25
]
by the first and third authors.
However, the cardinal invariants of
M_{\mathscr{I}}
and
K_{\mathscr{I}}
implicitly appeared in previous literature in different contexts. Forcing results in the aforementioned paper by Sabok–Zapletal
[
SZ11
]
have direct corollaries on cardinal invariants of
M_{\mathscr{I}}
, e.g.

\non(M_{\mathrm{nwd}})=\non(\mathcal{M})\text{ and }\non(M_{\mathcal{S}})\geq\cov(\mathcal{N}),

where
\mathrm{nwd}
is the nowhere dense ideal and
\mathcal{S}
is the Solecki ideal (cf.
4.34
,
4.39
). In
[
Paw00
]
, Pawlikowski studied the ideal
K_{\mathcal{Z}}
, where
\mathcal{Z}
is the asymptotic density zero ideal on
\omega
, and proved that a poset adding a perfect set of random reals forces
\omega^{\omega}\cap V\in K_{\mathcal{Z}}
. Another result in the paper immediately implies that

\non(K_{\mathcal{Z}})\leq\max\{\mathfrak{b},\non(\mathcal{E})\}\text{ and }\cov(K_{\mathcal{Z}})\geq\min\{\mathfrak{d},\cov(\mathcal{E})\},

where
\mathcal{E}
denotes the
\sigma
-ideal on
2^{\omega}
generated by closed null sets (cf.
4.50
). Also,
\non(K_{\mathscr{I}})
and
\cov(K_{\mathscr{I}})
appear as one of the slalom numbers in
[
Sup23
]
and
[
CGM+24
]
. According to the notation in
[
CGM+24
]
, we have

\non(K_{\mathscr{I}})=\mathfrak{sl}_{\mathrm{t}}^{\bot}(\mathscr{I},\mathrm{Fin})\text{ and }\cov(K_{\mathscr{I}})=\mathfrak{sl}_{\mathrm{t}}(\mathscr{I},\mathrm{Fin}).

Slalom numbers are related to topological selection principles. For example, in
[
Sup23
]
, Šupina showed that
\cov(K_{\mathscr{I}})
is the least size of non-
S_{1}(\mathscr{I}\mathchar 45\Gamma,\mathcal{O})
Hausdorff spaces. He also proved

\cov(K_{\mathscr{I}})\geq\min\{\mathfrak{d},\cov^{*}(\mathscr{I})\}.

Moreover, one can see uniformity and covering numbers of
M_{\mathscr{I}}
and
K_{\mathscr{I}}
as idealized version of evasion/prediction numbers. Blass studied many variants of the evasion number
\mathfrak{e}
in
[
Bla10
]
and, according to his terminologies,
\non(M_{\mathscr{I}})
is the evasion number for global adaptive predictions with values in
\mathscr{I}
, while
\non(K_{\mathscr{I}})
is the evasion number for global non-adaptive predictions with values in
\mathscr{I}
.

The first step to compute the cardinal invariants of the
\sigma
-ideals
M_{\mathscr{I}}
and
K_{\mathscr{I}}
is to clarify their connections with those of the original ideal
\mathscr{I}
. Such a connection was already noticed in
[
CM25
]
and we refine their results to get the following.

Theorem A

(
4.9
,
4.11
,
4.14
)
.

Let
\mathscr{I}
be an ideal on a countable set. Then the following hold:

(1)

\add(M_{\mathscr{I}})=\add(K_{\mathscr{I}})=\min\{\mathfrak{b},\add^{*}_{\omega}(\mathscr{I})\}
.

(2)

\mathfrak{b}\leq\non(K_{\mathscr{I}})\leq\non(M_{\mathscr{I}})\leq\max\{\mathfrak{b},\non^{*}_{\omega}(\mathscr{I})\}
.

(3)

\min\{\mathfrak{d},\cov^{*}(\mathscr{I})\}\leq\cov(M_{\mathscr{I}})\leq\cov(K_{\mathscr{I}})\leq\mathfrak{d}
.

(4)

\cof(M_{\mathscr{I}})=\cof(K_{\mathscr{I}})=\max\{\mathfrak{d},\cof^{*}_{\omega}(\mathscr{I})\}
.

Here,
\add^{*}_{\omega}(\mathscr{I}),\non^{*}_{\omega}(\mathscr{I})
and
\cof^{*}_{\omega}(\mathscr{I})
are the “
\omega
-versions” of
\add^{*}(\mathscr{I}),\non^{*}(\mathscr{I})
and
\cof^{*}(\mathscr{I})
. See
3.1
. These cardinal invariants (for ultrafilters) were introduced by Brendle–Shelah
[
BS99
]
to compute the cardinal invariants of null ideals of Laver and Mathias type forcings associated to ultrafilters. While the usual
*
-numbers have been extensively studied as surveyed in
[
HH07
]
, these
\omega
-versions have been ignored for a long time. The only papers discussing the
\omega
-versions for Borel ideals are
[
CM25
]
and
[
FK25
]
.
1
1

1

In
[
FK25
]
, they studied
\add^{*}_{\omega}(\mathscr{I})
, but not
\non^{*}_{\omega}(\mathscr{I})
and
\cof^{*}_{\omega}(\mathscr{I})
.
In this work, we continue this line of research, and compute additional values of these
\omega
-versions of
*
-numbers for even more examples of Borel ideals, see
Table

1
.

Table 1.
Values of cardinal invariants associated with Borel ideals.

The
\omega
-versions of
*
-numbers can be seen as improved versions of the original ones. Recall that the original
*
-numbers do not necessarily behave well for non-P-ideals:
\add^{*}(\mathscr{I})=\omega
for every non-P-ideal
\mathscr{I}
and
\non^{*}(\mathscr{I})=\omega
for every Borel ideal
\mathscr{I}
that is not Katětov reducible to
\mathcal{ED}_{\mathrm{fin}}
. While
\add^{*}_{\omega}(\mathscr{I})=\add^{*}(\mathscr{I})
and
\non^{*}_{\omega}(\mathscr{I})=\non^{*}(\mathscr{I})
hold for all P-ideals
\mathscr{I}
, the
\omega
-versions take nontrivial values for non-P-ideals. For example, we show that
\add^{*}_{\omega}(\mathscr{I})\geq\add_{t}(\mathcal{N})
for some
F_{\sigma}
non-P-ideal
\mathscr{I}
, where
\add_{t}(\mathcal{N})
denotes the transitive additivity of the null ideal (
3.15
,
3.16
). As a corollary, we get the consistency of
\add^{*}_{\omega}(\mathscr{I})>\mathfrak{d}
for such an ideal
\mathscr{I}
. This result should be compared to the fact that there are only three known values of
\add^{*}(\mathscr{I})
so far, which are
\omega,\add(\mathcal{N})
and
\mathfrak{b}
. We also note that the consistency of
\cof^{*}_{\omega}(\mathscr{I})<\mathfrak{b}
for some
F_{\sigma}
non-P-ideal
\mathscr{I}
was essentially shown in
[
HRZ14
]
. Even though
\add^{*}_{\omega}(\mathscr{I})
and
\cof^{*}_{\omega}(\mathscr{I})
do not have dual definitions, our proof looks like dual to the argument in
[
HRZ14
]
. So, by considering
\omega
-versions of
*
-numbers, we can naturally extend duality between additivity and cofinality even for non-P-ideals. A similar duality also appears between
\non^{*}_{\omega}(\mathscr{I})
and
\cov^{*}(\mathscr{I})
in our computation of
\non^{*}_{\omega}(\mathcal{S})
and
\non^{*}_{\omega}(\mathcal{ED})
.

While the additivity and cofinality of
M_{\mathscr{I}}
and
K_{\mathscr{I}}
can be computed from
\add^{*}_{\omega}(\mathscr{I})
and
\cof^{*}_{\omega}(\mathscr{I})
, the uniformity and covering numbers of
M_{\mathscr{I}}
and
K_{\mathscr{I}}
have very complicated patterns. Our
\mathsf{ZFC}
-provable results on
\non(M_{\mathscr{I}})
and
\non(K_{\mathscr{I}})
are summarized in
Table

1
, but let us highlight some of them by stating here:

Theorem B
.

(1)

(
4.23
and
4.29
)

\non(M_{\mathrm{Fin}\otimes\mathrm{Fin}})=\mathfrak{e}^{\mathrm{const}}_{\leq}(2)
and
\cov(M_{\mathrm{Fin}\otimes\mathrm{Fin}})=\mathfrak{v}^{\mathrm{const}}_{\leq}(2)
.
On the other hand,
\non(K_{\mathrm{Fin}\otimes\mathrm{Fin}})=\mathfrak{b}
and
\cov(K_{\mathrm{Fin}\otimes\mathrm{Fin}})=\mathfrak{d}
.

(2)

(
4.40
,
4.48
)

\non(M_{\mathcal{S}})=\non(K_{\mathcal{S}})=\max\{\mathfrak{b},\cov_{\omega}(\mathcal{N})\}
and
\cov(M_{\mathcal{S}})=\cov(K_{\mathcal{S}})=\min\{\mathfrak{d},\non(\mathcal{N})\}
.

(3)

(
4.54
)

\non(M_{\mathcal{Z}})=\max\{\mathfrak{b},\non(\mathcal{E})\}
and
\cov(M_{\mathcal{Z}})=\min\{\mathfrak{d},\cov(\mathcal{E})\}
.

In
B
(1),
\mathfrak{e}^{\mathrm{const}}_{\leq}(2)
and
\mathfrak{v}^{\mathrm{const}}_{\leq}(2)
denote variants of constant evasion and prediction numbers introduced by Kamo
[
Kam00
]
. Such variations are considered in
[
CR25
]
. It is known that
\mathfrak{e}^{\mathrm{const}}_{\leq}(2)
and
\mathfrak{v}^{\mathrm{const}}_{\leq}(2)
are consistently different from
\mathfrak{b}
and
\mathfrak{d}
, respectively. Thus,
\mathrm{Fin}\otimes\mathrm{Fin}
is an example of ideals
\mathscr{I}
such that
\non(M_{\mathscr{I}})
and
\non(K_{\mathscr{I}})
are consistently different. Since we also know that
\non^{*}_{\omega}(\mathrm{Fin}\otimes\mathrm{Fin})=\mathfrak{d}
,
\mathrm{Fin}\otimes\mathrm{Fin}
is also an example of ideals
\mathscr{I}
such that
\non(M_{\mathscr{I}})<\max\{\mathfrak{b},\non^{*}(\mathscr{I})\}
is consistent, which should be compared to
A
(
2
). In
B
(2),
\cov_{\omega}(\mathcal{N})
denotes the least size of a family
\mathcal{F}
of Lebesgue null sets such that any countable set
A
of reals is included by some
N\in\mathcal{F}
, which turns out to be equal to
\non^{*}_{\omega}(\mathcal{S})
. So,
\mathcal{S}
is an example of ideals
\mathscr{I}
such that
\non(M_{\mathscr{I}})=\non(K_{\mathscr{I}})=\non\{\mathfrak{b},\non^{*}_{\omega}(\mathscr{I})\}
is provable. Moreover, combining
B
(3) with results of Raghavan
[
Rag20
]
, we get new bounds for
\non^{*}(\mathcal{Z})
and
\cov^{*}(\mathcal{Z})
:

\non(\mathcal{E})\leq\non^{*}(\mathcal{Z}),\cov^{*}(\mathcal{Z})\leq\cov(\mathcal{E})\text{ and }\cov^{*}(\mathcal{Z})\leq\non^{*}(\mathcal{Z}).

See
4.58
. It is still unknown whether
\non(K_{\mathcal{Z}})=\max\{\mathfrak{b},\non(\mathcal{E})\}
and
\cov(K_{\mathcal{Z}})=\min\{\mathfrak{d},\cov(\mathcal{E})\}
hold or not.

We also get numerous consistency results that are not direct consequences of
\mathsf{ZFC}
-provable inequalities. In a similar way as
B
(1), we show that

\max\{\mathfrak{b},\mathfrak{e}^{\mathrm{const}}(2)\}\leq\non(M_{\mathcal{ED}})\text{ and }\max\{\mathfrak{b},\mathfrak{e}^{\mathrm{const}}_{b}(2)\}\leq\non(M_{\mathcal{ED}_{\mathrm{fin}}})

for any increasing function
b\in{\omega}^{\omega}
(
4.20
,
4.21
). The converses of these inequalities are not
\mathsf{ZFC}
-provable by the following results.

Theorem C
.

The following (and their duals) consistently hold:

(1)

(
5.35
)

\max\{\mathfrak{b},\mathfrak{e}^{\mathrm{const}}(2)\}<\non(M_{\mathcal{ED}})
.

(2)

(
5.41
)

\non(M_{\mathrm{Fin}\otimes\mathrm{Fin}})<\non(K_{\mathcal{ED}_{\mathrm{fin}}})
.

(3)

(
5.42
)

\max\{\mathfrak{b},\mathfrak{e}^{\mathrm{const}}_{b}(2)\}<\non(K_{\mathcal{ED}_{\mathrm{fin}}})
for any increasing
b\in{\omega}^{\omega}
.

These results suggest that
\non(M_{\mathcal{ED}})
and
\non(M_{\mathcal{ED}_{\mathrm{fin}}})
cannot be characterized by constant evasion numbers, unlike
\non(M_{\mathrm{Fin}\otimes\mathrm{Fin}})=\mathfrak{e}^{\mathrm{const}}_{\leq}(2)
.
The main method to prove (1) is the
Fréchet-limit (Fr-limit)
method, introduced by Mejía
[
Mej19
]
, which preserves
\mathfrak{b}
small through the forcing iteration. To show (2), we introduce a variant of this method, called
closed-Fr-limit
method, which preserves
\non(M_{\mathrm{Fin}\otimes\mathrm{Fin}})
small. (3) is obtained by combining the arguments of (1) and (2).

In addition to the separations into two values in
C
, we could construct a model of extended Cichoń’s maximum with uniformity and covering numbers of
M_{\mathscr{I}}
and
K_{\mathscr{I}}
for some ideal
\mathscr{I}
. Consequently, we encounter the following ideals, which are closely related to the linear growth ideal
\mathscr{I}_{L}
(cf.
[
Hru11
, Page 56]
,
[
BM14
, Page 3]
):

\mathscr{J}_{L}\coloneqq\{A\subseteq\omega\times\omega:\exists k<\omega~\forall^{\infty}n<\omega~\lvert\{m<\omega\colon\langle n,m\rangle\in A\}\rvert\leq k\cdot i\},

and for
f\in{\omega}^{\omega}
with
\textstyle\lim_{n\to\infty}f(n)/2^{n}=0
,

\mathscr{I}_{L}^{f}\coloneqq\{A\subseteq\omega\colon\exists k<\omega~\forall^{\infty}n<\omega~\lvert A\cap[2^{n}-1,2^{n+1}-1)\rvert\leq k\cdot f(n)\}.

These ideals provide the classes of ideals
\mathscr{I}_{0},\mathscr{I}_{1}
such that cardinal invariants of
M_{\mathscr{I}_{0}}
and
K_{\mathscr{I}_{1}}
can be added to a model of Cichoń’s maximum with distinct values:

Theorem D

(
5.61
)
.

Let
\aleph_{1}\leq\theta_{1}\leq\cdots\leq\theta_{12}
be regular cardinals and
\theta_{\mathfrak{c}}
an infinite cardinal with
\theta_{\mathfrak{c}}\geq\theta_{12}
and
\theta_{\mathfrak{c}}^{\aleph_{0}}=\theta_{\mathfrak{c}}
. Then there exists a ccc poset that forces the separation constellation described in
Figure

2
.
Moreover, in the forcing extension, for ideals
\mathscr{I}_{0},\mathscr{I}_{1}
on countable sets such that
\mathscr{J}_{L}\leq_{\mathrm{K}}\mathscr{I}_{0}\leq_{\mathrm{K}}\mathrm{Fin}\otimes\mathrm{Fin}
and
\mathscr{I}_{L}^{f}\leq_{\mathrm{K}}\mathscr{I}_{1}\leq_{\mathrm{K}}\mathcal{Z}
for some
f\in\omega^{\omega}
such that
\textstyle\lim_{n\to\infty}f(n)=\infty
and
\textstyle\lim_{n\to\infty}f(n)/2^{n}=0
, we have:

\non(M_{\mathscr{I}_{0}})=\theta_{4},~\cov(M_{\mathscr{I}_{0}})=\theta_{9},

\non(K_{\mathscr{I}_{1}})=\non(M_{\mathscr{I}_{1}})=\theta_{5},~\cov(K_{\mathscr{I}_{1}})=\cov(M_{\mathscr{I}_{1}})=\theta_{8}.

This result implies a substantial difference of the
\sigma
-ideals
M_{\mathscr{I}_{0}}
and
K_{\mathscr{I}_{1}}
for such ideals
\mathscr{I}_{0},\mathscr{I}_{1}
from Cichoń
\sigma
-ideals.
The framework to construct a model of the extended Cichoń’s maximum in
D
is based on the fourth author’s work
[
Yam25
]
,
[
Yam24
]
. To construct such a model, we need to use a stronger method called
Ultrafilter-limit (UF-limit)
method than the Fr-limit method, introduced by Goldstern–Mejía–Shelah
[
GMS16
]
, to control
\mathfrak{b}
in a finer way. We also need the
closed-UF-limit
method, introduced by the fourth author
[
Yam24
]
, to control
\non(M_{\mathrm{Fin}\otimes\mathrm{Fin}})
. According to these necessities, we find the ideals
\mathscr{J}_{L}
and
\mathscr{I}^{L}_{f}
and the classes of ideals for
\mathscr{I}_{0},\mathscr{I}_{1}
in
D
.
These classes of ideals seem interesting in themselves. It turns out
\mathscr{J}_{L}
and
\mathscr{I}_{L}^{f}
are critical for some selective properties. The class of ideals for
\mathscr{I}_{0}
is considered in
[
DFGT21
]
and every tall non-pathological analytic P-ideal belongs to the class of ideals for
\mathscr{I}_{1}
. See
Section

6
.

represents any function
f\in{\omega}^{\omega}
such that
\textstyle\lim_{n\to\infty}f(n)=\infty
and
\textstyle\lim_{n\to\infty}f(n)/2^{n}=0
.

2.
Preliminaries

Given a set
X
,
\mathscr{I}\subseteq\mathcal{P}(X)
is an
ideal
if it is closed under taking finite unions and subsets. An ideal
\mathscr{I}
is called a
\sigma
-ideal
if it is closed under taking countable unions. We always assume that an ideal
\mathscr{I}
on
X
is
proper
in the sense that
X\notin\mathscr{I}
and
\mathscr{I}
contains every finite subset of
X
. An ideal
\mathscr{I}
on
X
is
tall
if every infinite subset of
X
contains an infinite set in
\mathscr{I}
.

Let
\mathscr{I}
be an ideal on
X
. We say that
A\subseteq X
is
\mathscr{I}
-positive
if
A\notin\mathscr{I}
. We write
\mathscr{I}^{+}=\mathcal{P}(X)\setminus\mathscr{I}
. The
dual filter
of
\mathscr{I}
is defined by
\mathscr{I}^{*}\coloneq\{A\subseteq X:X\setminus A\in\mathscr{I}\}
.

The following are well-known relations among ideals and partial orders.

Definition 2.1
.

Let
\mathscr{I},\mathscr{J}
be ideals on
X,Y
respectively.

(1)

\mathscr{I}
is
Katětov reducible
to
\mathscr{J}
, denoted by
\mathscr{I}\leq_{\mathrm{K}}\mathscr{J}
, if there is a function
f\colon Y\to X
such that for all
A\in\mathscr{I}
,
f^{-1}[A]\in\mathscr{J}
.

(2)

\mathscr{I}
is
Katětov–Blass reducible
to
\mathscr{J}
, denoted by
\mathscr{I}\leq_{\mathrm{KB}}\mathscr{J}
, if there is a finite-to-one function
f\colon Y\to X
such that for all
A\in\mathscr{I}
,
f^{-1}[A]\in\mathscr{J}
.

Definition 2.2
.

Let
\langle P,\leq\rangle
be a partially ordered set. A subset
X\subseteq P
is
bounded
if there is
a\in P
such that
\forall x\in X\,(x\leq a)
holds. We also say that
X
is
\sigma
-bounded
if there is a countable subset
A\subseteq P
such that
\forall x\in X\,\exists a\in A\,(x\leq a)
holds.

Let
P,Q
be partially ordered sets.

(1)

P
is
Tukey reducible
to
Q
, denoted by
P\preceq_{T}Q
, if there is
f\colon P\to Q
such that for any unbounded subset
X\subseteq P
,
f[X]
is a unbounded subset of
Q
.

(2)

P
is
\omega
-Tukey reducible
to
Q
, denoted by
P\preceq_{\omega T}Q
, if there is
f\colon P\to Q
such that for any
\sigma
-unbounded subset
X\subseteq P
,
f[X]
is a
\sigma
-unbounded subset of
Q
.

Trivially,
P\preceq_{T}Q
implies
P\preceq_{\omega T}Q
.

For any countable set
X
, its power set
\mathcal{P}(X)
, equipped with the product topology, is a Polish space homeomorphic to
2^{\omega}
. Thus, it makes sense to classify an ideal
\mathscr{I}\subseteq\mathcal{P}(X)
by its complexity, i.e.
F_{\sigma}
, Borel, or analytic. We now list the definitions of the Borel ideals that appear in
Table

1
.

•

The random graph ideal
\mathcal{R}
is the ideal on
\omega
generated by the homogeneous sets in Rado’s random graph.

•

The Solecki ideal
\mathcal{S}
is the ideal on the countable set

\Omega\coloneqq\{U\in\operatorname{Clopen}(2^{\omega}):U\text{ has Lebesgue measure }1/2\}

generated by subsets
A\subseteq\Omega
with non-empty intersection.

•

The nowhere dense ideal, denoted by
\mathrm{nwd}
, is the ideal on the rational numbers
\mathbb{Q}\cong 2^{<\omega}
of nowhere dense subsets of
2^{<\omega}
.

•

The convergent ideal, denoted by
\mathrm{conv}
, is the ideal on
\mathbb{Q}\cap[0,1]
generated by convergent sequences in
\mathbb{Q}\cap[0,1]
.

•

\Fin\otimes\Fin
is the Fubini product of two
\Fin
’s, where
\Fin
is the ideal of finite sets. In general, for ideals
\mathscr{I},\mathscr{J}
on
X,Y
respectively, their Fubini product
\mathscr{I}\otimes\mathscr{J}
is the ideal on
X\times Y
defined by

\mathscr{I}\otimes\mathscr{J}=\{A\subseteq X\times Y:\{x\in X:(A)_{x}\notin\mathscr{J}\}\in\mathscr{I}\},

where
(A)_{x}\coloneqq\{y\in Y:\langle x,y\rangle\in A\}
.

•

The eventually different ideal is defined by

\mathcal{ED}\coloneqq\{A\subseteq\omega\times\omega:\exists k<\omega~\forall^{\infty}n<\omega~|(A)_{n}|\leq k\}.

Also, we define
\mathcal{ED}_{\mathrm{fin}}\coloneqq\mathcal{ED}\mathpunct{\upharpoonright}\Delta
, where
\Delta\coloneqq\{(n,m)\in\omega\times\omega:m\leq n\}
.

•

Fix the interval partition
\bar{P}^{\mathrm{exp}}=(P_{i}^{\mathrm{exp}})_{i<\omega}
of
\omega
such that
\lvert P^{\mathrm{exp}}_{i}\rvert=2^{i}
.
The linear growth ideal
\mathscr{I}_{L}
is defined by

\mathscr{I}_{L}=\{A\subseteq\omega:\exists k<\omega\,\forall^{\infty}i<\omega\,(\lvert A\cap P^{\mathrm{exp}}_{i}\rvert\leq k\cdot i)\}

The polynomial growth ideal
\mathscr{I}_{P}
is

\mathscr{I}_{P}=\{A\subseteq\omega:\exists k<\omega\,\forall^{\infty}i<\omega\,(\lvert A\cap P^{\mathrm{exp}}_{i}\rvert\leq i^{k})\}

•

The asymptotic density zero ideal
\mathcal{Z}
is defined by

\mathcal{Z}=\left\{A\subseteq\omega:\lim_{n\to\infty}\frac{\lvert A\cap n\rvert}{n}=0\right\}.

Figure

3
illustrates the Katětov-Blass orders among the Borel ideals listed above (including
\Fin
). The details can be consulted in
[
Hru17
]
(
\mathcal{ED}_{\mathrm{fin}}\leq_{\mathrm{KB}}\mathscr{I}_{L}
is easy to see and the KB-reductions
\mathscr{I}_{L}\leq_{\mathrm{KB}}\mathscr{I}_{P}\leq_{\mathrm{KB}}\mathcal{Z}
follow from the inclusions
\mathscr{I}_{L}\subseteq\mathscr{I}_{P}\subseteq\mathcal{Z}
).

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{nwd}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Fin}\otimes\mathrm{Fin}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{Z}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathscr{I}_{P}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathscr{I}_{L}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{ED}_{\mathrm{fin}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{S}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{conv}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{ED}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{R}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Fin}

Figure 3.
Diagram of Borel ideals and Katětov-Blass orders. An arrow
\mathscr{I}\to\mathscr{J}
denotes that
\mathscr{I}\leq_{\mathrm{KB}}\mathscr{J}
holds.

Recall that an ideal
\mathscr{I}
is called a
P-ideal
if for any
\overline{A}\in[\mathscr{I}]^{\omega}
, there is an
B\in\mathscr{I}
such that
\forall A\in\overline{A}\,(A\subseteq^{*}B)
. The classes of
F_{\sigma}
ideals and analytic P-ideals (on countable sets) are especially important subclasses of Borel ideals, so let us state relevant definitions and basic results:

Definition 2.3
.

A function
\varphi\colon\mathcal{P}(\omega)\to\left[0,\infty\right]
is called a
submeasure
on
\omega
if:

•

\varphi(\emptyset)=0
,

•

A\subseteq B\Rightarrow\varphi(A)\leq\varphi(B)
,

•

\varphi(A\cup B)\leq\varphi(A)+\varphi(B)
.

A submeasure
\varphi
is
lower semi-continuous
if
\varphi(A)=\displaystyle\lim_{n\to\infty}\varphi(A\cap n)
for all
A\subseteq\omega
.

Fact 2.4
.

Let
\mathscr{I}
be an ideal on
\omega
.

•

(Mazur
[
Maz91
]
)

\mathscr{I}
is an
F_{\sigma}
ideal if and only if there is a lower semi-continuous submeasure
\varphi
such that

\mathscr{I}=\Fin(\varphi)\coloneqq\{A\subseteq\omega:\varphi(A)<\infty\}.

•

(Solecki
[
Sol99
]
)

\mathscr{I}
is an analytic P-ideal if and only if there is a lower semi-continuous submeasure
\varphi
such that

\mathscr{I}=\Exh(\varphi)\coloneqq\{A\subseteq\omega:\lim_{n\to\infty}\varphi(A\setminus n)=0\}.

The following is a natural subclass of
F_{\sigma}
non-P-ideals, introduced in
[
HRZ14
]
.

Definition 2.5
.

An ideal
\mathscr{I}
on
\omega
is
fragmented
if there is a partition
\langle P_{n}\rangle_{n<\omega}
of
\omega
into nonempty finite sets and a sequence
\langle\varphi_{n}\rangle_{n<\omega}
such that each
\varphi_{n}
is a submeasure on
P_{n}
such that

X\in\mathscr{I}\iff\sup_{n<\omega}\varphi_{n}(X\cap P_{n})<\infty.

A fragmented ideal associated to
\langle P_{n},\varphi_{n}\rangle_{n<\omega}
is
gradually fragmented
if there is a function
f\colon\omega\to\omega
such that for all
n<\omega
, the following holds:

\forall i<\omega\,\forall^{\infty}j<\omega\,\forall\overline{B}\in[\mathcal{P}(P_{j})]^{\leq i}\,\left(\forall B\in\overline{B}\,(\varphi_{j}(B)\leq n)\Rightarrow\varphi_{j}\left(\bigcup\overline{B}\right)\leq f(n)\right).

Let
\mathscr{I}
be a fragmented ideal on
\omega
associated to
\langle P_{n},\varphi_{n}\rangle_{n<\omega}
. Then a function
\varphi\colon\mathcal{P}(\omega)\to[0,\infty]
defined by
\varphi(A)=\sup_{n<\omega}\varphi_{n}(A\cap P_{n})
is a lower semi-continuous submeasure on
\omega
such that
\mathscr{I}=\Fin(\varphi)
. By
2.4
(1),
\mathscr{I}
is
F_{\sigma}
. It was shown in
[
BM14
, Corollary 2.9]
that any tall (proper) fragmented ideal is not a P-ideal. One can find more information about fragmented ideals in
[
BM14
]
.

Next, we introduce the standard cardinal invariants associated to ideals.

Definition 2.6
.

Let
\mathcal{I}
be a
\sigma
-ideal on a set
X
. Then we define

\displaystyle\add(\mathcal{I})

\displaystyle=\min\{\lvert\mathcal{A}\rvert:\mathcal{A}\subseteq\mathcal{I}\land\bigcup\mathcal{A}\notin\mathcal{I}\}

\displaystyle\non(\mathcal{I})

\displaystyle=\min\{\lvert A\rvert:A\subseteq X\land A\notin\mathcal{I}\}

\displaystyle\cov(\mathcal{I})

\displaystyle=\min\{\lvert\mathcal{A}\rvert:\mathcal{A}\subseteq\mathcal{I}\land\bigcup\mathcal{A}=X\}

\displaystyle\cof(\mathcal{I})

\displaystyle=\min\{\lvert\mathcal{A}\rvert:\mathcal{A}\subseteq\mathcal{I}\land\forall B\in\mathcal{I}\,\exists A\in\mathcal{A}\,(B\subseteq A)\}

Definition 2.7
.

Let
\mathscr{I}
be an ideal on a countable set
X
. Then we define

\displaystyle\add^{*}(\mathscr{I})

\displaystyle=\min\{\lvert\mathcal{A}\rvert:\mathcal{A}\subseteq\mathscr{I}\land\forall B\in\mathscr{I}\,\exists A\in\mathcal{A}\,(A\not\subseteq^{*}B)\}

\displaystyle\non^{*}(\mathscr{I})

\displaystyle=\min\{\lvert\mathcal{A}\rvert:\mathcal{A}\subseteq[X]^{\omega}\land\forall B\in\mathscr{I}\,\exists A\in\mathcal{A}\,(\lvert A\cap B\rvert<\omega)\}

\displaystyle\cov^{*}(\mathscr{I})

\displaystyle=\min\{\lvert\mathcal{A}\rvert:\mathcal{A}\subseteq\mathscr{I}\land\forall B\in[X]^{\omega}\,\exists A\in\mathcal{A}\,(\lvert A\cap B\rvert=\omega)\}

\displaystyle\cof^{*}(\mathscr{I})

\displaystyle=\min\{\lvert\mathcal{A}\rvert:\mathcal{A}\subseteq\mathscr{I}\land\forall B\in\mathscr{I}\,\exists A\in\mathcal{A}\,(B\subseteq^{*}A)\}

If
\mathscr{I}
is not tall,
\cov^{*}(\mathscr{I})
is ill-defined. In this case, we set
\cov^{*}(\mathscr{I})=\infty
for convenience.
2
2

2

Recall that our implicit assumption that an ideal is always proper. This guarantees that
\add^{*}(\mathscr{I})
and
\non^{*}(\mathscr{I})
are well-defined.
\cof^{*}(\mathscr{I})
is well-defined even without properness.

The notation in
2.7
come from
[
HH07
]
.
Note that if
\mathscr{I}
is tall, then
\cov^{*}(\mathscr{I})
and
\cof^{*}(\mathscr{I})
are always uncountable, but
\add^{*}(\mathscr{I})
and
\non^{*}(\mathscr{I})
may not. There is an easy criteria for
\add^{*}(\mathscr{I})
and
\non^{*}(\mathscr{I})
to be countable as follows.

Lemma 2.8
.

Let
\mathscr{I}
be a tall ideal on a countable set. Then the following hold:

(1)

\add^{*}(\mathscr{I})>\omega\iff\mathscr{I}
is a P-ideal.

(2)

(
[
HMM10
]
)
If
\mathscr{I}
is Borel, then
\non^{*}(\mathscr{I})>\omega\iff\mathscr{I}\geq_{\mathrm{KB}}\mathcal{ED}_{\mathrm{fin}}
.

We recall the role of idealized forcings for computing cardinal invariants. Let
\mathcal{I}
be a
\sigma
-ideal on a Polish space
X
. The poset of
\mathcal{I}
-positive Borel sets ordered by inclusion is denoted by
\mathbb{P}_{\mathcal{I}}
. The forcing
\mathbb{P}_{\mathcal{I}}
adds a
canonical real

\dot{r}^{\mathcal{I}}_{\text{gen}}
such that, for every Borel
B\subseteq X
coded in the ground model,
B
is in the generic filter if and only if
\dot{r}^{\mathcal{I}}_{\text{gen}}\in B

[
Zap08
, Proposition 2.1.2]
.

When computing
\non(\mathcal{I})
and
\cov(\mathcal{I})
, it is natural to look at the forcing
\mathbb{P}_{\mathcal{I}}
because of the following connection between the cardinal invariants and the generic reals added by
\mathbb{P}_{\mathcal{I}}
.

Definition 2.9

(
[
Kho11
, Definition 2.3.1]
)
.

Let
\mathcal{I}
be a
\sigma
-ideal on a Polish space
X
and
M
a transitive model of set theory. A real
x
is called
\mathcal{I}
-quasi-generic
over
M
if for every Borel set
B\in\mathcal{I}
whose Borel code lies in
M
,
x\notin B
.

Lemma 2.10
.

Let
\mathcal{I},\mathcal{J}
be
\sigma
-ideals on Polish spaces
X
and
Y
generated by Borel sets. Assume that there is a Borel function
f\colon Y\to X
such that

\mathbb{P}_{\mathcal{J}}\Vdash f(\dot{r}^{\mathcal{J}}_{\mathrm{gen}})\text{ is }\mathcal{I}\text{-quasi-generic}.

Then, for any
A\in\mathcal{I}
,
f^{-1}[A]\in\mathcal{J}
holds (in other words,
f
witnesses
\mathcal{I}\leq_{\mathrm{K}}\mathcal{J}
). In particular,
\non(\mathcal{I})\leq\non(\mathcal{J})
and
\cov(\mathcal{I})\geq\cov(\mathcal{J})
hold.

Proof.

Let
A\in\mathcal{I}
and we may assume
A
is Borel. Assume
f^{-1}[A]\notin\mathcal{J}
. Then
f^{-1}[A]
is Borel so
f^{-1}[A]\in\mathbb{P}_{\mathcal{J}}
.
f^{-1}[A]
forces
\dot{r}^{\mathcal{J}}_{\mathrm{gen}}\in f^{-1}[A]
, and hence
f(\dot{r}^{\mathcal{J}}_{\mathrm{gen}})\in A
, which contradicts that
f(\dot{r}^{\mathcal{J}}_{\mathrm{gen}})
is forced to be
\mathcal{I}
-quasi-generic.
∎

3.
\omega
-versions of cardinal invariants of
\mathscr{I}

To compute the cardinal invariants of
M_{\mathscr{I}}
, the following “
\omega
-versions” of the cardinal invariants in
2.7
will be useful.

Definition 3.1
.

Let
\mathscr{I}
be an ideal on a countable set
X
. Then we define

\displaystyle\add^{*}_{\omega}(\mathscr{I})

\displaystyle=\min\{\lvert\mathcal{A}\rvert:\mathcal{A}\subseteq\mathscr{I}\land\forall\overline{B}\in[\mathscr{I}]^{\omega}\,\exists A\in\mathcal{A}\,\forall B\in\overline{B}\,(A\not\subseteq^{*}B)\}

\displaystyle\non^{*}_{\omega}(\mathscr{I})

\displaystyle=\min\{\lvert\mathcal{A}\rvert:\mathcal{A}\subseteq[X]^{\omega}\land\forall\overline{B}\in[\mathscr{I}]^{\omega}\,\exists A\in\mathcal{A}\,\forall B\in\overline{B}\,(\lvert A\cap B\rvert<\omega)\}

\displaystyle\cof^{*}_{\omega}(\mathscr{I})

\displaystyle=\min\{\lvert\mathcal{A}\rvert:\mathcal{A}\subseteq[\mathscr{I}]^{\omega}\land\forall\overline{B}\in[\mathscr{I}]^{\omega}\,\exists\overline{A}\in\mathcal{A}\,\forall B\in\overline{B}\,\exists A\in\overline{A}\,(B\subseteq^{*}A)\}

If
\mathscr{I}
is countably generated, then
\add^{*}_{\omega}(\mathscr{I})
is ill-defined. In this case, we set
\add^{*}_{\omega}(\mathscr{I})=\infty
for convenience.
3
3

3

Properness of
\mathscr{I}
guarantees that
\non^{*}_{\omega}(\mathscr{I})
is well-defined. Even without properness,
\cof^{*}_{\omega}(\mathscr{I})
is well-defined.

One might think that we could naturally define the
\omega
-version of
\cov^{*}(\mathscr{I})
by

\cov^{*}_{\omega}(\mathscr{I})=\min\{\lvert\mathcal{A}\rvert:\mathcal{A}\subseteq\mathscr{I}\land\forall\overline{B}\in[[X]^{\omega}]^{\omega}\,\exists A\in\mathcal{A}\,\forall B\in\overline{B}\,(\lvert A\cap B\rvert=\omega)\},

but obviously
\cov^{*}_{\omega}(\mathscr{I})=\cov^{*}(\mathscr{I})
holds.

Except for
\cof^{*}_{\omega}(\mathscr{I})
, the notions in
3.1
come from
[
CM25
]
, but the cardinal invariants themselves were introduced in
[
BS99
]
for ultrafilters. Using the notation in
[
BS99
]
, we have

\mathfrak{p}^{\prime}(\mathcal{U})=\add^{*}_{\omega}(\mathcal{U}^{*}),\pi\chi_{\sigma}(\mathcal{U})=\non^{*}_{\omega}(\mathcal{U}^{*}),\chi_{\sigma}(\mathcal{U})=\cof^{*}_{\omega}(\mathcal{U}^{*})

for any ultrafilter
\mathcal{U}
on
\omega
. In
[
LV99
]
,
\add_{\omega}(P)
was defined for partial orders
P
and
\add^{*}_{\omega}(\mathscr{I})=\add_{\omega}(\langle\mathcal{I},\subseteq^{*}\rangle)
holds. Also,
\add^{*}_{\omega}(\mathscr{I})
was studied in
[
FK25
]
, but denoted by
\add_{\omega}(\mathscr{I})
.

It is natural to ask when these
\omega
-versions of
*
-numbers are different from the original
*
-numbers. The following observations cover the case of
P
-ideals.

Lemma 3.2
.

Let
\mathscr{I}
be a tall ideal on a countable set. Then the following hold:

(1)

\omega_{1}\leq\add^{*}_{\omega}(\mathscr{I})\leq\non^{*}_{\omega}(\mathscr{I})\leq\cof^{*}_{\omega}(\mathscr{I})
.

(2)

Let
\mathrm{inv}
denote either
\add,\non
, or
\cov
. Then
\mathrm{inv}^{*}(\mathscr{I})\leq\mathrm{inv}^{*}_{\omega}(\mathscr{I})
and if
\mathscr{I}
is a P-ideal, then
\mathrm{inv}^{*}(\mathscr{I})=\mathrm{inv}^{*}_{\omega}(\mathscr{I})
.

This lemma implies that
\add^{*}(\mathscr{I})=\add^{*}_{\omega}(\mathscr{I})\iff\mathscr{I}
is a P-ideal (If
\add^{*}(\mathscr{I})=\add^{*}_{\omega}(\mathscr{I})
then
\add^{*}(\mathscr{I})
is uncountable by (1) and thus
\mathscr{I}
has to be a P-ideal. The other direction is (2)).
By
2.8
(2), there are many tall ideals
\mathscr{I}
such that
\omega=\non^{*}(\mathscr{I})<\non^{*}_{\omega}(\mathscr{I})
holds (in
\mathsf{ZFC}
). Also, it is not hard to find a Borel ideal
\mathscr{I}
such that
\omega_{1}\leq\non^{*}(\mathscr{I})<\non^{*}_{\omega}(\mathscr{I})
is consistent (
3.27
).
In addition, it is consistent that
\cof^{*}(\mathscr{I})<\cof^{*}_{\omega}(\mathscr{I})
for some ideal
\mathscr{I}
(see
3.23
).

Lemma 3.3
.

Let
\mathscr{I},\mathscr{J}
be tall ideals on
\omega
.

(1)

If
\mathscr{I}\preceq_{\omega T}\mathscr{J}
then
\add^{*}_{\omega}(\mathscr{I})\geq\add^{*}_{\omega}(\mathscr{J})
and
\cof^{*}_{\omega}(\mathscr{I})\leq\cof^{*}_{\omega}(\mathscr{J})
hold.

(2)

(
[
CM25
, Proposition 4.6]
)

\mathscr{I}\leq_{\mathrm{KB}}\mathscr{J}
implies
\non^{*}_{\omega}(\mathscr{I})\leq\non^{*}_{\omega}(\mathscr{J})
.

Proof.

To show (1), let
f\colon\mathscr{I}\to\mathscr{J}
be a witness of
\mathscr{I}\preceq_{\omega T}\mathscr{J}
.

To see
\add^{*}_{\omega}(\mathscr{I})\geq\add^{*}_{\omega}(\mathscr{J})
, let
\mathcal{A}\subseteq\mathscr{I}
be such that
\lvert\mathcal{A}\rvert<\add^{*}_{\omega}(\mathscr{J})
. Then
f[\mathcal{A}]
is
\sigma
-bounded in
\mathscr{J}
and thus
\mathcal{A}
is
\sigma
-bounded in
\mathscr{I}
. So,
\lvert\mathcal{A}\rvert<\add^{*}_{\omega}(\mathscr{I})
.

To see
\cof^{*}_{\omega}(\mathscr{I})\leq\cof^{*}_{\omega}(\mathscr{J})
, let
\mathcal{A}\subseteq[\mathscr{J}]^{\omega}
witness
|\mathcal{A}|=\cof^{*}_{\omega}(\mathscr{J})
.
We may assume:

\forall\overline{X}\in[\mathscr{J}]^{\omega}\exists\overline{A}\in\mathcal{A}\forall X\in\overline{X}\exists A\in\overline{A}\,(X\subseteq A).

For each
\overline{A}\in\mathcal{A}
, there is
\mathcal{A}_{\overline{A}}\in[\mathscr{I}]^{\omega}
witnessing
\overline{I}_{\overline{A}}\coloneqq\{X:f(X)\subseteq A\text{ for some }A\in\overline{A}\}\subseteq\mathscr{I}
is
\sigma
-bounded, since
\overline{A}\subseteq\mathscr{J}
is
\sigma
-bounded. To see
\{\overline{I}_{\overline{A}}:A\in\mathcal{A}\}
witnesses
\cof^{*}_{\omega}(\mathscr{J})\leq|\mathcal{A}|
, let
\overline{X}\in[\mathscr{I}]^{\omega}
be arbitrary. Take
\overline{A}\in\mathcal{A}
such that for all
X\in\overline{X}
there is
A\in\overline{A}
such that
f(X)\subseteq A
. Thus
X\in\overline{I}_{\overline{A}}
and hence there is
A^{\prime}\in\mathcal{A}_{\overline{A}}
such that
X\subseteq A^{\prime}
, which finishes the proof.
∎

3.1.
Computation of
\add^{*}_{\omega}(\mathscr{I})
and
\cof^{*}_{\omega}(\mathscr{I})

We compute
\add^{*}_{\omega}(\mathscr{I})
for various Borel non-P ideals
\mathscr{I}
. According to
[
Hru11
]
(the paragraph before Question 6.21), there are only three known distinct values of
\add^{*}(\mathscr{I})
for analytic ideals
\mathscr{I}
, which are
\omega
,
\add(\mathcal{N})
and
\mathfrak{b}
. In contrast, we will see that
\add^{*}_{\omega}(\mathscr{I})
can take values other than
\omega_{1},\add(\mathcal{N})
and
\mathfrak{b}
. Indeed,
\add^{*}_{\omega}(\mathrm{nwd})=\add(\mathcal{M})
and
\add^{*}_{\omega}(\mathscr{I})>\mathfrak{b}
is consistent for some
F_{\sigma}
non-P ideal
\mathscr{I}
. In
[
CM25
]
and
[
FK25
]
, the following was already shown.

Proposition 3.4

(Cieślak–Martínez-Celis
[
CM25
]
and Filipów–Kwela
[
FK25
]
4
4

4

All the proofs can be found in Section 4 of
[
CM25
]
. In
[
FK25
]
, only
\add^{*}_{\omega}(\mathrm{Fin}\otimes\mathrm{Fin})
and
\add^{*}_{\omega}(\mathcal{S})
are computed as Example 5.12 and Theorem 5.13(7).
)
.

(1)

\add^{*}_{\omega}(\mathrm{Fin}\otimes\mathrm{Fin})=\mathfrak{b}
.

(2)

\add^{*}_{\omega}(\mathscr{I})=\omega_{1}
for
\mathscr{I}=\mathcal{S},\mathcal{R},\mathrm{conv},\mathcal{ED},\mathcal{ED}_{\mathrm{fin}}
.

Theorem 5.11(3) of
[
FK25
]
can be improved as follows:

Proposition 3.5
.

For any ideals
\mathscr{I},\mathscr{J}
on countable sets,

\add^{*}_{\omega}(\mathscr{I}\otimes\mathscr{J})=\min\{\mathfrak{b},\add^{*}_{\omega}(\mathscr{I}),\add^{*}_{\omega}(\mathscr{J})\}.

Proof.

We may assume that the ideals
\mathscr{I}
and
\mathscr{J}
are both on
\omega
.

(
\leq
) The inequality
\add^{*}_{\omega}(\mathscr{I}\otimes\mathscr{J})\leq\min\{\add^{*}_{\omega}(\mathscr{I}),\add^{*}_{\omega}(\mathscr{J})\}
was shown in
[
FK25
]
as Theorem 5.11(3), so it suffices to prove that
\add^{*}_{\omega}(\mathscr{I}\otimes\mathscr{J})\leq\mathfrak{b}
.

Let
\{f_{\alpha}:\alpha<\mathfrak{b}\}\subseteq\omega^{\omega}
be an unbounded family of strictly increasing functions and let

A_{\alpha}=\{\langle i,j\rangle\in\omega\times\omega:j\leq f_{\alpha}(i)\}.

Clearly
A_{\alpha}\in\mathrm{Fin}\otimes\mathrm{Fin}\subseteq\mathscr{I}\otimes\mathscr{J}
. We claim that
\{A_{\alpha}:\alpha<\mathfrak{b}\}
is a witness for
\add^{*}_{\omega}(\mathscr{I}\otimes\mathscr{J})
. To show this, let
\{B_{n}:n<\omega\}\subseteq\mathscr{I}\otimes\mathscr{J}
. For each
n<\omega
, there is a function
g_{n}\in\omega^{\omega}
such that for all
m<\omega
,

(\omega\setminus m\times g_{n}(m))\setminus B_{n}\neq\emptyset,

since otherwise
(\omega\setminus m)\times\omega\subseteq B_{n}
for some
m<\omega
, which contradicts
B_{n}\in\mathscr{I}\otimes\mathscr{J}
. We also choose
\alpha<\mathfrak{b}
such that
f_{\alpha}\not\leq^{*}g_{n}
for all
n<\omega
. Now we show that
A_{\alpha}\not\subseteq^{*}B_{n}
for all
n<\omega
: For each
n<\omega
, there is
m<\omega
such that
f_{\alpha}(m)>g_{n}(m)
. By the choice of
g_{n}
, we can find
\langle i,j\rangle\in\omega\times\omega
such that
i\geq m,j\leq g_{n}(m)
, and
\langle i,j\rangle\notin B_{n}
. Since

f_{\alpha}(i)\geq f_{\alpha}(m)>g_{n}(m)\geq j,

so
\langle i,j\rangle\in A_{\alpha}\setminus B_{n}
.

(
\geq
) Let
\kappa<\min\{\mathfrak{b},\add^{*}_{\omega}(\mathscr{I}),\add^{*}_{\omega}(\mathscr{J})\}
and let
\mathcal{A}\coloneqq\{A_{\alpha}:\alpha<\kappa\}\subseteq\mathscr{I}\otimes\mathscr{J}
. We want to show that
\mathcal{A}
is not a witness for
\add^{*}_{\omega}(\mathscr{I}\otimes\mathscr{J})
.

For each
\alpha<\kappa
, let
J_{\alpha}=\{n<\omega:(A_{\alpha})_{n}\notin\mathscr{I}\}
. Clearly
J_{\alpha}\in\mathscr{J}
. Since
\kappa<\add^{*}_{\omega}(\mathscr{I})
, there must be
\{I_{n}:n<\omega\}\subseteq\mathscr{I}
such that for any
\alpha<\kappa
and any
n\notin J_{\alpha}
, there is
m<\omega
such that
(A_{\alpha})_{n}\subseteq I_{m}
. We may assume that
I_{n}\subseteq I_{n+1}
for all
n<\omega
. For each
\alpha<\kappa
, let
g_{\alpha}\in\omega^{\omega}
be such that for all
n<\omega
,

n\notin J_{\alpha}\Rightarrow(A_{\alpha})_{n}\subseteq I_{g_{\alpha}(n)}.

As
\kappa<\mathfrak{b}
, there is a function
f\in\omega^{\omega}
such that
f\geq^{*}g_{\alpha}
for all
\alpha<\kappa
. Moreover, since
\kappa<\add^{*}_{\omega}(\mathscr{J})
, there is
\{J^{\prime}_{n}:n<\omega\}\subseteq\mathscr{J}
such that for every
\alpha<\kappa
, there is
n<\omega
such that
J_{\alpha}\subseteq J^{\prime}_{n}
. We may also assume that
n\subseteq J^{\prime}_{n}\subseteq J^{\prime}_{n+1}
for all
n<\omega
.

For each
n<\omega
, we define
B_{n}\subseteq\omega\times\omega
by

B_{n}=\{\langle i,j\rangle:i\in J^{\prime}_{n}\lor j\in I_{f(i)}\}.

Clearly
B_{n}\in\mathscr{I}\otimes\mathscr{J}
. We claim that for each
\alpha<\kappa
there is
n<\omega
such that
A_{\alpha}\subseteq B_{n}
, which implies
\mathcal{A}
is not a witness for
\add^{*}_{\omega}(\mathscr{I}\otimes\mathscr{J})
. To show this, fix
\alpha<\kappa
. Then we can find
n<\omega
such that
f(m)\geq g_{\alpha}(m)
for all
m\geq n
and
J_{\alpha}\subseteq J^{\prime}_{n}
. It is easy to see
A_{\alpha}\subseteq B_{n}
as follows: let
\langle i,j\rangle\in A_{\alpha}
. If
i\in J^{\prime}_{m}
, then
\langle i,j\rangle\in B_{n}
by definition. Otherwise, since
i\notin J_{\alpha}
and
i\geq n
, we have
j\in(A_{\alpha})_{i}\subseteq I_{g_{\alpha}(i)}\subseteq I_{f(i)}
and thus
\langle i,j\rangle\in B_{n}
.
∎

The following proposition was independently proved by Osvaldo Guzmán and Francisco Santiago Nieto de la Rosa. It gives an affirmative answer to
[
CM25
, Question 4.10]
.

Proposition 3.6

(Independently, Guzmán–Nieto de la Rosa)
.

\add^{*}_{\omega}(\mathrm{nwd})=\add(\mathcal{M})
.

Proof.

In
[
CM25
, Theorem 4.9]
,
\add^{*}_{\omega}(\mathrm{nwd})\leq\add(\mathcal{M})
was proved. To show the converse inequality, let
\kappa<\add(\mathcal{M})
and assume that
\{A_{\alpha}:\alpha<\kappa\}\subseteq\mathrm{nwd}
. It suffices to find a countable family
\{D_{k}:k<\omega\}
of open dense subsets of
2^{<\omega}
such that for each
\alpha<\kappa
there is
k<\omega
such that
A_{\alpha}\cap D_{k}=\emptyset
. Since
\kappa<\add(\mathcal{M})
, there is a countable family
\{T_{n}:n<\omega\}
of nowhere dense subtrees of
2^{<\omega}
(i.e. for any
s\in 2^{<\omega}
, there is
t\supseteq s
with
t\notin T_{n}
) such that
\bigcup_{\alpha<\kappa}[A_{\alpha}]\subseteq\bigcup_{n<\omega}[T_{n}]
.

Fix
t\in 2^{<\omega}
for now. Since each
T_{n}
is nowhere dense, we can inductively take
t\subsetneq s_{0}\subsetneq s_{1}\subsetneq\cdots\in 2^{<\omega}
such that
s_{n}\notin T_{n}
. Let
x_{t}=\bigcup_{n<\omega}s_{n}\in 2^{\omega}
. Since
x_{t}\notin\bigcup_{n<\omega}[T_{n}]
, for each
\alpha<\kappa
there is
m_{t,\alpha}<\omega
such that
t\subseteq x_{t}\upharpoonright m_{t,\alpha}\notin A_{\alpha}
. Since
\kappa<\add(\mathcal{M})\leq\mathfrak{b}
, there is
m_{t}<\omega
such that for all
\alpha<\kappa
and for all but finitely many
t\in 2^{<\omega}
,
m_{t,\alpha}\leq m_{t}
.

Fix some bijection
e\colon\omega\to 2^{<\omega}
and for each
k<\omega
, let

D_{k}=\{s\in 2^{<\omega}:\exists n\geq k\,(s\supseteq x_{e(n)}\upharpoonright m_{e(n)})\}.

Clearly
D_{k}
is open dense. For each
\alpha<\kappa
, there is
k<\omega
such that for all
n\geq k
,
m_{e(n),\alpha}\leq m_{e(n)}
. To show
A_{\alpha}\cap D_{k}=\emptyset
, let
s\in D_{k}
be arbitrary. Then
s\supseteq x_{e(n)}\upharpoonright m_{e(n)}
for some
n\geq k
. Since
x_{e(n)}\upharpoonright m_{e(n)}\supseteq x_{e(n)}\upharpoonright m_{e(n),\alpha}\notin A_{\alpha}
, we have
s\notin A_{\alpha}
.
∎

Now we aim to find Borel ideals
\mathscr{I}
such that
\add^{*}_{\omega}(\mathscr{I})>\mathfrak{b}
is consistent. By the following classical results, such
\mathscr{I}
must be a
F_{\sigma}
non-P-ideal.

Theorem 3.7

(Todorčević,
[
Tod96
]
)
.

For every tall analytic P-ideal
\mathscr{I}
,

\add(\mathcal{N})\leq\add^{*}(\mathscr{I})\leq\mathfrak{b}\text{ and }\mathfrak{d}\leq\cof^{*}(\mathscr{I})\leq\cof(\mathcal{N}).

Theorem 3.8

(Louveau–Velickovic,
[
LV99
]
)
.

Let
\mathscr{I}
be a tall analytic ideal on
\omega
. Then either
\mathscr{I}
is
F_{\sigma}
or
(\omega^{\omega},\leq)
is Tukey reducible to
(\mathcal{I},\subseteq)
.

Corollary 3.9
.

If
\mathscr{I}
is a tall non-
F_{\sigma}
ideal on a countable set, then

\add^{*}_{\omega}(\mathscr{I})\leq\mathfrak{b}\text{ and }\cof^{*}(\mathscr{I})\geq\mathfrak{d}.

Even for
F_{\sigma}
non-P ideals
\mathscr{I}
, it is often the case that
\add^{*}(\mathscr{I})=\omega_{1}
, which follows from the following results.

Definition 3.10
.

Given an ideal
\mathscr{I}
, a subset
X\subseteq\mathscr{I}
of
\mathscr{I}
is
strongly unbounded
if for every countable
A\subseteq X
we have that
\bigcup A\notin\mathscr{I}
.

One can easily show that
\mathscr{I}_{L}
has an uncountable perfect strongly unbounded set.

Proposition 3.11
.

If an ideal
\mathscr{I}
has a strongly unbounded uncountable subset, then
\add^{*}_{\omega}(\mathscr{I})=\omega_{1}
.

Proof.

Observe that, if
X
is a strongly unbounded subset of size
\omega_{1}
, then every
I\in\mathscr{I}
can only contain finitely many elements of
X
. So if
\{I_{n}:n\in\omega\}\subseteq\mathscr{I}
, then there is
A\in X
not contained in any of the
I_{n}
’s, thus
X
is a witness for
\add_{\omega}(\mathscr{I})
, i.e.
\add^{*}_{\omega}(\mathscr{I})=\omega_{1}
.
∎

Corollary 3.12
.

\add^{*}_{\omega}(\mathscr{I}_{L})=\omega_{1}
.

The ideal
\mathscr{I}_{P}
is the typical example of a fragmented ideal that has no strongly unbounded subsets (see
[
HRZ14
]
). We will see that
\add^{*}_{\omega}(\mathscr{I}_{P})>\mathfrak{b}
is consistent. To prove this, we need to recall the following cardinal invariant introduced by Pawlikowski
[
Paw85
]
.

Definition 3.13
.

Let
\mathcal{I}
be a
\sigma
-ideal on
2^{\omega}
. Note that
2^{\omega}
can be regarded as a topological group with the coordinate-wise addition modulo 2. Then the transitive additivity of
\mathcal{I}
, denoted by
\add_{t}(\mathcal{I})
, is defined by

\add_{t}(\mathcal{I})=\min\{\lvert A\rvert:A\subseteq 2^{\omega}\land\exists X\in\mathcal{I}\,(A+X\notin\mathcal{I})\}.

We use Pawlikowski’s characterization of
\add_{t}{(\mathcal{N})}
, where
\mathcal{N}
is the null ideal on
2^{\omega}
.
5
5

5

There is a slight abuse of notation here, because
\mathcal{N}
also denotes the null ideal on
\omega^{\omega}
in this paper.

Lemma 3.14

(Pawlikowski,
[
Paw85
, Lemma 2.2]
)
.

\add_{t}(\mathcal{N})=\min\left\{\lvert\mathcal{A}\rvert:\exists g\in(\omega\setminus 1)^{\omega}\,\left(\mathcal{A}\subseteq\prod_{i<\omega}g(i)\land\forall S\in\prod_{i<\omega}[g(i)]^{\leq i}\,\exists f\in\mathcal{A}\,(f\not\in^{*}S)\right)\right\},

where
f\in^{*}S
means that
\forall^{\infty}i<\omega\,(f(i)\in S(i))
holds.

In
[
Paw85
]
, Pawlikowski also showed that

\add(\mathcal{N})=\min\{\mathfrak{b},\add_{t}(\mathcal{N})\}

holds and that
\add(\mathcal{N})=\mathfrak{b}<\add_{t}(\mathcal{N})
is consistent. By Shelah
[
She92
]
(see also
[
Bre95
, Section 3.4, Theorem 2]
), it is also known that
\mathfrak{d}<\add_{t}(\mathcal{N})
is consistent. One can find other results on
\add_{t}(\mathcal{N})
in
[
BJ95
, Section 2.7]
and more recently in
[
CMR25
]
.

Proposition 3.15
.

\add^{*}_{\omega}(\mathscr{I}_{P})\geq\add_{t}(\mathcal{N})
.

Proof.

Assume that
\kappa<\add_{t}(\mathcal{N})
and let
\mathcal{A}\subseteq\mathscr{I}_{P}
be of size
\kappa
. It suffices to find
\{B_{n}:n<\omega\}\subseteq\mathscr{I}_{P}
such that for every
A\in\mathcal{A}
there is
n\in\omega
such that
A\subseteq^{*}B_{n}
. For each
n<\omega
, let

\mathcal{A}_{n}=\{A\in\mathcal{A}:\forall i<\omega\,(\lvert A\cap P_{i}^{\mathrm{exp}}\rvert\leq i^{n})\}.

Clearly,
\mathcal{A}=\bigcup_{n\in\omega}\mathcal{A}_{n}
. For each
A\in\mathcal{A}_{n}
, we define a function
f^{n}_{A}\colon\omega\to[P_{i}^{\mathrm{exp}}]^{\leq i^{n}}
by

f^{n}_{A}(i)=A\cap P_{i}^{\mathrm{exp}}.

By
3.14
, for each
n<\omega
, we can find a function
S_{n}
on
\omega
such that

(1)

\lvert S_{n}(i)\rvert\leq i
,

(2)

S_{n}(i)\subseteq[P_{i}^{\mathrm{exp}}]^{\leq i^{n}}
, and

(3)

for every
A\in\mathcal{A}_{n}
,
\forall^{\infty}i<\omega\,(f^{n}_{A}(i)\in S_{n}(i))
holds.

Then we set
B_{n}=\bigcup_{i<\omega}\bigcup S_{n}(i)
.
By (1) and (2), we have

\lvert B_{n}\cap P_{i}^{\mathrm{exp}}\rvert=\left\lvert\bigcup S_{n}(i)\right\rvert\leq i\cdot i^{n}=i^{n+1}

and thus
B_{n}\in\mathscr{I}_{P}
. By (3), for any
A\in\mathcal{A}_{n}
,
A\subseteq^{*}B_{n}
holds.
∎

The above proof can be generalized to show the following.

Theorem 3.16
.

For any gradually fragmented ideal
\mathscr{I}
on
\omega
,
\add_{\omega}^{*}(\mathscr{I})\geq\add_{t}{(\mathcal{N})}
.

Proof.

Assume that
\kappa<\add_{t}(\mathcal{N})
and let
\mathcal{A}\subseteq\mathscr{I}_{P}
of size
\kappa
. It suffices to find
\{B_{n}:n<\omega\}\subseteq\mathscr{I}_{P}
such that for every
A\in\mathcal{A}
there is
n\in\omega
such that
A\subseteq^{*}B_{n}
.

Assume that a partition
\langle P_{i}\rangle_{i<\omega}
of
\omega
and a sequence
\langle\varphi_{i}\rangle_{i<\omega}
of submeasures on
P_{i}
witness that
\mathscr{I}
is fragmented. Let
\varphi
be a lower semi-continuous submeasure on
\omega
defined by
\varphi(A)=\sup\{\varphi_{i}(A\cap P_{i}):i<\omega\}
. Note that
\mathscr{I}=\mathrm{Fin}(\varphi)
. Also, let
f\in\omega^{\omega}
be a function witnessing that
\mathscr{I}
is gradually fragmented. We may assume that
f
is increasing and
f(0)=0
. For each
n<\omega
, let
g_{n}\in\omega^{\omega}
be such that for all
i<\omega
,

(3.1)

\forall j\geq g_{n}(i)\,\forall\overline{B}\in[\mathcal{P}(P_{j})]^{\leq i}\,\left(\forall B\in\overline{B}\,(\varphi_{j}(B)\leq n)\Rightarrow\varphi_{j}\left(\bigcup\overline{B}\right)\leq f(n)\right).

For each
i,n<\omega
, let
P^{n}_{i}=\bigcup_{j\in[g_{n}(i),g_{n}(i+1))]}P_{j}
. For each
n<\omega
, let

\mathcal{A}_{n}=\{A\in\mathcal{A}:\varphi(A)\leq n\}.

As
\mathscr{I}=\mathrm{Fin}(\varphi)
, we have
\mathcal{A}=\bigcup_{n<\omega}\mathcal{A}_{n}
. For each
A\in\mathcal{A}_{n}
, we define
f^{n}_{A}\colon\omega\to\mathcal{P}(P^{n}_{i})
by

f^{n}_{A}(i)=A\cap P^{n}_{i}.

By
3.14
, for each
n<\omega
, we can find a function
S_{n}
on
\omega
such that

(1)

\lvert S_{n}(i)\rvert\leq i
,

(2)

S_{n}(i)\subseteq\mathcal{P}(P^{n}_{i})
,

(3)

for every
B\in S_{n}(i)
,
\varphi(B)\leq n
, and

(4)

for every
A\in\mathcal{A}_{n}
,
\forall^{\infty}i<\omega\,(f^{n}_{A}(i)\in S_{n}(i))
holds.

Then we set
B_{n}=\bigcup_{i<\omega}\bigcup S_{n}(i)
.
Using
(*)
, it follows from (1), (2) and (3) that

\varphi(B_{n}\cap P^{n}_{i})=\varphi(\bigcup S_{n}(i))\leq f(n)

and thus
B_{n}\in\Fin(\varphi)=\mathscr{I}
. By (4), for any
A\in\mathcal{A}_{n}
,
A\subseteq^{*}B_{n}
.
∎

Next we consider
\cof^{*}_{\omega}(\mathscr{I})
. One interesting phenomena is that we have several arguments for
\cof^{*}_{\omega}(\mathscr{I})
that can be seen as dual to the previous arguments for
\add^{*}_{\omega}(\mathscr{I})
, even though the definitions of
\add^{*}_{\omega}(\mathscr{I})
and
\cof^{*}_{\omega}(\mathscr{I})
are not really dual.

Proposition 3.17
.

(1)

\cof^{*}_{\omega}(\mathscr{I})=\mathfrak{c}
in the case that
\mathscr{I}
is
\mathcal{R},\mathcal{S},\mathrm{conv},\mathcal{ED},\mathcal{ED}_{fin},\mathscr{I}_{L}
.

(2)

\cof^{*}_{\omega}(\mathrm{Fin}\otimes\mathrm{Fin})=\mathfrak{d}
.

(3)

\cof^{*}_{\omega}(\mathrm{nwd})=\cof(\mathcal{M})
.

Proof.

Recall that
\cof^{*}(\mathscr{I})\leq\cof^{*}_{\omega}(\mathscr{I})
always holds.

(1) For those ideals
\mathscr{I}
, it is known that
\cof^{*}(\mathscr{I})=\mathfrak{c}
; For
\mathscr{I}=\mathcal{R},\mathcal{S},\mathrm{conv},\mathcal{ED}
, see
[
Hru11
, Section 3]
. For
\mathscr{I}=\mathcal{ED}_{\mathrm{fin}},\mathscr{I}_{L}
, this follows from
[
HRZ14
, Theorem 2.6]
, since
\mathcal{ED}_{\mathrm{fin}},\mathscr{I}_{L}
are fragmented but not gradually fragmented (cf.
[
BM14
]
).

(2) It is known that
\cof^{*}(\mathrm{Fin}\otimes\mathrm{Fin})=\mathfrak{d}
(cf.
[
Hru11
]
), so it suffices to show that
\cof^{*}_{\omega}(\mathrm{Fin}\otimes\mathrm{Fin})\leq\mathfrak{d}
. For any
n<\omega
and
f\in\omega^{\omega}
, let

A_{n,f}=(n\times\omega)\cup\{\langle i,j\rangle\in\omega\times\omega:j\leq f(i)\}.

Now let
\mathcal{F}\subseteq\omega^{\omega}
be a dominating family of size
\mathfrak{d}
. Then it is easy to see that
\mathcal{A}:=\{\{A_{n,f}:n<\omega\}:f\in\mathcal{F}\}
witnesses
\cof^{*}_{\omega}(\mathrm{Fin}\otimes\mathrm{Fin})\leq\mathfrak{d}
.

(3) It is known that
\cof^{*}(\mathrm{nwd})=\cof(\mathcal{M})
(due to Fremlin
[
Fre91
]
, but also see
[
BHH04
, Theorem 1.6(i)]
), so it suffices to show that
\cof^{*}_{\omega}(\mathrm{nwd})\leq\cof(\mathcal{M})
. Note that the following argument is a dual of
3.6
. Let
\{A_{\alpha}:\alpha<\cof(\mathcal{M})\}\subseteq\mathcal{M}
be cofinal in
\mathcal{M}
. We may assume
A_{\alpha}=\bigcup_{n<\omega}[T_{\alpha,n}]
, where
T_{\alpha,n}\subseteq 2^{<\omega}
is a nowhere dense tree.
For each
t\in 2^{<\omega}
, take
x^{\alpha}_{t}\in 2^{\omega}
such that
t\subseteq x^{\alpha}_{t}\notin\bigcup_{n<\omega}[T_{\alpha,n}]
. Let
\{f_{\beta}:\beta<\mathfrak{d}\}
be a dominating family of functions from
2^{<\omega}
to
\omega
. Fix some bijection
e\colon\omega\to 2^{<\omega}
and for each
k<\omega
, let

D_{\alpha,\beta,k}=\{s\in 2^{<\omega}:\exists n\geq k\,(s\supseteq x^{\alpha}_{e(n)}\upharpoonright f_{\beta}(e(n))\}.

Clearly
D_{\alpha,\beta,k}
is open dense. It is routine to see that
\{\{2^{<\omega}\setminus D_{\alpha,\beta,k}:k<\omega\}:\alpha<\cof(\mathcal{M})\land\beta<\mathfrak{d}\}
witnesses
\cof^{*}_{\omega}(\mathrm{nwd})\leq\max\{\cof(\mathcal{M}),\mathfrak{d}\}=\cof(\mathcal{M})
.
∎

We show that
\cof^{*}_{\omega}(\mathscr{I})<\mathfrak{d}
is consistent for some ideal
\mathscr{I}
by proving the dual of
3.15
and
3.16
. In
[
Kad00
]
, Kada introduced the following cardinal invariant that is closely related to the Laver property.

Definition 3.18
.

\mathfrak{l}=\min\{\kappa:\forall g\in\omega^{\omega}\,\exists\mathcal{S}\subseteq\prod_{i<\omega}[g(i)]^{\leq i}\,(\lvert\mathcal{S}\rvert=\kappa\land\forall f\leq^{*}g\,\exists S\in\mathcal{S}\,(f\in^{*}S))\}.

Note that
\mathfrak{l}
is a dual notion to the combinatorial characterization of
\add_{t}(\mathcal{N})
by Pawlikowski. In
[
Kad00
]
, it was mentioned that
\cof(\mathcal{N})=\min\{\mathfrak{d},\mathfrak{l}\}
and that in the Laver model,
\omega_{1}=\mathfrak{l}<\mathfrak{b}=\mathfrak{d}=\omega_{2}
holds.

Proposition 3.19
.

\cof^{*}_{\omega}(\mathscr{I}_{P})\leq\mathfrak{l}
.

Proof.

For each
i<\omega
, let

Q_{i}=\prod_{n\leq i}[P_{i}^{\mathrm{exp}}]^{\leq i^{n}}.

Let
\{S_{\alpha}:\alpha<\mathfrak{l}\}\subseteq\prod_{i<\omega}[Q_{i}]^{\leq i}
be such that every
f\in\prod_{i<\omega}Q_{i}
is captured by some
S_{\alpha}
in the sense that for all but finitely many
i<\omega
,
f(i)\in S_{\alpha}(i)
holds. For each
\alpha<\mathfrak{l}
and each
n<\omega
, we define

A_{\alpha}^{n}=\bigcup_{i\geq n}\bigcup\pi_{n}[S_{\alpha}(i)],

where
\pi_{n}
is the
n
-th projection map, i.e.
\pi_{n}(f)=f(n)
for every function
f
with
n\in\dom(f)
. For each
n<\omega
,
A_{\alpha}^{n}\in\mathscr{I}_{P}
because for all
i\geq n
,

\lvert A_{\alpha}^{n}\cap P^{\mathrm{exp}}_{i}\rvert=\lvert\pi_{n}[S_{\alpha}(i)]\rvert\leq i\cdot i^{n}=i^{n+1}

holds. Let
A_{\alpha}=\{A^{n}_{\alpha}:n<\omega\}
for each
\alpha<\mathfrak{l}
and we claim that
\{A_{\alpha}:\alpha<\mathfrak{l}\}
witnesses
\cof^{*}_{\omega}(\mathscr{I}_{P})
; Let
\{B_{n}:n\in\omega\}\subseteq\mathscr{I}_{P}
. By adding more elements to the sequence if necessary, we may assume that
\varphi(B_{n})\leq n
. Let
f
be a function on
\omega
defined by

f(i)=\langle B_{0}\cap P_{i}^{\mathrm{exp}},B_{1}\cap P_{i}^{\mathrm{exp}},\ldots,B_{i}\cap P_{i}^{\mathrm{exp}}\rangle.

Since
f(i)\in Q_{i}
for all but finitely many
i<\omega
,
f
must be captured by some
S_{\alpha}
. This implies
B_{n}\subseteq^{*}A^{n}_{\alpha}
for all
n<\omega
.
∎

More generally, the following holds.

Theorem 3.20
.

\cof^{*}_{\omega}(\mathscr{I})\leq\mathfrak{l}
for every gradually fragmented ideal
\mathscr{I}
.

Proof.

As in
3.16
, let
\langle P_{i},\varphi_{i}\rangle_{i<\omega}
witness that
\mathscr{I}
is fragmented and let
\varphi
be a lower semi-continuous submeasure on
\omega
defined by
\varphi(A)=\sup\{\varphi_{i}(A\cap P_{i}):i<\omega\}
. Also, let
f\in\omega^{\omega}
be an increasing function witnessing that
\mathscr{I}
is gradually fragmented and for each
n<\omega
, let
g_{n}\in\omega^{\omega}
be such that for all
i<\omega
, (
3.1
) holds. For each
i,n<\omega
, let
P^{n}_{i}=\bigcup_{j\in[g_{n}(i),g_{n}(i+1))]}P_{j}
. For each
i<\omega
, let

Q_{i}=\prod_{n\leq i}\{A\subseteq P^{n}_{i}:\varphi(A)\leq n\}.

Let
\{S_{\alpha}:\alpha<\mathfrak{l}\}\subseteq\prod_{i<\omega}[Q_{i}]^{\leq i}
be such that every element of
\prod_{i<\omega}Q_{i}
is captured by some
S_{\alpha}
. For each
\alpha<\mathfrak{l}
and each
n<\omega
, we define

A_{\alpha}^{n}=\bigcup_{i\geq n}\bigcup\pi_{n}[S_{\alpha}(i)],

where
\pi_{n}
is the
n
-th projection map. Using (
3.1
), we have
A_{\alpha}^{n}\in\mathrm{Fin}(\varphi)=\mathscr{I}
. Let
A_{\alpha}=\{A^{n}_{\alpha}:n<\omega\}
for each
\alpha<\mathfrak{l}
and one can show that
\{A_{\alpha}:\alpha<\mathfrak{l}\}
witnesses
\cof^{*}_{\omega}(\mathscr{I})
by the same argument for
3.19
.
∎

This result can be seen as a reformulation of the following “
\omega
-version” of
[
HRZ14
, Theorem 2.2 & Proposition 2.3]
:

Theorem 3.21
.

Let
\mathscr{I}
be a gradually fragmented ideal on
\omega
and let
\mathbb{P}
be a forcing with the Laver property. Then
[\mathscr{I}]^{\omega}\cap V
witnesses
\cof^{*}_{\omega}(\mathscr{I})
. Therefore, in the Laver model, for all gradually fragmented ideals
\mathscr{I}
on
\omega
,
\cof^{*}_{\omega}(\mathscr{I})<\mathfrak{b}=\mathfrak{d}
holds.

Proof.

We continue to use the notation introduced in the proof of
3.20
. Let
\{\dot{X}_{n}:n<\omega\}
be
\mathbb{P}
-names of a subset of
\omega
and assume that
\Vdash_{\mathbb{P}}\varphi(\dot{X}_{n})<\infty
. It suffices to find
\{A_{n}\colon n<\omega\}\in[\mathscr{I}]^{\omega}\cap V
such that for all
n<\omega
,
\Vdash_{\mathbb{P}}\dot{X}_{n}\subseteq^{*}A_{n}
holds.
For each
n<\omega
, find a name
\dot{Y}_{n}
of a function on
\omega
such that it is forced that

\dot{Y}_{n}(i)=\begin{cases}\dot{X}_{n}\cap P^{n}_{i}&\text{if }\varphi(\dot{X}_{n})\leq i,\\
\emptyset&\text{if }\varphi(\dot{X}_{n})>i.\end{cases}

For each
n<\omega
, it is forced that
\forall i\in\omega\,(\varphi(\dot{Y}_{n}(i))\leq i)
and that
\dot{X}_{n}\subseteq^{*}\bigcup_{i<\omega}\dot{Y}_{n}(i)
. Let
\dot{h}
be a name of a function on
\omega
such that it is forced that

\dot{h}(i)=\langle\dot{Y}_{0}(i),\dot{Y}_{1}(i),\ldots,\dot{Y}_{i}(i)\rangle.

Since
\Vdash_{\mathbb{P}}\dot{h}\in\prod_{i<\omega}Q_{i}
, the Laver property implies that there is a function
S\in V
such that, for all
i<\omega
,
S(i)\in[Q_{i}]^{\leq i}
and
\Vdash_{\mathbb{P}}\forall i<\omega\,(\dot{h}(i)\in S(i))
. For each
n<\omega
, we define

A_{n}=\bigcup_{i\geq n}\bigcup\pi_{n}[S(i)]\in V.

Using (
3.1
),
A_{n}\in\mathrm{Fin}(\varphi)=\mathscr{I}
. Also, it is forced that
\dot{X}_{n}\subseteq^{*}\bigcup_{i<\omega}\dot{Y}_{n}(i)\subseteq^{*}A_{n}
. This completes the proof.
∎

The following question still remains open.

Question 3.22
.

Is it consistent that
\cof^{*}(\mathscr{I})<\cof^{*}_{\omega}(\mathscr{I})
for some tall Borel ideal
\mathscr{I}
on
\omega
?

Remark 3.23
.

There is a non-Borel example for this question:
First, it is not hard to see that
\cof^{*}_{\omega}(\mathscr{J})
has uncountable cofinality for any ideal
\mathscr{J}
in ZFC.
Add
\aleph_{\omega}
many Cohen reals
c_{\alpha}\in[{\omega}]^{\omega}
and let
\mathscr{I}
be the ideal generated by
\{c_{\alpha}:\alpha<\aleph_{\omega}\}
. Then,
\cof^{*}(\mathscr{I})=\aleph_{\omega}
can be easily seen. Hence
\cof^{*}(\mathscr{I})<\cof^{*}_{\omega}(\mathscr{I})
.

We note that in
[
BS99
]
, the same question was raised for an ultrafilter as Question 1 in Chapter 8.

3.2.
Computation of
\non^{*}_{\omega}(\mathscr{I})

We compute
\non^{*}_{\omega}(\mathscr{I})
for various Borel non-P ideals
\mathscr{I}
. The first and third authors show the following in
[
CM25
]
.

Proposition 3.24

(Cieślak–Martínez-Celis
[
CM25
, Proposition 4.7]
)
.

\non^{*}_{\omega}(\mathcal{R})=\non^{*}_{\omega}(\mathrm{conv})=\omega_{1}.

We start with studying Fubini products of ideals.

Proposition 3.25
.

\non^{*}_{\omega}(\mathrm{Fin}\otimes\mathrm{Fin})=\mathfrak{d}
.

Proof.

To show that
\mathfrak{d}\leq\non^{*}_{\omega}(\mathrm{Fin}\otimes\mathrm{Fin})
, let
\mathcal{A}\subseteq[\omega\times\omega]^{\omega}
be of size
<\mathfrak{d}
. We claim that
\mathcal{A}
cannot be a witness for
\non^{*}_{\omega}(\mathrm{Fin}\otimes\mathrm{Fin})
. By shrinking each member of
\mathcal{A}
, we may assume that every
A\in\mathcal{A}
satisfies either

(1)

A\subseteq n\times\omega
for some
n<\omega
, or

(2)

A
is an infinite subset of (a graph of) some function
h_{A}\colon\omega\to\omega
.

Since
\lvert\mathcal{A}\rvert<\mathfrak{d}
, there is
f\in\omega^{\omega}
such that for all
A\in\mathcal{A}
,
f\not\leq^{*}h_{A}
(whenever
h_{A}
is well-defined). Now let

X_{n}=(n\times\omega)\cup\{\langle i,j\rangle:j\leq f(i)\}.

Then
\{X_{n}:n<\omega\}\in[\mathrm{Fin}\otimes\mathrm{Fin}]^{\omega}
witnesses that
\mathcal{A}
does not witness
\non^{*}_{\omega}(\mathrm{Fin}\otimes\mathrm{Fin})
, as desired.

To show that
\non^{*}_{\omega}(\mathrm{Fin}\otimes\mathrm{Fin})\leq\mathfrak{d}
, let
\mathcal{D}
be a dominating family of strictly increasing functions such that
\forall g\in\omega^{\omega}\,\exists f\in\mathcal{D}\,(g<f)
. For each
f\in\mathcal{D}
, we let

C_{f}=\{\langle n,f(n)\rangle:n<\omega\}.

Then it is straightforward to check that
\mathcal{C}=\{C_{f}:f\in\mathcal{D}\}
witnesses
\non^{*}_{\omega}(\mathrm{Fin}\otimes\mathrm{Fin})
. We write the details for general cases below.
∎

3.25
can be generalized as follows
but we note that
3.26
and
3.27
were already shown for maximal ideals in
[
BS99
]
.

Proposition 3.26

(cf. Brendle–Shelah
[
BS99
, Proposition 5.1(d)]
)
.

For any ideals
\mathscr{I},\mathscr{J}
on countable sets,

\non^{*}_{\omega}(\mathscr{I}\otimes\mathscr{J})=\max\{\mathfrak{d},\non^{*}_{\omega}(\mathscr{I}),\non^{*}_{\omega}(\mathscr{J})\}.

Proof.

We may assume that both
\mathscr{I}
and
\mathscr{J}
are ideals on
\omega
.
Since
\mathrm{Fin}\otimes\mathrm{Fin}\subseteq\mathscr{I}\otimes\mathscr{J}
,
3.3
(2) and
3.25
imply that
\mathfrak{d}=\non^{*}_{\omega}(\mathrm{Fin}\otimes\mathrm{Fin})\leq\non^{*}_{\omega}(\mathscr{I}\otimes\mathscr{J})
.

Note that
\non^{*}_{\omega}(\mathscr{I}),\non^{*}_{\omega}(\mathscr{J})\leq\non^{*}_{\omega}(\mathscr{I}\otimes\mathscr{J})
do not directly follow from
3.3
(2), because
\mathscr{I}
and
\mathscr{J}
are Katětov reducible to
\mathscr{I}\otimes\mathscr{J}
, but not necessarily Katětov–Blass reducible.
However, the inequalities still follow from the easy fact that if
\mathcal{A}\subseteq[\omega\times\omega]^{\omega}
is a witness for
\non^{*}_{\omega}(\mathscr{I}\otimes\mathscr{J})
, then
\{\pi_{0}[A]:A\in\mathcal{A}\}
and
\{\pi_{1}[A]:A\in\mathcal{A}\}
are witnesses for
\non^{*}_{\omega}(\mathscr{I})
and
\non^{*}_{\omega}(\mathscr{J})
respectively. Here,
\pi_{i}\colon\omega\times\omega\to\omega
is defined by
\pi_{i}(\langle n_{0},n_{1}\rangle)=n_{i}
.

Now we show that
\non^{*}_{\omega}(\mathscr{I}\otimes\mathscr{J})\leq\max\{\mathfrak{d},\non^{*}_{\omega}(\mathscr{I}),\non^{*}_{\omega}(\mathscr{J})\}
.
Let
\mathcal{D}\subseteq\omega^{\omega}
be a dominating family of strictly increasing functions such that
\forall g\in\omega^{\omega}\,\exists f\in\mathcal{D}\,(g<f)
. Let
\mathcal{A}
and
\mathcal{B}
be witnesses
\non^{*}_{\omega}(\mathscr{I})
and
\non^{*}_{\omega}(\mathscr{J})
respectively. For each
f\in\mathcal{D},A\in\mathcal{A},B\in\mathcal{B}
, we write

C_{f,A,B}=\{\langle A(n),B(f(n))\rangle:n<\omega\},

where
A(k),B(k)
denote the
k
-th element of the increasing enumeration of
A
,
B
respectively. We claim that

\mathcal{C}:=\{C_{f,A,B}:f\in\mathcal{D},A\in\mathcal{A},B\in\mathcal{B}\}

witnesses
\non^{*}_{\omega}(\mathscr{I}\otimes\mathscr{J})
. To prove this, let
\{X_{n}:n<\omega\}\in[\mathscr{I}\otimes\mathscr{J}]^{\omega}
be arbitrary. Then there are
Y_{n}\in\mathscr{I}
and
\phi_{n}\colon\omega\to\mathscr{J}
such that

X_{n}\subseteq(Y_{n}\times\omega)\cup\{\langle i,j\rangle:j\in\phi_{n}(i)\}.

Let
A\in\mathcal{A}
such that
\forall n<\omega\,(\lvert A\cap Y_{n}\rvert<\omega)
.
Let
B\in\mathcal{B}
such that
\forall n,i<\omega\,(\lvert B\cap\phi_{n}(i)\rvert<\omega)
. For each
n<\omega
, take
h_{n}\in\omega^{\omega}
such that

\forall i<\omega\,(B\cap\phi_{n}(i)\subseteq B(h_{n}(i))).

Now let
f\in\mathcal{D}
be such that
for all
n<\omega
,
h_{n}\leq^{*}f
. Then
\lvert C_{f,A,B}\cap X_{n}\rvert<\omega
for all
n<\omega
, since

\forall^{\infty}m<\omega~A(m)\notin Y_{n}
holds and

h_{n}\leq^{*}f
implies
\lvert C_{f,A,B}\cap\{\langle i,j\rangle:j\in\phi_{n}(i)\}\rvert<\omega
.
∎

Corollary 3.27

(cf. Brendle–Shelah
[
BS99
, Corollary 5.3]
)
.

Let
\kappa<\lambda
be regular cardinals. Then it is consistent that there is a Borel ideal
\mathscr{I}
on
\omega
such that
\non^{*}(\mathscr{I})=\kappa
and
\non^{*}_{\omega}(\mathscr{I})=\lambda
.

Proof.

If
\kappa=\omega
, then
\mathscr{I}
can be taken as
\mathrm{Fin}\otimes\mathrm{Fin}
and assume
\mathfrak{d}=\lambda
. If
\kappa
is uncountable, then
\mathscr{I}
can be taken as
\Fin\otimes\mathcal{ED}_{\mathrm{fin}}
and assume
\cov(\mathcal{M})=\kappa
and
\mathfrak{d}=\lambda
. (By
[
HMM10
, Proposition 3.6]
,
\cov(\mathcal{M})=\min\{\mathfrak{d},\non^{*}(\mathcal{ED}_{\mathrm{fin}})\}
.) Notice that
\non^{*}(\mathscr{I}\otimes\mathscr{J})=\non^{*}(\mathscr{J})
.
∎

Proposition 3.28
.

\non^{*}_{\omega}(\mathrm{nwd})=\non(\mathcal{M})
.

Proof.

To show
\non^{*}_{\omega}(\mathrm{nwd})\leq\non(\mathcal{M})
, let
X\subseteq 2^{\omega}
be such that
X\notin\mathcal{M}
. For
x\in X
let
Y_{x}\coloneqq\{x\mathpunct{\upharpoonright}n:n\in\omega\}\in[2^{<\omega}]^{\omega}
. To see
\{Y_{x}:x\in X\}
witnesses
\non^{*}_{\omega}(\mathrm{nwd})\leq|X|
, let
\{T_{n}:n\in\omega\}
be a countable family of nowhere dense trees in
2^{<\omega}
. Take
x\in X
such that
x\notin\bigcup_{n\in\omega}[T_{n}]
. Then
Y_{x}
is almost disjoint with all
T_{n}
.

To show that
\non(\mathcal{M})\leq\non^{*}_{\omega}(\mathrm{nwd})
, let
\kappa<\mathrm{non}(\mathcal{M})
and let
\{A_{\alpha}:\alpha<\kappa\}\subseteq[2^{<\omega}]^{\omega}
. It suffices to find a countable family
\{T_{n}:n<\omega\}
of nowhere dense trees such that for every
\alpha<\kappa
there is
n<\omega
such that
\rvert A_{\alpha}\cap T_{n}\rvert=\omega
.
We may assume that for each
\alpha<\kappa
,
A_{\alpha}
forms an antichain and there is
x_{\alpha}\in 2^{\omega}
such that for every
i<\omega
all but finitely many members of
A_{\alpha}
extend
x_{\alpha}\upharpoonright i
. Let
\{\sigma_{\alpha}^{n}:n<\omega\}
be an enumeration of
A_{\alpha}
and set
\sigma_{\alpha}^{-1}=\emptyset
for convenience.
We define a sequence
\{a^{n}_{\alpha}:n<\omega\}
such that

a^{n}_{\alpha}=\min\{i<\lvert\sigma_{\alpha}^{n}\rvert:\sigma_{\alpha}^{n}(i)\neq x_{\alpha}(i)\}.

Without loss of generality, we assume that for every
n<\omega,\lvert\sigma_{\alpha}^{n-1}\rvert<a_{\alpha}^{n}<\lvert\sigma_{\alpha}^{n}\rvert
holds. Let
y_{\alpha}\in 2^{\omega}
be defined by

y_{\alpha}(i)=\begin{cases}x_{\alpha}(i)&\text{ if }i\in[\lvert\sigma_{\alpha}^{n-1}\rvert,a_{\alpha}^{n})\text{ for some }n<\omega,\\
\sigma_{\alpha}^{n}(i)&\text{ if }i\in[a_{\alpha}^{n},\lvert\sigma_{\alpha}^{n}\rvert)\text{ for some }n<\omega.\\
\end{cases}

As
\kappa<\mathrm{non}(\mathcal{M})
, the set
\{x_{\alpha},y_{\alpha}:\alpha<\kappa\}
is meager. So there is a real
z\in 2^{\omega}
and an interval partition
\langle I_{n}\rangle_{n\in\omega}
of
\omega
such that

\{x_{\alpha},y_{\alpha}:\alpha<\kappa\}\subseteq\{x\in 2^{\omega}:\forall^{\infty}n\in\omega\,(x\upharpoonright I_{n}\neq z\upharpoonright I_{n})\}

holds (cf.
[
Bla10
, Theorem 5.2]
). For each
n<\omega
, let
T_{n}\subseteq 2^{<\omega}
be a tree such that

[T_{n}]=\{x\in 2^{\omega}:\forall m\geq n\,(x\upharpoonright I_{m}\neq z\upharpoonright I_{m})\}.

It is easy to see that for every
\alpha<\kappa
there is
n\in\omega
such that
A_{\alpha}\cap T_{n}
is infinite.
∎

Now we consider
\non^{*}_{\omega}(\mathscr{I})
for
F_{\sigma}
non-P-ideals. In
[
CM25
, Question 4.11]
, the first and third authors asked the values of
\non^{*}_{\omega}(\mathscr{I})
in the case that
\mathscr{I}=\mathcal{S},\mathcal{ED},\mathcal{ED}_{\mathrm{fin}}
. We give complete answers for
\mathcal{S}
and
\mathcal{ED}
below. To characterize
\non^{*}_{\omega}(\mathcal{S})
, we introduce the following variant of the covering number of the null ideal.

Definition 3.29
.

\operatorname{cov}_{\omega}(\mathcal{N})\coloneqq\min\{\lvert\mathcal{A}\rvert:\mathcal{A}\subseteq\mathcal{N}\land\forall B\in[\mathbb{R}]^{\omega}\,\exists A\in\mathcal{A}\,(B\subseteq A)\}
.

Using the notation in
[
BJ95
, Definition 2.1.3]
,
\operatorname{cov}_{\omega}(\mathcal{N})=\cof([\mathbb{R}]^{\omega},\mathcal{N})
.
In the following,
\mathfrak{r}_{\sigma}
is the “
\omega
-version” of the reaping number
\mathfrak{r}
defined by

\mathfrak{r}_{\sigma}=\min\{|\mathcal{A}|:\mathcal{A}\subseteq\omega^{\omega}\land\forall\overline{B}\in[\mathcal{P}(\omega)]^{\omega}~\exists A\in\mathcal{A}~\forall B\in\overline{B}~(A\subseteq^{*}B\lor A\cap B=^{*}\emptyset)\}.

The question whether
\mathfrak{r}=\mathfrak{r}_{\sigma}
was originally posted in
[
Voj92
]
, and as far as we know, is still open. The reader interested in more information about this cardinal invariant can consult
[
Bla10
]
.

Proposition 3.30
.

(1)

\cov(\mathcal{N})\leq\operatorname{cov}_{\omega}(\mathcal{N})\leq\min\{\non(\mathcal{M}),\mathfrak{r}_{\sigma}\}
.

(2)

\cf(\operatorname{cov}_{\omega}(\mathcal{N}))\geq\omega_{1}
.

Proof.

(1) By definition,
\cov(\mathcal{N})\leq\operatorname{cov}_{\omega}(\mathcal{N})
. To show
\operatorname{cov}_{\omega}(\mathcal{N})\leq\non(\mathcal{M})
, let
X\subseteq\mathbb{R}
be a nonmeager set. Partition
\mathbb{R}
into a null set
A
and a meager set
B
. Given
Y\in[\mathbb{R}]^{\omega}
, it suffices to show that there is
x\in X
such that
Y\subseteq x+A\coloneqq\{x+a:a\in A\}
. Assume otherwise. Then for all
x\in X
there is
y\in Y
such that
y-x\notin A
, i.e.,
y-x\in B
. Thus we have
-X\coloneqq\{-x:x\in\mathbb{R}\}\subseteq\bigcup_{y\in Y}(-y+B)
, which means that a nonmeager set is contained by a meager set, a contradiction.
The proof of
\cov(\mathcal{N})\leq\mathfrak{r}_{\sigma}
is similar to the standard proof of
\cov(\mathcal{N})\leq\mathfrak{r}
: Let
\kappa<\operatorname{cov}_{\omega}(\mathcal{N})
and let
\{A_{\alpha}:\alpha<\kappa\}\subseteq\omega^{\omega}
, we will show that
\{A_{\alpha}:\alpha<\kappa\}
is not a witness for
\mathfrak{r}_{\sigma}
. For all
\alpha<\kappa
, let
N_{\alpha}=\{B\in\omega^{\omega}:A_{\alpha}\subseteq^{*}B\lor A_{\alpha}\cap B=^{*}\emptyset\}
. It is easy to show that each
N_{\alpha}
is Lebesgue null in
\omega^{\omega}
, and since
\kappa<\operatorname{cov}_{\omega}(\mathcal{N})
, there must be a sequence
\langle B_{n}:n<\omega\rangle
such that, for each
\alpha<\kappa
, there is
n<\omega
such that
B_{n}\notin N_{\alpha}
. But then, for each
\alpha<\kappa
there is
n<\omega
such that
|A_{\alpha}\cap B_{n}|=|A_{\alpha}\cap(\omega\setminus B_{n}))|=\omega
, and therefore
\kappa<\mathfrak{r}_{\sigma}
.

(2) Assume on the contrary that there are strictly increasing cardinals
\langle\lambda_{i}\rangle_{i<\omega}
such that
\lambda\coloneqq\sup_{i<\omega}\lambda_{i}=\operatorname{cov}_{\omega}(\mathcal{N})
. Let
\{A_{\alpha}:\alpha<\lambda\}\subseteq\mathcal{N}
witness
\lambda=\operatorname{cov}_{\omega}(\mathcal{N})
. For
i<\omega
, there is
B_{i}\in[\mathbb{R}]^{\omega}
such that
B_{i}\nsubseteq N_{\alpha}
for all
\alpha<\lambda_{i}
. Then
B\coloneq\bigcup_{i<\omega}B_{i}\in[\mathbb{R}]^{\omega}
cannot be covered by any
A_{\alpha}
for
\alpha<\lambda
, which is a contradiction.
∎

Since
\cf(\cov(\mathcal{N}))=\omega
is consistent by
[
She00
]
, we particularly have:

Corollary 3.31
.

\cov(\mathcal{N})<\operatorname{cov}_{\omega}(\mathcal{N})
is consistent.

Proposition 3.32
.

\non^{*}_{\omega}(\mathcal{S})=\operatorname{cov}_{\omega}(\mathcal{N})
.

To prove this, we use the following lemma:

Lemma 3.33

(
[
HMM10
, Lemma 5.5]
)
.

For any
X\subseteq 2^{\omega}
with
\mu^{*}(X)<\frac{1}{2}
, there is
Y\in[\Omega]^{\omega}
such that for any
x\in X
and for all but finitely many
V\in Y
,
x\notin V
.

Proof of
3.32
.

(
\leq
) Let
\mathcal{F}\subseteq\mathcal{N}
witness
|\mathcal{F}|=\operatorname{cov}_{\omega}(\mathcal{N})
. For each
N\in\mathcal{F}
, let
Y_{N}\in[\Omega]^{\omega}
be as in Lemma
3.33
when
X\coloneqq N
. To show that
\{Y_{N}:N\in\mathcal{F}\}
witnesses
|\mathcal{F}|\geq\non^{*}_{\omega}(\mathcal{S})=\min\{|\mathcal{A}|:\mathcal{A}\subseteq[\Omega]^{\omega},(\forall X\in[2^{\omega}]^{\omega})~(\exists Y\in\mathcal{A})~(\forall x\in X)~(\forall^{\infty}V\in Y)~(x\notin V)\}
, let
X\in[2^{\omega}]^{\omega}
and take
N\in\mathcal{F}
such that
X\subseteq N
. Then, for any
x\in X\subseteq N
and for all but finitely many
V\in Y_{N}
, we have
x\notin V
by Lemma
3.33
and hence
\non^{*}_{\omega}(\mathcal{S})\leq|\mathcal{F}|
.

(
\geq
) For
F\in[2^{\omega}]^{<\omega}
let
S_{F}=\{C\in\Omega:F\cap C\neq\emptyset\}
. Let
\kappa<\operatorname{cov}_{\omega}(\mathcal{N})
and let
\{X_{\alpha}:\alpha<\kappa\}\subseteq[\Omega]^{\omega}
. Define
Y_{\alpha}
as
\{x\in 2^{\omega}:x\text{ belongs to infinitely many elements of }X_{\alpha}\}
. We have that
Y_{\alpha}
is of measure greater
\frac{1}{2}
. Note also that if
x\in Y_{\alpha}
then
|S_{\{x\}}\cap X_{\alpha}|=\omega
. Let
Z_{\alpha}=Y_{\alpha}+\mathbb{Q}
. Clearly
Z_{\alpha}
is of full measure. Now, as
\kappa<\operatorname{cov}_{\omega}(\mathcal{N})
, there is
A\in[\mathbb{R}]^{\omega}
such that
A\cap Z_{\alpha}\neq\emptyset
for all
\alpha<\kappa
. Then the collection
\{S_{\{x+q\}}:x\in A,q\in\mathbb{Q}\}
is such that for every
\alpha<\kappa
there are
x\in A
and
q\in\mathbb{Q}
such that
X_{\alpha}\cap S_{\{x+q\}}
is infinite.
∎

Next, we consider the eventual different ideal.

Proposition 3.34
.

\non^{*}_{\omega}(\mathcal{ED})=\cov(\mathcal{M})
.

To prove this, we will use the following lemma:

Lemma 3.35

(
[
BJ95
, Lemma 2.4.2]
,
[
Mil82
]
,
[
Bar87
]
,
[
CM23
, Theorem 5.1]
)
.

For any cardinal
\kappa
, the following are equivalent:

•

\kappa<\cov(\mathcal{M})
.

•

\forall F\in[{\omega}^{\omega}]^{\kappa}~\forall G\in[[{\omega}]^{\omega}]^{\kappa}~\exists g\in{\omega}^{\omega}~\forall f\in F~\forall X\in G~\exists^{\infty}n\in X~(f(n)=g(n))
.

•

\forall F\in[{\omega}^{\omega}]^{\kappa}~\forall h\in\omega^{\omega}:\text{increasing}~\exists S\in\prod_{n<\omega}[\omega]^{\leq h(n)}~\forall f\in F~\exists^{\infty}n<\omega~(f(n)\in S(n))
.

Proof of
3.34
.

To show
\cov(\mathcal{M})\leq\non^{*}_{\omega}(\mathcal{ED})
, let
\mathcal{A}\subseteq[\omega\times\omega]^{\omega}
be of size
<\cov(\mathcal{M})
. It suffices to show that
\mathcal{A}
is not a witness for
\non^{*}_{\omega}(\mathcal{ED})
. For each
A\in\mathcal{A}
,

(1)

A
contains an infinite subset of
n\times\omega
or

(2)

A
contains a graph of a function
f_{A}\colon X_{A}\to\omega
for some infinite set
X_{A}
.

By
3.35
, there is some
g\in{\omega}^{\omega}
such that whenever
A\in\mathcal{A}
satisfies (
2
),
f_{A}(n)=g(n)
holds for infinitely many
n\in X_{A}
. For each
n<\omega
, let

B_{n}=(n\times\omega)\cup\{\langle i,g(i)\rangle:i<\omega\}\in\mathcal{ED}.

Whichever
A\in\mathcal{A}
satisfies (
1
) or (
2
),
\lvert A\cap B_{n}\rvert=\omega
for some
n<\omega
. Thus,
\lvert\mathcal{A}\rvert<\non^{*}_{\omega}(\mathcal{ED})
.

To show
\non^{*}_{\omega}(\mathcal{ED})\leq\cov(\mathcal{M})
, let
F\subseteq{\omega}^{\omega}
of size
<\non^{*}_{\omega}(\mathcal{ED})
. For
f\in F
, let
A_{f}\subseteq\omega\times\omega
be the graph of
f
. Since
\lvert F\rvert<\non^{*}_{\omega}(\mathcal{ED})
, there is
\{B_{i}:i<\omega\}\subseteq\mathcal{ED}
such that for each
f\in F
,
\lvert A_{f}\cap B_{i}\rvert=\omega
for some
i<\omega
. We may assume that every vertical section of
B_{i}
is finite. For each
i<\omega
, we define
S_{i}\in([\omega]^{<\omega})^{\omega}
by
S_{i}(n)=(B_{i})_{n}
for
n<\omega
. Since
B_{i}\in\mathcal{ED}
,
S_{i}\in([\omega]^{\leq k_{i}})^{\omega}
for some
k_{i}<\omega
. Note that for any
f\in F
, there is
i<\omega
such that
\exists^{\infty}n<\omega~f(n)\in S_{i}(n)
. Now let
h\geq^{*}1
be arbitrary. We choose sufficiently slow-growing function
