g\in\omega^{\omega}
so that
\sum_{i\leq g(n)}k_{i}\leq h(n)
for all
n<\omega
. Then we define
S\in\prod_{n<\omega}[\omega]^{\leq h(n)}
by
S(n)=\bigcup_{i\leq g(n)}S_{i}(n)
. Since for all
f\in F
,
\exists^{\infty}n<\omega~f(n)\in S(n)
, we have
\lvert F\rvert<\cov(\mathcal{M})
by
3.35
.
∎

We have nothing more than observation about the values of
\non^{*}_{\omega}(\mathscr{I})
for other
F_{\sigma}
ideals
\mathscr{I}
such as
\mathcal{ED}_{\mathrm{fin}},\mathscr{I}_{L}
and
\mathscr{I}_{P}
:

Lemma 3.36
.

Let
f\in{\omega}^{\omega}
go to the infinity and consider
\mathcal{ED}_{\mathrm{fin}}
on the set
\{(n,m):m\leq 2^{n}\}
, which is
KB
-equivalent to the original
\mathcal{ED}_{\mathrm{fin}}
.
Then, for any
\{I_{i}:i<\omega\}\subseteq\mathcal{ED}_{\mathrm{fin}}
there is
I\in\mathscr{I}_{L}
such that
I_{i}\subseteq^{*}I
for all
i<\omega
.

Corollary 3.37
.

\non^{*}_{\omega}(\mathcal{ED}_{\mathrm{fin}})\leq\non^{*}(\mathscr{I}_{L})
.

Proof.

Let
\mathcal{A}\subseteq[\{(n,m):m\leq 2^{n}\}]^{\omega}
witness
|\mathcal{A}|=\non^{*}(\mathscr{I}_{L})
. Given
\{I_{i}:i<\omega\}\subseteq\mathcal{ED}_{\mathrm{fin}}
, take
I\in\mathscr{I}_{L}
as in
3.36
. Take
A\in\mathcal{A}
almost disjoint from
I
. Then
A
is almost disjoint from all
I_{i}
, so we have
\non^{*}_{\omega}(\mathcal{ED}_{\mathrm{fin}})\leq|\mathcal{A}|=\non^{*}(\mathscr{I}_{L})
.
∎

Remark 3.38
.

A similar argument shows that
\non^{*}_{\omega}(\mathscr{I}_{L})\leq\non^{*}(\mathscr{I}_{P})
. For the summable ideal
\mathscr{I}_{1/n}
, it is known that
\mathscr{I}_{P}\leq_{\mathrm{KB}}\mathscr{I}_{1/n}
and
\non^{*}_{\omega}(\mathscr{I}_{1/n})=\non^{*}(\mathscr{I}_{1/n})\leq\non(\mathcal{N})
(cf.
[
HH07
, Theorem 3.7(2)]
), so
\non^{*}_{\omega}(\mathscr{I}_{P})\leq\non(\mathcal{N})
also holds.

Question 3.39
.

Is there an
F_{\sigma}
ideal
\mathscr{I}
such that
\omega_{1}\leq\non^{*}(\mathscr{I})<\non^{*}_{\omega}(\mathscr{I})
is consistent? Are
\mathcal{ED}_{\mathrm{fin}},\mathscr{I}_{L},\mathscr{I}_{P}
such examples?

We have the following general result for
F_{\sigma}
ideals, which is the “
\omega
-version” of
[
HMM10
, Corollary 4.6(1)]
.

Theorem 3.40
.

For every
F_{\sigma}
ideal
\mathscr{I}
on
\omega
,
\non^{*}_{\omega}(\mathscr{I})\leq\mathfrak{l}
. Therefore, it is consistent that for all
F_{\sigma}
ideals
\mathscr{I}
on
\omega
,
\non^{*}_{\omega}(\mathscr{I})<\mathfrak{b}
holds (e.g. it holds in the Laver model).

Proof.

Let
\varphi
be a lower semi-continuous submeasure such that
\mathrm{Fin}(\varphi)=\mathscr{I}
. Fix an interval partition
\langle P_{n}\rangle_{n<\omega}
of
\omega
such that
\varphi(P_{n})>n^{3}
. For each
n<\omega
, we set
Q_{n}=\{A\subseteq P_{n}:\varphi(A)\leq n^{2}\}
. Let
\{S_{\alpha}:\alpha<\mathfrak{l}\}\subseteq\prod_{n<\omega}[Q_{n}]^{\leq n}
such that for any
H\in\prod_{n<\omega}Q_{n}
, there is
\alpha<\mathfrak{l}
such that
\forall^{\infty}n<\omega\,(H(n)\in S_{\alpha}(n))
. For each
\alpha<\mathfrak{l}
and
n<\omega
, we can pick
a_{\alpha}^{n}\in P_{n}\setminus\bigcup S_{\alpha}(n)
because
\varphi(P_{n})>\varphi(\bigcup S_{\alpha}(n))
. Let
A_{\alpha}=\{a_{\alpha}^{n}:n<\omega\}
.

We claim that
\mathcal{A}=\{A_{\alpha}:\alpha<\mathfrak{l}\}
is a witness for
\non^{*}_{\omega}(\mathscr{I})
. To show this, let
\{X_{n}:n<\omega\}\subseteq\mathscr{I}
be arbitrary. For each
n<\omega
, let
H_{n}
be a function with domain
\omega
defined by

H_{n}(k)=\begin{cases}P_{k}\cap X_{n}&\text{if }\varphi(X_{n})\leq k,\\
\emptyset&\text{if }\varphi(X_{n})>k.\end{cases}

Also, let
H
be a function on
\omega
defined by
H(k)=\bigcup_{n<k}H_{n}(k)
. Then
H\in\prod_{n<\omega}Q_{n}
. So, there is
\alpha<\mathfrak{l}
such that
\forall^{\infty}n<\omega\,(H(n)\in S_{\alpha}(n))
. Note that for all
n<\omega
,
X_{n}\subseteq^{*}\bigcup_{k<\omega}H(k)
, since
\varphi(X_{n})<\infty
. It follows that for all
n<\omega
,
\lvert A_{\alpha}\cap X_{n}\rvert<\omega
.
∎

This can be regarded as a reformulation of the “
\omega
-version” of
[
HMM10
, Theorem 4.3 and Lemma 4.4]
.
Recall that a forcing poset
\mathbb{P}
has the
Laver property
if for any
H\in{\omega}^{\omega}
and any
\mathbb{P}
-name
\dot{f}
of a member of
\prod_{n<\omega}H(n)
,

\Vdash_{\mathbb{P}}\exists A\in\prod_{n<\omega}[H(n)]^{\leq 2^{n}}\cap V~\forall n<\omega~(f(n)\in A(n)).

Theorem 3.41
.

Let
\mathscr{I}
be an
F_{\sigma}
ideal on
\omega
and let
\mathbb{P}
be a poset with the Laver property. Then

(3.2)

\Vdash_{\mathbb{P}}\forall\langle\dot{X_{n}}:n\in\omega\rangle\subseteq\mathscr{I}~\exists X\in[\omega]^{\omega}\cap V~\forall n\in\omega~(|\dot{X_{n}}\cap X|<\omega).

Proof.

Let
\varphi
be a lower semi-continuous submeasure such that
\mathrm{Fin}(\varphi)=\mathscr{I}
and let
\langle\dot{X_{n}}:n\in\omega\rangle
be a sequence of names such that
\Vdash{\forall n\in\omega(\varphi(\dot{X_{n}})\text{ is finite})}
. Find an interval partition of
\omega=\bigcup_{n<\omega}P_{n}
such that
\varphi(P_{n})>n^{2}\cdot 2^{n}
. For each
n\in\omega
find
\dot{H_{n}}
the name of a function such that it is forced that:

\dot{H_{n}}(k)=\begin{cases}\emptyset&\text{if }\varphi(\dot{X_{n}})>k,\\
P_{k}\cap\dot{X_{n}}&\text{if }\varphi(\dot{X_{n}})\leq k.\end{cases}

Observe that
\Vdash{\forall n\in\omega(\varphi(\dot{H_{n}}(k))\leq k)}
. Let
\dot{H}
be a name for a function such that
\dot{H}(k)=\bigcup_{i<k}\dot{H_{i}}(k)
. We will see that, for all
k\in\omega
,

(1)

\Vdash{\varphi(\dot{H}(k))\leq k^{2}}
,

(2)

\Vdash{\dot{H}(k)\subseteq P_{k}}
,

(3)

\Vdash{\dot{X_{k}}\subseteq^{*}\bigcup_{i\in\omega}\dot{H}(i)}
.

1 and 2 are immediate from the definition of
\dot{H}
. To see 3: Let
p\in\mathbb{P}
and let
q\leq p
and
\ell\in\omega
be such that
k<\ell
and
q\Vdash{\varphi(\dot{X}_{k})\leq\ell}
. Then, for every
i\geq\ell
we have that
q\Vdash{\dot{H_{k}}(i)=P_{i}\cap\dot{X_{k}}}
, and therefore, if
n=\max P_{\ell}
, we have that
q\Vdash{\dot{X_{k}}\setminus n\subseteq\bigcup_{i>\ell}\dot{H}(i)}
.

We will now continue with the proof: Since
\dot{H}
is the name of a bounded function, by Laver property, there must be a function
G
such that, for all
k\in\omega

(a)

G(k)
is a list of subsets of
P_{k}
of measure
\leq k^{2}
,

(b)

|G(k)|\leq 2^{k}
,

(c)

\Vdash{\dot{H}(k)\in G(k)}
.

Therefore, for each
k\in\omega
, (1), (2), (a) and (b) imply that
\bigcup G(k)\subseteq P_{k}
and
\varphi(\bigcup G(k))\leq k^{2}\cdot 2^{k}
, so there is
x_{k}\in P_{k}\setminus\bigcup G(k)
. If
X=\{x_{k}:k\in\omega\}
then
X\cap\bigcup_{k\in\omega}\bigcup G(k)=\emptyset
, and therefore, by (3) and (c), we have that for all
k\in\omega
,
\Vdash{X\cap\dot{X_{k}}\text{ is finite}}
.
∎

By carefully choosing functions
f,g\in{\omega}^{\omega}
which would depend on the submeasure associated to
\mathscr{I}
, we can prove Theorem
3.41
for
\langle f,g\rangle
-bounding forcings (see
[
BJ95
, Definition 7.2.13]
and
[
BJ95
, Theorem 7.2.19]
). Therefore, if we consider the forcing
\mathbb{PT}_{f,g}
(see
[
BJ95
, Definition 7.3.3]
and
[
BJ95
, Theorem 7.3.9]
), we get the following result:

Corollary 3.42
.

Let
\mathscr{I}
be a tall
F_{\sigma}
ideal. Then it is consistent that
\mathfrak{b}=\non^{*}_{\omega}(\mathscr{I})<\non(\mathcal{M})
.

Proof.

See
[
BJ95
, Model 7.6.6]
.
∎

Note that the choice of functions
f,g
depends on
\mathscr{I}
, so the argument does not prove the consistency of “
\mathfrak{b}=\non^{*}_{\omega}(\mathscr{I})<\non(\mathcal{M})
for all
F_{\sigma}
ideals
\mathscr{I}
.”

3.3.
Remarks on
\mathscr{I}
-Louveau and
\mathscr{I}^{*}
-Ramsey null ideals

As a direct application of
\omega
-versions of
*
-numbers of ideals, we observe that some results in Brendle–Shelah
[
BS99
]
for ultrafilters actually hold for arbitrary ideals. Brendle–Shelah
[
BS99
]
studied the null ideals of Laver forcing associated to ultrafilters, which they call the Louveau ideals, and computed their cardinal invariants. In
[
CM25
]
, the first and third authors also considered the null ideals of Laver forcing associated to arbitrary ideals.

Definition 3.43
.

For an ideal
\mathscr{I}
on a countable set
X
, the
\mathscr{I}
-Louveau null ideal

L_{\mathscr{I}}
is defined as the
\sigma
-ideal on
X^{\omega}
generated by sets of the form

L_{\phi}=\{x\in X^{\omega}:\exists^{\infty}n<\omega\,(x(n)\in\phi(x\upharpoonright n))\},

where
\phi\colon\omega^{<\omega}\to\mathscr{I}
.

Given an ideal
\mathscr{I}
on a countable set
X
, we say that a tree
T\subseteq X^{<\omega}
is an
\mathscr{I}
-Laver tree
if letting
\sigma
be the stem of
T
, for every
\tau\in T
with
\sigma\subseteq\tau
,
\mathrm{succ}_{T}(\tau)\in\mathscr{I}^{+}
. The
\mathscr{I}
-Laver forcing
, denoted by
\mathbb{L}_{\mathscr{I}}
, is the forcing poset of all
\mathscr{I}
-Laver trees ordered by inclusion. Note that
\mathbb{L}_{\Fin}
is the standard Laver forcing. Then
\mathbb{L}_{\mathscr{I}}
is forcing equivalent to
\mathbb{P}_{L_{\mathscr{I}}}
(see
[
Mil12
]
).
One can also define a variant of
L_{\mathscr{I}}
as follows.

Definition 3.44
.

For an ideal
\mathscr{I}
on a countable set
X
,
J_{\mathscr{I}}
is defined as the
\sigma
-ideal on
X^{\omega}
generated by sets of the form

J_{\phi}=\{x\in X^{\omega}:\exists^{\infty}n<\omega\,(x(n)\in\phi(n))\},

where
\phi\colon\omega\to\mathscr{I}
.

It turned out that the computation in
[
BS99
]
works for arbitrary ideals and there is no difference in the cardinal invariants of
L_{\mathscr{I}}
and
J_{\mathscr{I}}
.

Theorem 3.45

(Brendle–Shelah
[
BS99
, Theorem 2]
)
.

For any ideal
\mathscr{I}
on a countable set, the following hold:

(1)

\add(L_{\mathscr{I}})=\add(J_{\mathscr{I}})=\min\{\mathfrak{b},\add^{*}_{\omega}(\mathscr{I})\}
.

(2)

\non(L_{\mathscr{I}})=\non(J_{\mathscr{I}})=\max\{\mathfrak{d},\non^{*}_{\omega}(\mathscr{I})\}
.

(3)

\cov(L_{\mathscr{I}})=\cov(J_{\mathscr{I}})=\min\{\mathfrak{b},\cov^{*}(\mathscr{I})\}
.

(4)

\cof(L_{\mathscr{I}})=\cof(J_{\mathscr{I}})=\max\{\mathfrak{d},\cof^{*}_{\omega}(\mathscr{I})\}
.

Proof.

(1)

It was already shown that
\min\{\mathfrak{b},\add^{*}_{\omega}(\mathscr{I})\}\leq\add(L_{\mathscr{I}})\leq\add^{*}_{\omega}(\mathscr{I})
in
[
CM25
, Proposition 3.6 and Theorem 4.2]
. The proof can be easily adapted to
J_{\mathscr{I}}
. So, it is enough to show that
\add(L_{\mathscr{I}})\leq\mathfrak{b}
and
\add(J_{\mathscr{I}})\leq\mathfrak{b}
. This follows from the stronger inequality
(\cov(L_{\mathscr{I}})\leq)\cov(J_{\mathscr{I}})\leq\mathfrak{b}
, which will be shown in (3).

(2)

Since
J_{\mathscr{I}}\subseteq L_{\mathscr{I}}
holds,
\non(J_{\mathscr{I}})\leq\non(L_{\mathscr{I}})
. So, it suffices to show that (i)
\mathfrak{d}\leq\non(J_{\mathscr{I}})
, (ii)
\non^{*}_{\omega}(\mathscr{I})\leq\non(J_{\mathscr{I}})
, and (iii)
\non(L_{\mathscr{I}})\leq\max\{\non^{*}_{\omega}(\mathscr{I}),\mathfrak{d}\}
.

(i)

Let
A\notin J_{\mathscr{I}}
. Then for any
f\in\omega^{\omega}
, there is
g\in A\setminus J_{f}
, which implies
f\leq^{*}g
. Therefore,
A
is a dominating family and thus
\lvert A\rvert\geq\mathfrak{d}
.

(ii)

Let
A\notin J_{\mathscr{I}}
. We claim that
\mathcal{A}:=\{\ran(f):f\in A\}
witnesses
\non^{*}_{\omega}(\mathscr{I})
: Given
\overline{X}=\{X_{n}:n<\omega\}\in[\mathscr{I}]^{\omega}
, any
f\in A\setminus J_{\phi}
, where
\phi\colon\omega\to\mathscr{I}
is defined by
\phi(n)=\bigcup_{m\leq n}X_{m}
, satisfies
\lvert\ran(f)\cap X_{n}\rvert<\omega
.

(iii)

To show
\non(L_{\mathscr{I}})\leq\max\{\mathfrak{d},\non^{*}_{\omega}(\mathscr{I})\}
, let
\mathcal{A}
be a witness for
\non^{*}_{\omega}(\mathscr{I})
and let
\mathcal{D}
be a dominating family of functions from
\omega^{<\omega}
to
\omega
. For each
A\in\mathcal{A}
and
f\in\mathcal{D}
, choose
x_{A,f}\in\omega^{\omega}
such that for all
\sigma\in\omega^{<\omega}
,
x_{A,f}(\sigma)\in A\setminus f(\sigma)
. We claim that
\{x_{A,f}:A\in\mathcal{A}\land f\in\mathcal{D}\}\notin L_{\mathscr{I}}
. To show this, let
\phi\colon\omega^{<\omega}\to\mathscr{I}
be arbitrary. Then let
A\in\mathcal{A}
be such that for all
\sigma\in\omega^{<\omega}
,
\lvert A\cap\phi(\sigma)\rvert<\omega
. Then let
f\in\mathcal{D}
be such that for all
\sigma\in\omega^{<\omega}
,
A\cap\phi(\sigma)\subseteq f(\sigma)
. Then
x_{A,f}\notin L_{\phi}
.

(3)

Since
J_{\mathscr{I}}\subseteq L_{\mathscr{I}}
holds,
\cov(J_{\mathscr{I}})\geq\cov(L_{\mathscr{I}})
. So, it suffices to show that (i)
\mathfrak{b}\geq\cov(J_{\mathscr{I}})
, (ii)
\cov^{*}(\mathscr{I})\geq\cov(J_{\mathscr{I}})
, and (iii)
\cov(L_{\mathscr{I}})\geq\min\{\mathfrak{b},\cov^{*}(\mathscr{I})\}
.

(i)

If
\mathcal{B}
is an unbounded family, then
\omega^{\omega}=\bigcup_{f\in\mathcal{B}}J_{f}
.

(ii)

If
\mathcal{A}\subseteq\mathscr{I}
is a witness for
\cov^{*}(\mathscr{I})
, then
\omega^{\omega}=\bigcup_{A\in\mathcal{A}}J_{A}
.

(iii)

Let
\kappa<\min\{\cov^{*}(\mathscr{I}),\mathfrak{b}\}
and let
\mathcal{A}\subseteq L_{\mathscr{I}}
be of size
\kappa
. We want to show that
\omega^{\omega}\neq\bigcup\mathcal{A}
. For each
A\in\mathcal{A}
, let
\phi_{A}\colon\omega^{<\omega}\to\mathscr{I}
such that
A\subseteq L_{\phi_{A}}
. Since
\kappa<\cov^{*}(\mathscr{I})
, we can find
X\in[\omega]^{\omega}
such that for all
A\in\mathcal{A}
and
\sigma\in\omega^{<\omega}
,
\lvert\phi_{A}(\sigma)\cap X\rvert<\omega
. Then for each
A\in\mathcal{A}
, we define
f_{A}\in\omega^{\omega}
by
f_{A}(\sigma)=\min\{m<\omega:\phi_{A}(\sigma)\cap X\subseteq m\}
. Since
\kappa<\mathfrak{b}
, there is a function
g\colon\omega^{<\omega}\to\omega
dominating all
f_{A}
’s. Now take a real
x\in\omega^{\omega}
such that
x(n)\in X\setminus g(x\upharpoonright n)
for all
n<\omega
. Then
x\notin L_{\phi_{A}}
for all
A\in\mathcal{A}
, so
x\notin\bigcup\mathcal{A}
.

(4)

We need to show that (i)
\cof(L_{\mathscr{I}})\leq\max\{\mathfrak{d},\cof^{*}_{\omega}(\mathscr{I})\}
, (ii)
\cof(L_{\mathscr{I}})\geq\cof^{*}_{\omega}(\mathscr{I})
, and (iii)
\cof(L_{\mathscr{I}})\geq\mathfrak{d}
. The proof below can be easily adopted to the proof for
J_{\mathscr{I}}
.

(i)

Let
\mathcal{A}
be a witness for
\cof^{*}_{\omega}(\mathscr{I})
and let
\mathcal{D}
be a dominating family of functions from
\omega^{<\omega}
to
\omega
. For each
\overline{A}:=\{A_{n}:n<\omega\}\in\mathcal{A}
and
f\in\mathcal{D}
, let
\phi_{\overline{A},f}\colon\omega^{<\omega}\to\mathscr{I}
be defined by
\phi_{\overline{A},f}(\sigma)=\bigcup_{i\leq f(\sigma)}A_{i}\cup f(\sigma)
. To see that
\{L_{\phi_{\overline{A},f}}:\overline{A}\in\mathcal{A}\land f\in\mathcal{D}\}
is a base of
L_{\mathscr{I}}
, let
\psi\colon\omega^{<\omega}\to\mathscr{I}
be arbitrary. Take
\overline{A}:=\{A_{n}:n<\omega\}\in\mathcal{A}
such that for each
\sigma\in\omega^{<\omega}
, there is
g(\sigma)<\omega
with
\psi(\sigma)\subseteq^{*}A_{g(\sigma)}
. Let
h\colon\omega^{<\omega}\to\omega
be such that
\psi(\sigma)\subseteq A_{g(\sigma)}\cup h(\sigma)
for each
\sigma\in\omega^{<\omega}
. Take
f\in\mathcal{D}
which dominates
g,h
. To see
L_{\psi}\subseteq L_{\phi_{\overline{A},f}}
, let
x\in L_{\psi}
be arbitrary. Then for infinitely many
n<\omega
,
x(n)\in\psi(x\mathpunct{\upharpoonright}n)\subseteq A_{g(x\mathpunct{\upharpoonright}n)}\cup h(x\mathpunct{\upharpoonright}n)\subseteq\bigcup_{i\leq f(x\mathpunct{\upharpoonright}n)}A_{i}\cup f(x\mathpunct{\upharpoonright}n)=\phi_{\overline{A},f}(x\mathpunct{\upharpoonright}n)
, which implies
x\in L_{\phi_{\overline{A},f}}
.

(ii)

Let
\mathcal{A}\subseteq L_{\mathscr{I}}
be a base of
L_{\mathscr{I}}
. For any
A\in\mathcal{A}
, let
\phi_{A}\colon\omega^{<\omega}\to\mathscr{I}
be such that
A\subseteq L_{\phi_{A}}
. We claim that
\overline{\mathcal{A}}=\{\mathrm{ran}(\phi_{A}):A\in\mathcal{A}\}
is a witness for
\cof^{*}_{\omega}(\mathscr{I})
. Let
\overline{B}:=\{B_{n}:n<\omega\}\in[\mathscr{I}]^{\omega}
be arbitrary. Using some function
f\colon\omega\to\omega
such that
\lvert f^{-1}[\{n\}]\rvert=\omega
for all
n<\omega
, we define
\phi\colon\omega^{<\omega}\to\mathscr{I}
by
\phi(\sigma)=B_{f(\lvert\sigma\rvert)}
. Take
A\in\mathcal{A}
such that
L_{\phi}\subseteq A
. Suppose that there is
B\in\overline{B}
such that for all
\sigma\in\omega^{<\omega}
,
B\not\subseteq^{*}\phi_{A}(\sigma)
. Then we can find
x\in\omega^{\omega}
such that
x(n)\in B\setminus\phi_{A}(x\upharpoonright n)
for all
n<\omega
, which implies
x\in L_{\phi}\setminus A
. This contradicts the choice of
A
.

(iii)

\cof(L_{\mathscr{I}})\geq\mathfrak{d}
follows from
\cof(L_{\mathscr{I}})\geq\non(L_{\mathscr{I}})\geq\mathfrak{d}
.

∎

Brendle–Shelah
[
BS99
]
also studied the null ideals of Mathias forcing associated to ultrafilters, which they call the Ramsey ideals, and computed their cardinal invariants.

Definition 3.46
.

Let
\mathscr{I}
be an ideal on a countable set
X
. We define the
\mathscr{I}^{*}
-Mathias forcing

\mathbb{R}_{\mathscr{I}^{*}}
as follows: the conditions are pairs
\langle s,A\rangle\in[X]^{<\omega}\times\mathscr{I}^{*}
such that
\max(s)<\min(A)
. The order is defined by

\langle t,B\rangle\leq\langle s,A\rangle\iff t\supseteq s\land B\subseteq A\land t\setminus s\subseteq A.

For
\langle s,A\rangle\in\mathbb{R}_{\mathscr{I}^{*}}
, we write

[s,A]=\{Y\in[X]^{\omega}:s\subseteq Y\subseteq s\cup A\}.

The
\mathscr{I}^{*}
-Ramsey null ideal

R_{\mathscr{I}^{*}}
consists of
Y\subseteq[X]^{\omega}
such that

\forall\langle s,A\rangle\in\mathbb{R}_{\mathscr{I}}\,\exists\langle t,B\rangle\leq\langle s,A\rangle\,(Y\cap[t,B]=\emptyset).

By definition, one can see that
\mathbb{R}_{\mathscr{I}^{*}}
is forcing equivalent to
\mathbb{P}_{R_{\mathscr{I}^{*}}}
. Brendle–Shelah
[
BS99
]
showed the theorem below only for ultrafilters, but their proof actually works for arbitrary filters. We leave the details to the careful readers.

Theorem 3.47

(Brendle–Shelah
[
BS99
, Theorem 1]
)
.

For any tall ideal
\mathscr{I}
on a countable set, the following holds:

\add(R_{\mathscr{I}^{*}})=\add^{*}(\mathscr{I}),\non(R_{\mathscr{I}^{*}})=\non^{*}(\mathscr{I}),\cov(R_{\mathscr{I}^{*}})=\cov^{*}(\mathscr{I}),\cof(R_{\mathscr{I}^{*}})=\cof^{*}_{\omega}(\mathscr{I}).

In contrast to
3.45
and
3.47
, we will see that the computation of cardinal invariants associated to
\mathscr{I}
-Miller null ideals are more complicated. Notably, uniformity and covering numbers of
\mathscr{I}
-Miller null ideals are not determined by the
*
-numbers of
\mathscr{I}
. Moreover, it is consistent that
M_{\mathscr{I}}
and
K_{\mathscr{I}}
have different uniformity and covering numbers for some ideal
\mathscr{I}
, e.g.
\mathrm{Fin}\otimes\mathrm{Fin}
and
\mathcal{ED}
.

4.
Computation of cardinal invariants of
M_{\mathscr{I}}

In this section, we discuss
\mathsf{ZFC}
-provable results on cardinal invariants of the
\sigma
-ideals
M_{\mathscr{I}}
and
K_{\mathscr{I}}
.

4.1.
Additivity and cofinality

It turns out that the additivity and cofinality of
M_{\mathscr{I}}
and
K_{\mathscr{I}}
are the same as
L_{\mathscr{I}}
and
J_{\mathscr{I}}
, which are characterized using the
\omega
-version of the
*
-additivity and cofinality of the original ideal
\mathscr{I}
, as in
3.45
. Namely, we obtain the following equalities for any ideal
\mathscr{I}
:

\displaystyle\add(M_{\mathscr{I}})=\add(K_{\mathscr{I}})

\displaystyle=\min\{\mathfrak{b},\add^{*}_{\omega}(\mathscr{I})\},

\displaystyle\cof(M_{\mathscr{I}})=\cof(K_{\mathscr{I}})

\displaystyle=\max\{\mathfrak{d},\cof^{*}_{\omega}(\mathscr{I})\}.

We start with the following bounds of
\add(M_{\mathscr{I}})
and
\cof(M_{\mathscr{I}})
, some of which were mentioned by the first and third authors in
[
CM25
]
:

Lemma 4.1
.

Let
\mathscr{I}
be an ideal on a countable set.

(1)

(Cieślak–Martínez-Celis
[
CM25
, Proposition 3.6, Theorem 4.2]
)

\min\{\mathfrak{b},\add^{*}_{\omega}(\mathscr{I})\}\leq\add(M_{\mathscr{I}})\leq\add^{*}_{\omega}(\mathscr{I})
.

(2)

\cof^{*}_{\omega}(\mathscr{I})\leq\cof(M_{\mathscr{I}})\leq\max\{\mathfrak{d},\cof^{*}_{\omega}(\mathscr{I})\}
.

The same inequalities hold for
K_{\mathscr{I}}
as well.

Proof.

We may assume that
\mathscr{I}
is an ideal on
\omega
. We will give proofs only for
M_{\mathscr{I}}
because the essentially same argument also proves the lemma for
K_{\mathscr{I}}
.

(
1
) The inequality is stated in
[
CM25
, Proposition 3.6 & Theorem 4.2]
without a proof. To see
\min\{\mathfrak{b},\add^{*}_{\omega}(\mathscr{I})\}\leq\add(M_{\mathscr{I}})
, let
\Phi
be a family of functions from
\omega^{<\omega}
to
\mathscr{I}
such that
\lvert\Phi\rvert<\min\{\add^{*}_{\omega}(\mathscr{I}),\mathfrak{b}\}
and we show that
\{M_{\phi}\colon\phi\in\Phi\}
is not a witness for
\add(M_{\mathscr{I}})
. Since
\{\phi(s):\phi\in\Phi\land s\in\omega^{<\omega}\}
has size
<\add^{*}_{\omega}(\mathscr{I})
, there is
\{B_{n}:n<\omega\}\subseteq\mathscr{I}
such that for all
\phi\in\Phi
and
s\in\omega^{<\omega}
, there is
n<\omega
such that
\phi(s)\subseteq^{*}B_{n}
. We may assume that
\langle B_{n}:n<\omega\rangle
is
\subseteq
-increasing and
n\subseteq B_{n}
for each
n<\omega
. Then for each
\phi\in\Phi
, we can define
f_{\phi}\colon\omega^{<\omega}\to\omega
by letting
f_{\phi}(s)
be the least
n<\omega
such that
\phi(s)\subseteq B_{n}
. Since
\lvert\Phi\rvert<\mathfrak{b}
, there is
f\colon\omega^{<\omega}\to\omega
such that for all
x\in\omega^{\omega}
,

\forall^{\infty}n<\omega~f_{\phi}(x\upharpoonright n)\leq f(x\upharpoonright n).

Define
\psi\colon\omega^{<\omega}\to\mathscr{I}
by
\psi(s)\coloneqq B_{f(s)}
. Then
M_{\phi}\subseteq M_{\psi}
for all
\phi\in\Phi
because if
x\in M_{\phi}
, then for all but finitely many
n<\omega
,

x(n)\in\phi(x\upharpoonright n)\subseteq B_{f_{\phi}(x\upharpoonright n)}\subseteq B_{f(x\upharpoonright n)}=\psi(x\upharpoonright n)

holds and hence
x\in M_{\psi}
.

To see
\add(M_{\mathscr{I}})\leq\add_{\omega}^{*}(\mathscr{I})
, let
\mathcal{A}\subseteq\mathscr{I}
of size
<\add(M_{\mathscr{I}})
and we show that
\mathcal{A}
is not a witness for
\add_{\omega}^{*}(\mathscr{I})
. For
A\in\mathcal{A}
, let
\phi_{A}\colon\omega^{<\omega}\to\mathscr{I}
denote the constant function of the value
A
. Since
\lvert\mathcal{A}\rvert<\add(M_{\mathscr{I}})
, there is
\psi\colon\omega^{<\omega}\to\mathscr{I}
such that
M_{\phi_{A}}\subseteq M_{\psi}
for all
A\in\mathcal{A}
. We claim that for any
A\in\mathcal{A}
,
A\subseteq^{*}\psi(s)
for all
s\in\omega^{<\omega}
. Suppose not. Then for some
A\in\mathcal{A}
, one can find
x\in\omega^{\omega}
such that
x(n)\in A\setminus\psi(x\upharpoonright n)
for
n<\omega
. Then
x
witnesses
M_{\phi_{A}}\not\subseteq M_{\psi}
, which is a contradiction.

(
2
) To show
\cof^{*}_{\omega}(\mathscr{I})\leq\cof(M_{\mathscr{I}})
, let
\{M_{\phi_{\alpha}}:\alpha<\kappa\}
be a basis of
M_{\mathscr{I}}
. We claim that
\{\mathrm{ran}(\phi_{\alpha}):\alpha<\kappa\}
is a witness for
\cof^{*}_{\omega}(\mathscr{I})
; Suppose otherwise. Then there is
\{B_{i}:i<\omega\}\subseteq\mathscr{I}
such that

\forall\alpha<\kappa\,\exists i_{\alpha}<\omega\,\forall s\in\omega^{<\omega}~\lvert B_{i_{\alpha}}\setminus\phi_{\alpha}(s)\rvert=\omega.

Then for each
\alpha<\kappa
, one can find
x\in\omega^{\omega}
such that for all
n<\omega
,
x(n)\in B_{i_{\alpha}}\setminus\phi_{\alpha}(x\upharpoonright n)
and thus
x\in M_{B_{i_{\alpha}}}\setminus M_{\phi_{\alpha}}
. Therefore, no
M_{\phi_{\alpha}}
covers
\bigcup_{i<\omega}M_{B_{i}}
, which contradicts the choice of
M_{\phi_{\alpha}}
’s.

Now we show
\cof(M_{\mathscr{I}})\leq\max\{\mathfrak{d},\cof^{*}_{\omega}(\mathscr{I})\}
. Let
\{\overline{A}_{\alpha}:\alpha<\cof^{*}_{\omega}(\mathscr{I})\}
be a witness for
\cof^{*}_{\omega}(\mathscr{I})
. For each
\alpha<\cof^{*}_{\omega}(\mathscr{I})
, we write
\overline{A}_{\alpha}=\{A_{\alpha}^{i}:i<\omega\}
.
Also, let
\{f_{\beta}:\beta<\mathfrak{d}\}
be a dominating family of functions from
\omega^{<\omega}
to
\omega
. We may assume that each
f_{\beta}
is increasing along branches, i.e.
s\subseteq t
implies that
f_{\beta}(s)\leq f_{\beta}(t)
. For every
\alpha<\cof^{*}_{\omega}(\mathscr{I})
and
\beta<\mathfrak{d}
, we define
\phi_{\alpha,\beta}\colon\omega^{<\omega}\to\mathscr{I}
by

\phi_{\alpha,\beta}(s)=\bigcup_{i=0}^{f_{\beta}(s)}A_{\alpha}^{i}\cup f_{\beta}(s)

for all
s\in\omega^{<\omega}
. We claim the collection
\{M_{\phi_{\alpha,\beta}}:\alpha<\cof^{*}_{\omega}(\mathscr{I})\land\beta<\mathfrak{d}\}
is a basis of
M_{\mathscr{I}}
; Take any set
M_{\psi}
, where
\psi\colon\omega^{<\omega}\to\mathscr{I}
. Let
\alpha<\cof^{*}_{\omega}(\mathscr{I})
be such that for each
s\in\omega^{<\omega}
there is
i<\omega
with
\psi(s)\subseteq^{*}A_{\alpha}^{i}
. Then let
g,h\colon\omega^{<\omega}\to\omega
be functions such that for
s\in\omega^{<\omega}
,

\psi(s)\subseteq A_{\alpha}^{g(s)}\cup h(s).

There is a
\beta<\mathfrak{d}
such that
f_{\beta}
dominates both
g
and
h
. Then we have
M_{\psi}\subseteq M_{\phi_{\alpha,\beta}}
, because if
x\in M_{\psi}
then for all but finitely many
n
,

x(n)\in\psi(x\upharpoonright n)\subseteq A_{\alpha}^{g(x\upharpoonright n)}\cup h(x\upharpoonright n)\subseteq\bigcup_{i=0}^{f_{\beta}(x\upharpoonright n)}A_{\alpha}^{i}\cup f_{\beta}(x\upharpoonright n)=\phi_{\alpha,\beta}(x\upharpoonright n)

holds and thus
x\in M_{\phi_{\alpha,\beta}}
. This completes the proof for
M_{\mathscr{I}}
, but also this argument works for
K_{\mathscr{I}}
.
∎

The following lemma is used to prove
\add(K_{\mathscr{I}})\leq\mathfrak{b}
and
\cof(K_{\mathscr{I}})\geq\mathfrak{d}
:

Lemma 4.2
.

Let
\phi,\psi\colon\omega\to\mathscr{I}
and assume that
\phi
does not take empty values. Then
K_{\phi}\subseteq K_{\psi}
implies
\forall^{\infty}n<\omega~\phi(n)\subseteq\psi(n)
.

Proof.

Assume that there is
D\in[\omega]^{\omega}
such that
\phi(n)\not\subseteq\psi(n)
for every
n\in D
. Take some
x\in\omega^{\omega}
such that
x(n)\in\phi(n)\setminus\psi(n)
for
n\in D
and
x(n)\in\phi(n)
for
n\notin D
. Then
x\notin K_{\phi}\setminus K_{\psi}
.
∎

Lemma 4.3
.

\add(K_{\mathscr{I}})\leq\mathfrak{b}
and
\cof(K_{\mathscr{I}})\geq\mathfrak{d}
.

Proof.

To see
\add(K_{\mathscr{I}})\leq\mathfrak{b}
, let
F\subseteq\omega^{\omega}
of size
<\add(K_{\mathscr{I}})
and it suffices to show that
F
is not an unbounded family. We may assume
f(n)>0
for all
f\in F
and
n<\omega
. Since
\omega\subseteq\Fin\subseteq\mathscr{I}
,
F\subseteq\mathscr{I}^{\omega}
and hence there is
\psi\in\mathscr{I}^{\omega}
such that
K_{f}\subseteq K_{\psi}
for all
f\in F
. By
4.2
,
\forall^{\infty}n<\omega~f(n)\subseteq\psi(n)
holds for all
f\in F
. Then a function
g\in\omega^{\omega}
defined by
g(n)\coloneqq\min(\omega\setminus\psi(n))
dominates
F
, which completes the proof. One can prove
\cof(K_{\mathscr{I}})\geq\mathfrak{d}
in a dual manner.
∎

In the case of
M_{\mathscr{I}}
, the idea is the same, but we need to be more careful.

Definition 4.4
.

For
\sigma,\tau\in\omega^{<\omega}
and
\phi\colon\omega^{<\omega}\to\mathscr{I}
, we write
\sigma\subseteq_{\phi}\tau
if
\sigma\subseteq\tau
and for all
n\in|\tau|\setminus|\sigma|
,
\tau(n)\in\phi(\tau\mathpunct{\upharpoonright}n)
.

Lemma 4.5
.

Let
\phi,\psi\colon\omega^{<\omega}\to\mathscr{I}
and assume that
\phi
does not take empty values. Then
M_{\phi}\subseteq M_{\psi}
implies
\forall\sigma\in\omega^{<\omega}\exists\tau\supseteq_{\phi}\sigma\forall\rho\supseteq_{\phi}\tau~\phi(\rho)\subseteq\psi(\rho)
.

Proof.

Assume otherwise. By inductive construction, we can find
x\in M_{\phi}
such that
x(n)\in\phi(x\mathpunct{\upharpoonright}n)\setminus\psi(x\mathpunct{\upharpoonright}n)
for infinitely many
n<\omega
and hence
x\in M_{\phi}\setminus M_{\psi}
.
∎

Definition 4.6
.

For
x\in{\omega}^{\omega}
and
g\colon\omega^{<\omega}\to\omega
, we say that
x
is
adaptively dominated
by
g
, denoted by
x\trianglelefteq^{*}g
, if
x(n)\leq g(x\mathpunct{\upharpoonright}n)
for all but finitely many
n<\omega
.

Lemma 4.7

(see e.g.
[
Bla10
, Table 1, Page 463]
)
.

Every unbounded family is
\trianglelefteq^{*}
-unbounded. Namely, for any (
\leq^{*}
-)unbounded family
B\subseteq{\omega}^{\omega}
and
g\colon\omega^{<\omega}\to\omega
, there is
x\in B
such that
\lnot(x\trianglelefteq^{*}g)
. In addition, the smallest sizes of a
\trianglelefteq^{*}
-unbounded family and a
\trianglelefteq^{*}
-dominating family are
\mathfrak{b}
and
\mathfrak{d}
, respectively.

Proof.

Let
B\subseteq\omega^{\omega}
be an unbounded family and
g\colon\omega^{<\omega}\to\omega
. Fix an enumeration
\{\sigma_{i}:i<\omega\}
of
\omega^{<\omega}
and for each
i<\omega
, let
T^{i}\subseteq\omega^{<\omega}
be the tree given by

\tau\in T^{i}\iff(\tau\subseteq\sigma_{i})\lor(\sigma_{i}\subseteq\tau\land\forall n\in\lvert\tau\rvert\setminus\lvert\sigma_{i}\rvert~\tau(n)\leq g(\tau\upharpoonright n))

and let
f^{i}\in\omega^{\omega}
be given by
f^{i}(n)=\max\{t(n):t\in T^{i}\cap\omega^{n+1}\}
. Also, we define
f\colon\omega\to\omega
by
f(n)=\max\{f^{i}(n):i\leq n\}
. Since
B\subseteq{\omega}^{\omega}
is an unbounded family, there is
x\in B
such that
x(n)>f(n)
for infinitely many
n<\omega
. Assume toward a contradiction that there exists
m<\omega
such that for all
n\geq m
,
x(n)\leq g(x\mathpunct{\upharpoonright}n)
. Let
i<\omega
be such that
x\mathpunct{\upharpoonright}m=\sigma_{i}
. Then
x
is a branch of
T^{i}
, so
x(n)\leq f(n)
for all
n\geq\max\{m,i\}
, which is a contradiction. The latter statement is straightforward.
∎

Lemma 4.8
.

\add(M_{\mathscr{I}})\leq\mathfrak{b}
and
\cof(M_{\mathscr{I}})\geq\mathfrak{d}
.

Proof.

To see
\add(M_{\mathscr{I}})\leq\mathfrak{b}
, let
F\subseteq{\omega}^{\omega}
of size
<\add(M_{\mathscr{I}})
. It suffices to find
g\colon\omega^{<\omega}\to\omega
such that
f\trianglelefteq^{*}g
for all
f\in F
by
4.7
. For
f\in F
, we define
\phi_{f}\colon\omega^{<\omega}\to\mathscr{I}
by
\phi_{f}(\sigma)=f(\lvert\sigma\rvert)+1
. Since
\lvert F\rvert<\add(M_{\mathscr{I}})
, there is
\psi\colon\omega^{<\omega}\to\mathscr{I}
such that
M_{\phi_{f}}\subseteq M_{\psi}
for all
f\in F
. Fix an enumeration
\{\tau_{i}:i<\omega\}
of
\omega^{<\omega}
.
For each
x\in\omega^{\leq\omega}
and
i<\omega
, we define
x^{i}\in\omega^{\leq\omega}
by
\dom(x^{i})=\dom(x),x^{i}(n)=\tau_{i}(n)
for
n<\lvert\tau_{i}\rvert
and
x^{i}(n)=x(n)
for
n\geq\lvert\tau_{i}\rvert
. We also define
\psi^{\prime}\colon\omega^{<\omega}\to\mathscr{I}
and
g\colon\omega^{<\omega}\to\omega
by

\displaystyle\psi^{\prime}(\sigma)

\displaystyle=\bigcup\{\psi(\sigma^{i}):i\leq|\sigma|\},

\displaystyle g(\sigma)

\displaystyle=\min(\omega\setminus\psi^{\prime}(\sigma)).

For each
f\in F
, by
4.5
(putting
\sigma\coloneqq\emptyset
), there is
i_{f}<\omega
such that

(4.1)

\forall\rho\supseteq_{\phi_{f}}\tau_{i_{f}}~(\phi_{f}(\rho)\subseteq\psi(\rho)).

Fix
f\in F
for now and write
i\coloneqq i_{f}
and
\phi\coloneqq\phi_{f}
. For
n\geq\rvert\tau_{i}\lvert
, since
f^{i}(n)=f(n)\in f(n)+1=\phi(f^{i}\mathpunct{\upharpoonright}n)
, we have
f^{i}\mathpunct{\upharpoonright}n\supseteq_{\phi}f^{i}\mathpunct{\upharpoonright}\lvert\tau_{i}\rvert=\tau_{i}
. Then by (
4.1
), for
n\geq\max\{\lvert\tau_{i}\rvert,i\}
,

f(n)+1=\phi(f^{i}\mathpunct{\upharpoonright}n)\subseteq\psi(f^{i}\mathpunct{\upharpoonright}n)\subseteq\psi^{\prime}(f\mathpunct{\upharpoonright}n),

which implies
f(n)\leq g(f\mathpunct{\upharpoonright}n)
. Since
f\in F
was arbitrary, we have
f\trianglelefteq^{*}g
for all
f\in F
. One can prove
\cof(M_{\mathscr{I}})\geq\mathfrak{d}
in a dual manner.
∎

Together with
4.1
, we obtain:

Theorem 4.9
.

For any ideal
\mathscr{I}
on a countable set, the following holds:

\displaystyle\add(K_{\mathscr{I}})=\add(M_{\mathscr{I}})

\displaystyle=\min\{\mathfrak{b},\add^{*}_{\omega}(\mathscr{I})\}

\displaystyle\cof(K_{\mathscr{I}})=\cof(M_{\mathscr{I}})

\displaystyle=\max\{\mathfrak{d},\cof^{*}_{\omega}(\mathscr{I})\}.

Most of the ideals
\mathscr{I}
in Table
1
satisfy
\add^{*}_{\omega}(\mathscr{I})\leq\mathfrak{b}
and
\cof^{*}_{\omega}(\mathscr{I})\geq\mathfrak{d}
and hence
\add(K_{\mathscr{I}})=\add(M_{\mathscr{I}})=\add^{*}_{\omega}(\mathscr{I})
and
\cof(K_{\mathscr{I}})=\cof(M_{\mathscr{I}})=\cof^{*}_{\omega}(\mathscr{I})
for such
\mathscr{I}
. However, this is not always the case for any ideal
\mathscr{I}
:

Corollary 4.10
.

(1)

It is consistent that for any gradually fragmented ideal
\mathscr{I}
,
\add(K_{\mathscr{I}})=\add(M_{\mathscr{I}})=\mathfrak{b}<\add^{*}_{\omega}(\mathscr{I})
holds.

(2)

It is consistent that for any gradually fragmented ideal
\mathscr{I}
,
\cof^{*}_{\omega}(\mathscr{I})<\mathfrak{d}=\cof(K_{\mathscr{I}})=\cof(M_{\mathscr{I}})
holds.

Proof.

(1)

Work in the model constructed in
[
Paw85
]
where
\mathfrak{b}<\add_{t}(\mathcal{N})
holds. Let
\mathscr{I}
be a gradually fragmented ideal. Then,
\add_{t}(\mathcal{N})\leq\add^{*}_{\omega}(\mathscr{I})
by
3.16
.

(2)

Work in the Laver model and let
\mathscr{I}
be a gradually fragmented ideal. Then,
\cof^{*}_{\omega}(\mathscr{I})<\mathfrak{b}=\mathfrak{d}
by
3.21
.

∎

4.2.
Uniformity and covering

We first observe that there are natural bounds for uniformity and covering numbers of
K_{\mathscr{I}}
and
M_{\mathscr{I}}
.

Lemma 4.11
.

For any ideal
\mathscr{I}
on a countable set,

\displaystyle\mathfrak{b}\leq\non(K_{\mathscr{I}})

\displaystyle\leq\non(M_{\mathscr{I}})\leq\non(\mathcal{M})

\displaystyle\cov(\mathcal{M})\leq\cov(M_{\mathscr{I}})

\displaystyle\leq\cov(K_{\mathscr{I}})\leq\mathfrak{d}.

Proof.

We may assume that
\mathscr{I}
is an ideal on
\omega
. Then we have

K_{\sigma}\subseteq K_{\mathscr{I}}\subseteq M_{\mathscr{I}}\subseteq\mathcal{M},

where
K_{\sigma}
is the ideal of
\sigma
-compact sets of the Baire space. This implies the claim, since
\non(K_{\sigma})=\mathfrak{b}
and
\cov(K_{\sigma})=\mathfrak{d}
.
∎

Proposition 4.12
.

K_{\mathscr{I}}=M_{\mathscr{I}}
if and only if
\mathscr{I}=\Fin
.

Proof.

K_{\Fin}=M_{\Fin}=K_{\sigma}
is easy.
When
\mathscr{I}\neq\Fin
, fix an infinite set
A=\{a_{0}<a_{1}<\cdots\}\in\mathscr{I}
and inductively construct an
\mathscr{I}
-branching tree
T\subseteq\omega^{<\omega}
as follows: for every
k<\omega
and
\sigma\in\omega^{2k}
,
\suc_{T}(\sigma)\coloneqq A
and
\suc_{T}(\sigma^{\frown}\langle a_{i}\rangle)\coloneqq\{i\}
. Clearly
T\in M_{\mathscr{I}}
, so we show
T\notin K_{\mathscr{I}}
. Let
\phi\colon\omega\to\mathscr{I}
be arbitrary. For each
k<\omega
, take a natural number
i_{k}\notin\phi(2k+1)
and define
x\in{\omega}^{\omega}
by
x(2k)\coloneqq a_{i_{k}}
and
x(2k+1)\coloneqq i_{k}
. Then,
x\in T
but for any
k<\omega
,
x(2k+1)\notin\phi(2k+1)
. Since
\phi
was arbitrary, we have
T\notin K_{\mathscr{I}}
.
∎

Lemma 4.13

(
[
CM25
, Proposition 4.16]
,
[
CGM+24
, Lemma 4.3]
)
.

Let
\mathscr{I},\mathscr{J}
be ideals on
\omega
such that
\mathscr{I}\leq_{\mathrm{K}}\mathscr{J}
. Then
\non(M_{\mathscr{I}})\leq\non(M_{\mathscr{J}})
and
\cov(M_{\mathscr{I}})\geq\cov(M_{\mathscr{J}})
hold. The same also holds for
K_{\mathscr{I}}
and
K_{\mathscr{J}}
.

Proof.

We only prove
\non(M_{\mathscr{I}})\leq\non(M_{\mathscr{J}})
.
Let
f\in{\omega}^{\omega}
witness
\mathscr{I}\leq_{\mathrm{K}}\mathscr{J}
.
Assume
F\subseteq{\omega}^{\omega}
of size
<\non(M_{\mathscr{I}})
is given.
For
y\in{\omega}^{\omega}
, define
y^{\prime}\in{\omega}^{\omega}
by
y^{\prime}(n)=f(y(n))
for
n<\omega
.
Since
F^{\prime}\coloneqq\{y^{\prime}:y\in F\}
has size
<\non(M_{\mathscr{I}})
,
there is
\phi\colon\omega^{<\omega}\to\mathscr{I}
such that
F^{\prime}\subseteq M_{\phi}
.
Define
\psi\colon\omega^{<\omega}\to\mathscr{J}
by
\psi(\sigma)\coloneqq f^{-1}(\phi(\sigma))
for
\sigma\in\omega^{<\omega}
. Then we have
F\subseteq M_{\psi}
.
∎

Theorem 4.14
.

For every ideal
\mathscr{I}
on a countable set,

\displaystyle\non(M_{\mathscr{I}})

\displaystyle\leq\max\{\mathfrak{b},\non^{*}_{\omega}(\mathscr{I})\},

\displaystyle\cov(M_{\mathscr{I}})

\displaystyle\geq\min\{\mathfrak{d},\cov^{*}(\mathscr{I})\}.

Proof.

We may assume that
\mathscr{I}
is an ideal on
\omega
. We only prove the former statement, since the latter is easier. Let
B\subseteq{\omega}^{\omega}
and
\cal{A}\subseteq[{\omega}]^{\omega}
witness
\mathfrak{b}=\lvert B\rvert
and
\non^{*}_{\omega}(\mathscr{I})=\lvert\mathcal{A}\rvert
.
For
A\in[{\omega}]^{\omega}
and
f\in{\omega}^{\omega}
, we define
h(A,f)\in{\omega}^{\omega}
by

h(A,f)(n)=\min(A\setminus f(n)).

We claim that
F\coloneqq\left\{{h(A,f)}:{A\in\cal{A},f\in B}\right\}
is not in
M_{\mathscr{I}}
. Given
\phi\colon\omega^{<\omega}\to\mathscr{I}
,
there is
A\in\cal{A}
such that for all
\sigma\in\omega^{<\omega}
,
\lvert A\cap\phi(\sigma)\rvert<\omega
. Let
g\colon\omega^{<\omega}\to\omega
be such that
A\cap\phi(\sigma)\subseteq g(\sigma)
for all
\sigma\in\omega^{<\omega}
. Notice that
\{h(A,f):f\in B\}
is also an unbounded family since
h(A,f)(n)\geq f(n)
for
f\in B
and
n<\omega
.
By
4.7
, there is
f\in B
such that
h(n)>g(h\mathpunct{\upharpoonright}n)
for infinitely many
n<\omega
, where
h\coloneqq h(A,f)
. Then
h(n)\in A\setminus\varphi(h\mathpunct{\upharpoonright}n)
for such
n<\omega
, so
h\in F\setminus M_{\phi}
.
∎

Remark 4.15
.

The converse inequalities of
4.14
cannot be proved in general, witnessed by
\mathscr{I}=\mathrm{Fin}\otimes\mathrm{Fin}
:
\non(M_{\mathrm{Fin}\otimes\mathrm{Fin}})\leq\non(\mathcal{M})
by
4.11
, but
\max\{\mathfrak{b},\non^{*}_{\omega}(\mathrm{Fin}\otimes\mathrm{Fin})\}=\mathfrak{d}
by
3.25
.

By
3.24
, we have:

Corollary 4.16
.

If
\mathscr{I}
is either
\mathcal{R}
or
\mathrm{conv}
,
\non(M_{\mathscr{I}})=\non(K_{\mathscr{I}})=\mathfrak{b}
and
\cov(M_{\mathscr{I}})=\cov(K_{\mathscr{I}})=\mathfrak{d}
.

By
3.42
, we have:

Corollary 4.17
.

Let
\mathscr{I}
be an
F_{\sigma}
ideal. Then it is consistent that
\mathfrak{b}=\non(M_{\mathscr{I}})<\non(\mathcal{M})
.

4.2.1.
Relations with constant evasion and prediction numbers

We relate
\non(M_{\mathscr{I}})
(resp.
\cov(M_{\mathscr{I}})
) for
\mathscr{I}=\mathcal{ED},\mathcal{ED}_{\mathrm{fin}},\mathrm{Fin}\otimes\mathrm{Fin}
to the constant evasion (resp. prediction) numbers, which stems from Kamo’s work (
[
Kam00
,
Kam01
]
). As one of the consequences, we obtain the affirmative answer for
[
CM25
, Question 4.19]
, where the first and third authors asked whether
\cov(M_{\mathrm{Fin}\otimes\mathrm{Fin}})<\mathfrak{d}
is consistent. We start with some definitions, following the notation introduced in the recent work by Cardona and Repický
[
CR25
]
.

Definition 4.18
.

•

A
predictor
is a function
\pi\colon\omega^{<\omega}\to\omega
. Let
\Pred
denote the set of all predictors.

•

Let
f\in{\omega}^{\omega}
and
\pi\in\Pred
. For
k\geq 2
, we say
\pi

k
-constantly predicts
f
, denoted by
f\sqsubset^{\mathrm{pc},k}\pi
, if:

\forall^{\infty}i<\omega~\exists j\in\left[i,i+k\right)~f(j)=\pi(f\mathpunct{\upharpoonright}j).

We say
\pi
constantly predicts
f
, denoted by
f\sqsubset^{\mathrm{pc}}\pi
, if
\pi

k
-constantly predicts
f
for some
k\geq 2
.

•

Define the following cardinal invariants:

\displaystyle\mathfrak{e}^{\mathrm{const}}(k)

\displaystyle\coloneqq\min\{|F|:F\subseteq{\omega}^{\omega},\forall\pi\in\Pred~\exists f\in F~\lnot(f\sqsubset^{\mathrm{pc},k}\pi)\},

\displaystyle\mathfrak{e}^{\mathrm{const}}

\displaystyle\coloneqq\min\{|F|:F\subseteq{\omega}^{\omega},\forall\pi\in\Pred~\exists f\in F~\lnot(f\sqsubset^{\mathrm{pc}}\pi)\},

\displaystyle\mathfrak{v}^{\mathrm{const}}(k)

\displaystyle\coloneqq\min\{|\Pi|:\Pi\subseteq\Pred,\forall f\in{\omega}^{\omega}~\exists\pi\in\Pi~f\sqsubset^{\mathrm{pc},k}\pi\},

\displaystyle\mathfrak{v}^{\mathrm{const}}

\displaystyle\coloneqq\min\{|\Pi|:\Pi\subseteq\Pred,\forall f\in{\omega}^{\omega}~\exists\pi\in\Pi~f\sqsubset^{\mathrm{pc}}\pi\}.

•

For
b\in(\omega\setminus 2)^{\omega}
, we define constant evasion/prediction on the restricted space
\prod b\coloneqq\prod_{n<\omega}b(n)
as follows:
\Pred_{b}
denotes the set of all functions
\pi
with
\dom(\pi)=\bigcup_{n<\omega}\prod_{i<n}b(i)
and
\pi(\sigma)\in b(|\sigma|)
for each
\sigma\in\dom(\pi)
.

\displaystyle\mathfrak{e}^{\mathrm{const}}_{b}(k)

\displaystyle\coloneqq\min\{|F|:F\subseteq\textstyle\prod b,\forall\pi\in\Pred_{b}~\exists f\in F~\lnot(f\sqsubset^{\mathrm{pc},k}\pi)\},

\displaystyle\mathfrak{e}^{\mathrm{const}}_{b}

\displaystyle\coloneqq\min\{|F|:F\subseteq\textstyle\prod b,\forall\pi\in\Pred_{b}~\exists f\in F~\lnot(f\sqsubset^{\mathrm{pc}}\pi)\},

\displaystyle\mathfrak{v}^{\mathrm{const}}_{b}(k)

\displaystyle\coloneqq\min\{|\Pi|:\Pi\subseteq\Pred_{b},\forall f\in\textstyle\prod b~\exists\pi\in\Pi~f\sqsubset^{\mathrm{pc},k}\pi\},

\displaystyle\mathfrak{v}^{\mathrm{const}}_{b}

\displaystyle\coloneqq\min\{|\Pi|:\Pi\subseteq\Pred_{b},\forall f\in\textstyle\prod b~\exists\pi\in\Pi~f\sqsubset^{\mathrm{pc}}\pi\}.

When
b
is the constant function with value
n<\omega
, namely, when
b=\omega\times\{n\}
for some
n<\omega
, we use
n
as subscripts instead of the function
b
itself.

One easily sees:

Fact 4.19
.

(1)

If
2\leq k\leq l
, then

\mathfrak{e}^{\mathrm{const}}(k)\leq\mathfrak{e}^{\mathrm{const}}(l)\leq\mathfrak{e}^{\mathrm{const}}\text{ and }\mathfrak{v}^{\mathrm{const}}\leq\mathfrak{v}^{\mathrm{const}}(l)\leq\mathfrak{v}^{\mathrm{const}}(k).

The same inequalities hold for constant evasions/predictions on
\prod b
for any
b\in(\omega\setminus 2)^{\omega}
.

(2)

If
b\leq^{*}b^{\prime}
, then

\displaystyle\mathfrak{e}^{\mathrm{const}}\leq\mathfrak{e}^{\mathrm{const}}_{b^{\prime}}\leq\mathfrak{e}^{\mathrm{const}}_{b}\leq\mathfrak{e}^{\mathrm{const}}_{2},

\displaystyle\mathfrak{v}^{\mathrm{const}}_{2}\leq\mathfrak{v}^{\mathrm{const}}_{b}\leq\mathfrak{v}^{\mathrm{const}}_{b^{\prime}}\leq\mathfrak{v}^{\mathrm{const}}.

Also, for
k\geq 2
,

\displaystyle\mathfrak{e}^{\mathrm{const}}(k)\leq\mathfrak{e}^{\mathrm{const}}_{b^{\prime}}(k)\leq\mathfrak{e}^{\mathrm{const}}_{b}(k)\leq\mathfrak{e}^{\mathrm{const}}_{2}(k),

\displaystyle\mathfrak{v}^{\mathrm{const}}_{2}(k)\leq\mathfrak{v}^{\mathrm{const}}_{b}(k)\leq\mathfrak{v}^{\mathrm{const}}_{b^{\prime}}(k)\leq\mathfrak{v}^{\mathrm{const}}(k).

Proposition 4.20
.

\mathfrak{e}^{\mathrm{const}}(2)\leq\non(M_{\mathcal{ED}})
and
\mathfrak{v}^{\mathrm{const}}(2)\geq\cov(M_{\mathcal{ED}})
.

Proof.

To show
\mathfrak{e}^{\mathrm{const}}(2)\leq\non(M_{\mathcal{ED}})
, let
F\subseteq(\omega\times\omega)^{\omega}
of size
<\mathfrak{e}^{\mathrm{const}}(2)
. For
(f,g)\in F
,
f*g\in{\omega}^{\omega}
is given by
f*g(2n)\coloneqq f(n)
and
f*g(2n+1)\coloneqq g(n)
. Put
F^{*}\coloneqq\{f*g:(f,g)\in F\}\subseteq{\omega}^{\omega}
. Since
F^{*}
has size
<\mathfrak{e}^{\mathrm{const}}(2)
, there is a predictor
\pi\colon\omega^{<\omega}\to\omega
such that
f*g\sqsubset^{\mathrm{pc},k}\pi
for all
(f,g)\in F
. In particular,

\forall(f,g)\in F~\forall^{\infty}n<\omega\text{ either }\begin{cases}f(n)=\pi((f*g)\mathpunct{\upharpoonright}2n),&\text{or}\\
g(n)=\pi((f*g)\mathpunct{\upharpoonright}2n+1).&\end{cases}

Define
\sigma\colon(\omega\times\omega)^{<\omega}\to\omega
by
\sigma(u,v)\coloneqq\pi(u*v)
. For
(u,v)\in(\omega\times\omega)^{<\omega}
, define
s(u,v)\colon\omega\to\omega
by
s(u,v)(a)\coloneqq\pi((u*v)^{\frown}a)
. Again for
(u,v)\in(\omega\times\omega)^{<\omega}
, let
\phi(u,v)\coloneqq\{(a,b)\in\omega\times\omega:a\leq\sigma(u,v)\text{ or }b=s(u,v)(a)\}
. Note
\phi(u,v)\in\mathcal{ED}
. Then it is routine to check
(f,g)\in M_{\phi}
for all
(f,g)\in F
. The latter inequality is proved in the dual manner.
∎

By the essentially same proof as above, we have:

Proposition 4.21
.

For any increasing
b\in\omega^{\omega}
,
\mathfrak{e}^{\mathrm{const}}_{b}(2)\leq\non(M_{\mathcal{ED}_{\mathrm{fin}}})
and
\mathfrak{v}^{\mathrm{const}}_{b}(2)\geq\cov(M_{\mathcal{ED}_{\mathrm{fin}}})
.

Moreover, we can even get characterizations of
\non(M_{\mathrm{Fin}\otimes\mathrm{Fin}})
and
\cov(M_{\mathrm{Fin}\otimes\mathrm{Fin}})
using the following variants of constant evasion and prediction:

Definition 4.22
.

Let
f\in\omega^{\omega}
and
\pi\in\Pred
. For each
k\geq 2
, we say
\pi

k
-constantly bounding predicts
f
, denoted by
f\sqsubset^{\mathrm{pc},k}_{\leq}\pi
, if

\forall^{\infty}i<\omega\,\exists j\in[i,i+k)\,(f(j)\leq\pi(f\upharpoonright j)).

Define:

\displaystyle\mathfrak{e}^{\mathrm{const}}_{\leq}(k)

\displaystyle\coloneqq\min\{|F|:F\subseteq{\omega}^{\omega},\forall\pi\in\Pred~\exists f\in F~\lnot(f\sqsubset^{\mathrm{pc},k}_{\leq}\pi)\},

\displaystyle\mathfrak{v}^{\mathrm{const}}_{\leq}(k)

\displaystyle\coloneqq\min\{|\Pi|:\Pi\subseteq\Pred,\forall f\in{\omega}^{\omega}~\exists\pi\in\Pi~f\sqsubset^{\mathrm{pc},k}_{\leq}\pi\}.

Proposition 4.23
.

\non(M_{\mathrm{Fin}\otimes\mathrm{Fin}})=\mathfrak{e}^{\mathrm{const}}_{\leq}(2)
and
\cov(M_{\mathrm{Fin}\otimes\mathrm{Fin}})=\mathfrak{v}^{\mathrm{const}}_{\leq}(2)
.

To see this, it is convenient to introduce a seemingly weaker version of constant bounding prediction:

Definition 4.24
.

Let
f\in{\omega}^{\omega}
and
\pi\in\Pred
. For each
k\geq 2
, we say
\pi

weakly
k
-constantly bounding predicts
f
, denoted by
f\sqsubset^{\mathrm{wpc},k}_{\leq}\pi
, if

\forall^{\infty}m<\omega\,\exists j\in[km,km+k)\,(f(j)\leq\pi(f\upharpoonright j)).

Define:

\displaystyle\mathfrak{e}^{\mathrm{wconst}}_{\leq}(k)

\displaystyle\coloneqq\min\{|F|:F\subseteq{\omega}^{\omega},\forall\pi\in\Pred~\exists f\in F~\lnot(f\sqsubset^{\mathrm{wpc},k}_{\leq}\pi)\},

\displaystyle\mathfrak{v}^{\mathrm{wconst}}_{\leq}(k)

\displaystyle\coloneqq\min\{|\Pi|:\Pi\subseteq\Pred,\forall f\in{\omega}^{\omega}~\exists\pi\in\Pi~f\sqsubset^{\mathrm{wpc},k}_{\leq}\pi\}.

Lemma 4.25
.

\mathfrak{e}^{\mathrm{wconst}}_{\leq}(k)=\mathfrak{e}^{\mathrm{const}}_{\leq}(k)
and
\mathfrak{v}^{\mathrm{wconst}}_{\leq}(k)=\mathfrak{v}^{\mathrm{const}}_{\leq}(k)
.

Proof.

\mathfrak{e}^{\mathrm{wconst}}_{\leq}(k)\geq\mathfrak{e}^{\mathrm{const}}_{\leq}(k)
is clear. To prove the converse inequality, let
F\subseteq{\omega}^{\omega}
of size
<\mathfrak{e}^{\mathrm{wconst}}_{\leq}(k)
. For
f\in{\omega}^{\omega}
and
l<k
, define the
l
-shift
f^{l}\in{\omega}^{\omega}
of
f
by:

f^{l}(n)=\begin{cases}0&\text{if }n<l\\
f(n-l)&\text{if }n\geq l\end{cases}

For
t\in\omega^{<\omega}
, define
t^{l}
analogously (so
\dom(t^{l})=l+|t|
). Let
F^{\prime}\coloneqq\{f^{l}:f\in F,l<k\}
. Since
F^{\prime}
has size
<\mathfrak{e}^{\mathrm{wconst}}_{\leq}(k)
, there is
\pi^{\prime}\colon\omega^{<\omega}\to\omega
such that
f^{l}\sqsubset^{\mathrm{wpc},k}_{\leq}\pi^{\prime}
for all
f\in F
and
l<k
. Define
\pi\colon\omega^{<\omega}\to\omega
by
\pi(t)=\max\{\pi^{\prime}(t^{l}):l<k\}
.
Let
f\in F
be arbitrary.
Since
f^{l}\sqsubset^{\mathrm{wpc},k}_{\leq}\pi^{\prime}
for all
l<k
, there is
m_{0}>0
such that:

\forall l<k~\forall m\geq m_{0}\,\exists j\in[km,km+k)\,(f^{l}(j)\leq\pi^{\prime}(f^{l}\upharpoonright j)).

To see
f\sqsubset^{\mathrm{pc},k}_{\leq}\pi
, let
i\geq km_{0}+k
be arbitrary. Let
m\geq m_{0}
and
l<k
be such that
i=km-l
. Take
j\in[km,km+k)
be such that
f^{l}(j)\leq\pi^{\prime}(f^{l}\mathpunct{\upharpoonright}j)
.
Thus
f(j-l)=f^{l}(j)\leq\pi^{\prime}(f^{l}\mathpunct{\upharpoonright}j)=\pi^{\prime}((f\mathpunct{\upharpoonright}(j-l))^{l})\leq\pi(f\mathpunct{\upharpoonright}(j-l))
.
Since
j-l\in[km-l,km+k-l)=[i,i+k)
and
i\geq km_{0}+k
was arbitrary, we have
f\sqsubset^{\mathrm{pc},k}_{\leq}\pi
.
∎

Proof of
4.23
.

By
4.25
, we show
\non(M_{\mathrm{Fin}\otimes\mathrm{Fin}})=\mathfrak{e}^{\mathrm{wconst}}_{\leq}(2)
instead.

(
\geq
) Let
F\subseteq(\omega\times\omega)^{\omega}
be of size
<\mathfrak{e}^{\mathrm{wconst}}_{\leq}(2)
. For
(f,g)\in(\omega\times\omega)^{\omega}
,
f*g\in\omega^{\omega}
is given by
f*g(2n)=f(n)
and
f*g(2n+1)=g(n)
.
For
(u,v)\in(\omega\times\omega)^{<\omega}
, define
u*v\in\omega^{<\omega}
analogously.
Put
F^{*}=\{f*g:(f,g)\in F\}\subseteq\omega^{\omega}
. Since
F^{*}
has size
<\mathfrak{e}^{\mathrm{wconst}}_{\leq}(2)
, there is a predictor
\pi\colon\omega^{<\omega}\to\omega
such that
f*g\sqsubset^{\mathrm{wpc},2}_{\leq}\pi
for all
(f,g)\in F
. Thus, we have

\forall(f,g)\in F~\forall^{\infty}n<\omega\text{ either }\begin{cases}f(n)\leq\pi((f*g)\upharpoonright 2n),&\text{or}\\
g(n)\leq\pi((f*g)\upharpoonright 2n+1).&\end{cases}

Define
\sigma\colon(\omega\times\omega)^{<\omega}\to\omega
by
\sigma(u,v)=\pi(u*v)
. For
(u,v)\in(\omega\times\omega)^{<\omega}
, define
s(u,v)\colon\omega\to\omega
by
s(u,v)(a)=\pi((u*v)^{\frown}a)
. Again for
(u,v)\in(\omega\times\omega)^{<\omega}
, let
\phi(u,v)=\{(a,b)\in\omega\times\omega:a\leq\sigma(u,v)\text{ or }b\leq s(u,v)(a)\}
. Note
\phi(u,v)\in\mathrm{Fin}\otimes\mathrm{Fin}
. Then it is routine to check
F\subseteq M_{\phi}
.

(
\leq
) Let
F\subseteq\omega^{\omega}
be of size
<\non(M_{\mathrm{Fin}\otimes\mathrm{Fin}})
. For each
f\in F
, define
f_{\mathrm{even}},f_{\mathrm{odd}}\in\omega^{\omega}
by
f_{\mathrm{even}}(n)=f(2n)
and
f_{\mathrm{odd}}(n)=f(2n+1)
. Put
F_{*}=\{\langle f_{\mathrm{even}},f_{\mathrm{odd}}\rangle:f\in F\}
. Since
F_{*}
has size
<\non(M_{\mathrm{Fin}\otimes\mathrm{Fin}})
, there is
\phi\colon\omega^{<\omega}\to\mathrm{Fin}\otimes\mathrm{Fin}
such that
F_{*}\subseteq M_{\phi}
. We may assume that there are
\sigma\colon(\omega\times\omega)^{<\omega}\to\omega
and
s\colon(\omega\times\omega)^{<\omega}\to\omega^{\omega}
such that
\phi(u,v)=\{(a,b):a\leq\sigma(u,v)\lor v\leq s(u,v)(a)\}
. Then for every
f\in F
,

\forall^{\infty}n<\omega\text{ either }\begin{cases}f_{\mathrm{even}}(n)\leq\sigma(f_{\mathrm{even}}\upharpoonright n,f_{\mathrm{odd}}\upharpoonright n),&\text{or}\\
f_{\mathrm{odd}}(n)\leq s(f_{\mathrm{even}}\upharpoonright n,f_{\mathrm{odd}}\upharpoonright n)(f_{\mathrm{even}}(n)).&\end{cases}

Now define
\pi\colon\omega^{<\omega}\to\omega
by

\displaystyle\pi(t)

\displaystyle=\sigma(t_{\mathrm{even}},t_{\mathrm{odd}})

\displaystyle\pi(t^{\frown}\langle n\rangle)

\displaystyle=s(t_{\mathrm{even}},t_{\mathrm{odd}})(n)

for all
t\in\omega^{<\omega}
of even length and all
n<\omega
, where
t_{\mathrm{even}},t_{\mathrm{even}}
are defined analogously. Then for all
f\in F
, we have
f\sqsubset^{\mathrm{wpc},2}_{\leq}\pi
.
\mathfrak{v}^{\mathrm{const}}_{b}(2)\geq\cov(M_{\mathcal{ED}_{\mathrm{fin}}})
is proved in the dual manner.
∎

The above proof can be easily generalized to higher dimensions. Let
\Fin^{\otimes 2}=\mathrm{Fin}\otimes\mathrm{Fin}
. For each
2\leq k<\omega
, we define
\Fin^{\otimes k+1}=\Fin\otimes\Fin^{\otimes k}
by induction.

Proposition 4.26
.

For each
2\leq k<\omega
,
\non(M_{\mathrm{Fin}^{\otimes k}})=\mathfrak{e}^{\mathrm{const}}_{\leq}(k)
and
\cov(M_{\mathrm{Fin}^{\otimes k}})=\mathfrak{v}^{\mathrm{const}}_{\leq}(k)
.

Brendle
[
Bre03
, Theorem 3.6(b)]
showed the consistency of
\mathfrak{e}^{\mathrm{const}}>\mathfrak{b}
and
\mathfrak{v}^{\mathrm{const}}<\mathfrak{d}
(with large continuum), but essentially proved more:

Theorem 4.27

(Brendle
[
Bre03
]
)
.

(1)

Given
\kappa<\lambda=\lambda^{<\lambda}
regular uncountable, there is a poset forcing
\mathfrak{e}^{\mathrm{const}}(2)=\lambda=\mathfrak{c}
and
\mathfrak{b}=\kappa
.

(2)

Given
\kappa
regular uncountable and
\lambda=\lambda^{<\omega}>\kappa
, there is a poset forcing
\mathfrak{v}^{\mathrm{const}}(2)=\kappa
and
\mathfrak{d}=\lambda=\mathfrak{c}
.

Thus, we particularly have:

Corollary 4.28
.

Both
\mathfrak{b}<\non(M_{\mathcal{ED}})
and
\cov(M_{\mathcal{ED}})<\mathfrak{d}
are consistent.

Together with the following result, we show that the uniformity and covering numbers of
K_{\mathscr{I}}
and
M_{\mathscr{I}}
are consistently different for
\mathscr{I}=\mathrm{Fin}^{\otimes k},\mathcal{ED}
.

Proposition 4.29
.

For each
2\leq k<\omega
,
\non(K_{\mathrm{Fin}^{\otimes k}})=\mathfrak{b}
and
\cov(M_{\mathrm{Fin}^{\otimes k}})=\mathfrak{d}
.

Proof.

We only prove the former equation for
k=2
, because our proof can be easily generalized to higher dimensions and the latter equation can be shown in a dual manner.

It suffices to show
\non(K_{\mathrm{Fin}\otimes\mathrm{Fin}})\leq\mathfrak{b}
. Let
B\subseteq{\omega}^{\omega}
be an unbounded family and assume all
f\in B
are strictly increasing. We shall show
F\coloneqq\{f\times g:f,g\in B\}\subseteq\left(\omega\times\omega\right)^{\omega}
is not in
K_{\mathrm{Fin}\otimes\mathrm{Fin}}
. Let
\phi\colon\omega\to\mathrm{Fin}\otimes\mathrm{Fin}
be arbitrary. For each
n<\omega
, there are
k_{n}<\omega
and
h_{n}\in{\omega}^{\omega}
such that
\phi(n)\subseteq(k_{n}\times\omega)\cup\{(i,j):i<\omega,j<h_{n}(i)\}
. Since
B
is unbounded, there are
f\in B
and
D\in[{\omega}]^{\omega}
such that
k_{n}<f(n)
for
n\in D
. For
n<\omega
, put
h(n)\coloneqq h_{n}(f(n))
and
h^{\prime}(n)\coloneqq h(d_{n})
, where
d_{n}
denotes the
n
-th element of
D
. Since
B
is unbounded, there are
g\in B
and
E\in[{\omega}]^{\omega}
such that
h^{\prime}(n)<g(n)
for
n\in E
. Then, for any
n\in E
,
g(d_{n})\geq g(n)>h^{\prime}(n)=h(d_{n})=h_{d_{n}}(f(d_{n}))
. Thus, for all
m\in\{d_{n}:n\in E\}
, we have
(f(m),g(m))\notin\phi(m)
, so
F
is not in
K_{\mathrm{Fin}\otimes\mathrm{Fin}}
.
∎

By
4.11
and
4.13
, we have:

Corollary 4.30
.

For every ideal
\mathscr{I}
on a countable set such that
\mathscr{I}\leq_{\mathrm{K}}\mathrm{Fin}^{\otimes k}
for some
2\leq k<\omega
,
\non(K_{\mathscr{I}})=\mathfrak{b}
and
\cov(K_{\mathscr{I}})=\mathfrak{d}
. In particular, these equalities hold for
\mathscr{I}=\mathcal{R},\mathrm{conv},\mathcal{ED}
.

By
4.27
, we get the following consistency.

Corollary 4.31
.

For every ideal
\mathscr{I}
on a countable set such that
\mathscr{I}\leq_{\mathrm{K}}\mathrm{Fin}^{\otimes k}
for some
2\leq k<\omega
,
\non(K_{\mathscr{I}})<\non(M_{\mathscr{I}})
and
\cov(K_{\mathscr{I}})>\cov(M_{\mathscr{I}})
are both consistent.

We do not know other examples of ideals
\mathscr{I}
such that
\non(K_{\mathscr{I}})
and
\non(M_{\mathscr{I}})
are consistently different. In particular, the consistency of
\non(K_{\mathcal{ED}_{\mathrm{fin}}})<\non(M_{\mathcal{ED}_{\mathrm{fin}}})
is not known (Instead, we will see the consistency of
\non(K_{\mathcal{ED}_{\mathrm{fin}}})>\mathfrak{b}
in
Section

5.2.2
).

4.2.2.
Nowhere dense ideal and Solecki ideal

By reformulating Sabok–Zapletal’s results on forcing properties of
\mathbb{M}_{\mathrm{nwd}}
and
\mathbb{M}_{\mathcal{S}}
in
[
SZ11
]
, we compute the uniformity and covering numbers of
M_{\mathrm{nwd}}
and
M_{\mathcal{S}}
. Recall
2.10
.

Definition 4.32
.

Let
\mathsf{concat}\colon(2^{<\omega})^{\omega}\rightarrow 2^{\omega}
be the concatenating function defined by
\mathsf{concat}(x)=x(0)^{\frown}x(1)^{\frown}x(2)^{\frown}\cdots\in 2^{\omega}
. We also define
\mathsf{concat}\colon(2^{<\omega})^{<\omega}\to 2^{<\omega}
in the analogous way and confuse notations.

Lemma 4.33

(Sabok–Zapletal,
[
SZ11
]
)
.

Let
\Vdash_{\mathbb{M}_{\mathrm{nwd}}}\dot{r}\coloneqq\bigcup\{\stem(T):T\in\dot{G}\}\in(2^{<\omega})^{\omega}
be (the canonical
\mathbb{M}_{\mathrm{nwd}}
-name of) an
\mathbb{M}_{\mathrm{nwd}}
-generic real. Then

\Vdash_{\mathbb{M}_{\mathrm{nwd}}}\mathsf{concat}(\dot{r})\in 2^{\omega}\text{ is Cohen generic.}

Proof.

Let
N\subseteq 2^{\omega}
be closed nowhere dense and let
S\subseteq 2^{<\omega}
be a tree such that
[S]=N
.
Let
T\in\mathbb{M}_{\mathrm{nwd}}
. We can now refine it to a tree
T^{\prime}\in\mathbb{M}_{\mathrm{nwd}}
such that, for all
\sigma\in T^{\prime}
,
\mathrm{succ}_{T^{\prime}}(\sigma)
is either a singleton or a
\mathrm{nwd}
-positive set disjoint from
S\cap\{\tau\in 2^{<\omega}\colon\mathsf{concat}(\sigma)\subseteq\tau\}
.

We claim that if
x\in[T^{\prime}]
then
\mathsf{concat}(x)\notin N
; Let
\sigma
be the stem of
T^{\prime}
. Then
\mathsf{concat}(\sigma)^{\frown}\langle x(\lvert\sigma\rvert)\rangle\notin S
. This implies that, for all
n>\lvert\sigma\rvert
,
\mathsf{concat}(x\restriction n)\notin S
, and therefore
\mathsf{concat}(x)\notin[S]=N
.
∎

By
4.11
and
2.10
, we have:

Corollary 4.34
.

\non(\mathcal{M})=\non(M_{\mathrm{nwd}})
and
\cov(\mathcal{M})=\cov(M_{\mathrm{nwd}})
.

In
[
SZ11
]
, it was shown that
\mathbb{M}_{\mathcal{S}}
collapses Lebesgue outer measure.
4.39
is a reformulation of this result. First, we recall that
\mathcal{S}_{\varepsilon}
is generated by the sets of the form

I_{y}=\{C\in\Omega_{\varepsilon}\colon y\in C\},

where
\Omega_{\varepsilon}
is the collection of clopen subsets of
2^{\omega}
of measure
\varepsilon
. Note that for every
A\subseteq\Omega_{\varepsilon}
,

A\in\mathcal{S}_{\varepsilon}^{+}\iff\forall y\in 2^{\omega}~\exists C\in A~(y\notin C).

The standard Solecki ideal
\mathcal{S}
is
\mathcal{S}_{1/2}
. Note that
2^{\omega}
with the standard Haar measure
\lambda
is isomorphic to
(2^{\omega})^{k}
with the product measure
\lambda_{k}
, so we may consider the clopen sets on
(2^{\omega})^{k}
instead so the respective Solecki ideal’s are going to be Katětov equivalent. For each
k\in\omega
, we define
h_{k}\colon\Omega\to\Omega_{\varepsilon}
by

h_{k}(C)=(2^{\omega})^{k}\setminus\prod_{i<k}(2^{\omega}\setminus C)

for any
C\subseteq 2^{\omega}
.

Lemma 4.35
.

h_{k}
is a Katětov reduction witnessing that
\mathcal{S}\geq_{K}\mathcal{S}_{1-2^{-k}}
.

Proof.

Let
y\coloneqq\langle y_{i}\rangle_{i<k}\in(2^{\omega})^{k}
and let
C\in\Omega_{\varepsilon}
be such that
y\in h_{k}(C)
. Then there must be an
i<k
such that
y_{i}\notin 2^{\omega}\setminus C
, so
y_{i}\in C
. In other words,
h_{k}^{-1}[I_{y}]\subseteq\bigcup_{i<k}I_{y_{i}}\in\mathcal{S}
.
∎

From now on we consider that
h_{k}
is a function between the clopen sets of
2^{\omega}
of the respective measures (instead of the clopens on
{2^{\omega}}^{k}
). Given
x\in\Omega^{\omega}
, define

B_{x}=\{y\in 2^{\omega}:\exists^{\infty}n<\omega~(y\notin h_{n}(x(n)))\}.

Note that
B_{x}
has measure zero, since each
h_{n}(x(n))
has measure
1-2^{-n}
.

Lemma 4.36
.

Let
y\in 2^{\omega}
and let
T\in\mathbb{M}_{\mathcal{S}}
, then there is a tree
T^{\prime}\in\mathbb{M}_{\mathcal{S}}
such that
T^{\prime}\leq T
, and for all
x\in[T^{\prime}]
,
y\in B_{x}
.

Proof.

The argument is similar to the proof of
4.33
. We can refine
T
to a tree
T^{\prime}\in\mathbb{M}_{\mathcal{S}}
such that, for all
\sigma\in T^{\prime}
,
\mathrm{succ}_{T^{\prime}}(\sigma)
is either a singleton or a
\mathcal{S}
-positive set disjoint from
h_{\lvert\sigma\rvert}^{-1}[I_{y}]
. To show that
T^{\prime}
has the desired property, let
x\in[T^{\prime}]
. For any
n<\omega
such that
\mathrm{succ}_{T^{\prime}}(x\upharpoonright n)\in\mathcal{S}^{+}
, we have
x(n)\notin h_{\lvert\sigma\rvert}^{-1}[I_{y}]
and thus
y\notin h_{n}(x(n))
. There are infinitely many such
n<\omega
, so
y\in B_{x}
.
∎

Combined with a standard fusion argument, we can strengthen this lemma as follows.

Lemma 4.37
.

Let
\{y_{n}:n\in\omega\}\subseteq 2^{\omega}
and let
T\in\mathbb{M}_{\mathcal{S}}
, then there is a tree
T^{\prime}\in\mathbb{M}_{\mathcal{S}}
,
T^{\prime}\leq T
such that, for all
x\in[T^{\prime}]
and all
n\in\omega
,
y_{n}\in B_{x}
.

Proof.

For a tree
T
, we write

\mathrm{lv}_{n}(T)=\{t\in T:t\text{ passes through at most }n\text{ splitting nodes}\}.

We also write
T^{\prime}\leq_{n}T
if
T^{\prime}\leq T
and
\mathrm{lv}_{n}(T)=\mathrm{lv}_{n}(T^{\prime})
.

Claim 4.38
.

Let
T\in\mathbb{M}_{\mathcal{S}}
and
n\in\omega
, then there is a tree
T^{\prime}\in\mathbb{M}_{\mathcal{S}}
such that
T^{\prime}\leq_{n}T
, and for all
x\in[T^{\prime}]
,
y_{n}\in B_{x}
.

Proof of the claim.

For each finial node
s
of
\mathrm{lv}_{n}(T)
, we apply
4.36
to
T_{s}
to obtain a tree
T^{\prime}_{s}\in\mathbb{M}_{\mathcal{S}}
such that
T^{\prime}_{s}\leq T_{s}
and for all
x\in[T_{s}^{\prime}]
,
y_{n}\in B_{x}
. The tree
T^{\prime}=\bigcup\{T^{\prime}_{s}:s\text{ is a final node of lv}_{n}(T)\}
is the tree we are looking for.
∎

We apply repeatedly the claim to get a sequence of trees
T=T_{0}\geq_{0}T_{1}\geq_{1}T_{2}\geq_{2}\cdots
such that, for all
n<\omega
, if
x\in[T_{n}]
then
y_{n}\in B_{x}
. Then the tree
T^{\prime}\coloneqq\bigcap_{n\in\omega}T_{n}
satisfies the requirements.
∎

Corollary 4.39
.

\cov_{\omega}(\mathcal{N})\leq\non(M_{\mathcal{S}})
and
\cov(M_{\mathcal{S}})\leq\non(\mathcal{N})
.

Proof.

If
X\notin M_{\mathcal{S}}
, then there is a tree
T\in\mathbb{M}_{\mathcal{S}}
such that every
T^{\prime}\in\mathbb{M}_{\mathcal{S}}
with
T^{\prime}\leq T
has the property that
[T^{\prime}]\cap X\neq\emptyset
. Then,
4.37
implies that
\{B_{x}:x\in X\}
witnesses
\operatorname{cov}_{\omega}(\mathcal{N})\leq|X|
.
The other inequality follows dually: Let
\kappa<\cov(M_{\mathcal{S}})
and
\{y_{\alpha}:\alpha\in\kappa\}\in 2^{\omega}
. Define
Y_{\alpha}\coloneqq\{x\in\Omega^{\omega}:y_{\alpha}\notin B_{x}\}
. By the previous lemma
Y_{\alpha}\in M_{\mathcal{S}}
and, since
\kappa<\cov(M_{\mathcal{S}})
, there must be
x\notin\bigcup_{\alpha<\kappa}Y_{\alpha}
. It follows that
y_{\alpha}\in B_{x}
for each
\alpha<\kappa
.
∎

By
3.32
and
4.14
, we get the exact values of
\non(M_{\mathcal{S}})
and
\cov(M_{\mathcal{S}})
:

Theorem 4.40
.

\non(M_{\mathcal{S}})=\max\{\mathfrak{b},\cov_{\omega}(\mathcal{N})\}
and
\cov(M_{\mathcal{S}})=\min\{\mathfrak{d},\non(\mathcal{N})\}
.

Remark 4.41
.

This implies that the inequalities of
4.14
are sharp: Indeed, when
\mathscr{I}=\mathcal{S}
,
\non(M_{\mathcal{S}})=\max\{\mathfrak{b},\non^{*}_{\omega}(\mathcal{S})\}
by
3.32
.

Remark 4.42
.

\mathfrak{b}<\cov(\mathcal{N})\leq\operatorname{cov}_{\omega}(\mathcal{N})
holds in the random model, and it is not hard to see that
\cov_{\omega}(\mathcal{N})<\mathfrak{b}
holds in the Laver model (see e.g.
[
BJ95
, Lemma 7.2.3]
). Thus
\mathfrak{b}
and
\operatorname{cov}_{\omega}(\mathcal{N})
have independent values. As in the case of Laver model, one can see that in the
\mathbb{PT}_{f,g}
-model,
\mathfrak{b}=\cov_{\omega}(\mathcal{N})<\non(\mathcal{M})
holds (see e.g.
[
BJ95
, Lemma 7.2.15]
). Therefore,
\non(M_{\mathcal{S}})=\max\{\mathfrak{b},\cov_{\omega}(\mathcal{N})\}
is consistently different from
\mathfrak{b}
and
\non(\mathcal{M})
.

4.40
can be improved for
K_{\mathcal{S}}
and we will prove this using
2.10
. Let us introduce the forcing notion
\mathbb{K}_{\mathscr{I}}
that corresponds to the
\sigma
-ideal
K_{\mathscr{I}}
:

Definition 4.43
.

Let
\mathbb{K}_{\mathscr{I}}
denote the collection of all
T\subseteq\omega^{\omega}
such that for every
\sigma\in T
there is
N>|\sigma|
such that the spectrum

\Spec_{T}(\tau,N)\coloneqq\{\tau(N):\tau\in T,\sigma\subseteq\tau,|\tau|=N+1\}

is
\mathscr{I}
-positive.

The following dichotomy holds:

Lemma 4.44
.

For any analytic set
A\subseteq\omega^{\omega}
either
A\in K_{\mathscr{I}}
or there is
T\in\mathbb{K}_{\mathscr{I}}
such that
[T]\subseteq A
. Therefore,
\mathbb{K}_{\mathscr{I}}
is forcing equivalent to
\mathbb{P}_{K_{\mathscr{I}}}
.

Proof.

We will show this for closed sets only. The standard unfolding trick gives the proof for analytic sets. Let
D\subseteq\omega^{\omega}
be a closed set and let
T\subseteq\omega^{<\omega}
be such a tree that
D=[T]
. We define a derivative of
T
as follows:

T^{\prime}=\{\sigma\in T:\exists N>|\sigma|
such that
\{\tau(N):\tau\in T,\sigma\subseteq\tau,|\tau|=N+1\}\in\mathscr{I}^{+}\}

Define
T_{0}=T
,
T_{\alpha+1}=T_{\alpha}^{\prime}
and for limit
\alpha<\omega_{1}
let
T_{\alpha}=\bigcap_{\gamma<\alpha}T_{\gamma}
. Let
\alpha^{*}<\omega_{1}
be the smallest ordinal such that
T_{\alpha^{*}+1}=T_{\alpha^{*}}
. Consider two cases:

First,
T_{\alpha^{*}}\neq\emptyset
. Then by the definition of derivative, it is easy to construct required tree.

Second,
T_{\alpha^{*}}=\emptyset
. Then
[T]=\bigcup_{\alpha<\alpha^{*}}([T_{\alpha}]\setminus[T_{\alpha+1}])
. By the definition of the derivative we have that
[T_{\alpha}]\setminus[T_{\alpha+1}]\in K_{\mathscr{I}}
. It follows that
[T]
is covered by countable union of
K_{\mathscr{I}}
-sets.
∎

Definition 4.45
.

For
y\in 2^{\omega}
, let
N_{y}\coloneqq\{x\in\Omega^{\omega}:y\notin B_{x}\}
.
Let
N_{\mathcal{S}}
be the
\sigma
-ideal on
\Omega^{\omega}
generated by
N_{y}
’s.

Lemma 4.46
.

\operatorname{cov}_{\omega}(\mathcal{N})\leq\non(N_{\mathcal{S}})
and
\cov(N_{\mathcal{S}})\leq\non(\mathcal{N})
.

Proof.

To see
\operatorname{cov}_{\omega}(\mathcal{N})\leq\non(N_{\mathcal{S}})
, let
X\subseteq\Omega^{\omega}
with
X\notin N_{\mathcal{S}}
. We claim that
\{B_{x}:x\in X\}\subseteq\mathcal{N}
is a witness for
\operatorname{cov}_{\omega}(\mathcal{N})
. To show this, let
Y\in[2^{\omega}]^{\omega}
. Since
N\coloneqq\bigcup_{y\in Y}N_{y}\in N_{\mathcal{S}}
and
X\notin N_{\mathcal{S}}
, there is
x\in X\setminus\bigcup_{y\in Y}N_{y}
. Then we have
Y\subseteq B_{x}
.

The other inequality
\cov(N_{\mathcal{S}})\leq\non(\mathcal{N})
can be proved in the dual manner.
∎

Lemma 4.47
.

Let
\Vdash_{\mathbb{K}_{\mathcal{S}}}\dot{r}\coloneqq\bigcup\{\stem(T):T\in\dot{G}\}\in\Omega^{\omega}
be (the canonical
\mathbb{K}_{\mathcal{S}}
-name of) a
\mathbb{K}_{\mathcal{S}}
-generic real. Then

\Vdash_{\mathbb{K}_{\mathcal{S}}}\dot{r}\text{ is $N_{\mathcal{S}}$-quasi-generic.}

Proof.

The proof is essentially the same as
4.36
.
Given
y\in 2^{\omega}
and
T\in\mathbb{K}_{\mathcal{S}}
, refine
T
to
T^{\prime}
such that for all
s\in T^{\prime}
, there is
N\geq\lvert\sigma\rvert
such that
\Spec_{T^{\prime}}(\sigma,N)
is
\mathcal{S}
-positive and disjoint from
h_{\lvert\sigma\rvert}^{-1}[I_{y}]
. Then
x\in[T^{\prime}]
implies
y\in B_{x}
. This means
\Vdash_{\mathbb{K}_{\mathcal{S}}}\dot{r}\in\Omega^{\omega}
is
N_{\mathcal{S}}
-quasi-generic.
∎

By
2.10
, we have:

Theorem 4.48
.

\non(K_{\mathcal{S}})=\max\{\mathfrak{b},\cov_{\omega}(\mathcal{N})\}
and
\cov(K_{\mathcal{S}})=\min\{\mathfrak{d},\non(\mathcal{N})\}
.

4.2.3.
Asymptotic density zero ideal

Pawlikowski
[
Paw00
]
studied
K_{\mathcal{Z}}
and proved:

Lemma 4.49

(
[
Paw00
, Lemma 2.3]
)
.

There is a (continuous) function
f\colon{\omega}^{\omega}\times 2^{\omega}\to{\omega}^{\omega}
such that for any
F\in K_{\mathcal{Z}}
, there is
g\in{\omega}^{\omega}
such that for any
x\in{\omega}^{\omega}
unbounded from
g
,
\left\{{y\in 2^{\omega}}:{f(x,y)\in F}\right\}\in\mathcal{E}
.

Corollary 4.50
.

\non(K_{\mathcal{Z}})\leq\max\{\mathfrak{b},\non(\mathcal{E})\}
and
\cov(K_{\mathcal{Z}})\geq\min\{\mathfrak{d},\cov(\mathcal{E})\}
.

Proof.

We will only prove the former inequality, as the other one follows dually. Let
B\subseteq{\omega}^{\omega}
and
E\subseteq 2^{\omega}
witness
\mathfrak{b}
and
\non(\mathcal{E})
, respectively. We shall show
f[B\times E]\notin K_{\mathcal{Z}}
where
f
is as above. If not, let
g\in{\omega}^{\omega}
as in Lemma
4.49
. Since
B
is unbounded, some
x\in B
satisfies
\mathcal{E}\ni\left\{{y\in 2^{\omega}}:{f(x,y)\in f[B\times E]}\right\}\supseteq E\notin\mathcal{E}
, a contradiction.
∎

We prove the
M_{\mathcal{Z}}
-version of
4.49
, in the following form:

Lemma 4.51
.

There is a (continuous) function
f\colon{\omega}^{\omega}\times 2^{\omega}\to{\omega}^{\omega}
such that for any
F\in M_{\mathcal{Z}}
, there is
g\colon\omega^{<\omega}\to\omega
such that for any
x\in{\omega}^{\omega}
,
x
is either adaptively dominated by
g
or
\left\{{y\in 2^{\omega}}:{f(x,y)\in F}\right\}\in\mathcal{E}
.

In the following proof, note that for any
A\in\mathcal{Z}
, there is
k_{0}<\omega
such that for any
k\geq k_{0}
,

\frac{|A\cap\left[k,k+2^{k}\right)|}{2^{k}}\leq\frac{1}{2}.

Also, for a set
u
of partial functions from
\omega
to
2
, define

[u]\coloneq\bigcup\{[\sigma]:\sigma\in u\}=\bigcup\{x\in 2^{\omega}:\exists\sigma\in u\,(\sigma\subseteq x)\}.

Proof.

Fix a function
e\colon\bigcup\{2^{K}:K\in[\omega]^{<\omega}\}\to\omega
such that for each
K\in[\omega]^{<\omega}
,
e
on
2^{K}
is bijective to
\left[|K|,|K|+2^{|K|}\right)
. For
x\in{\omega}^{\omega}
, let
\langle I_{x,i}:i\in\omega\rangle
denote the interval partition of
\omega
of each length
x(i)+1
. Define

f(x,y)\coloneq\langle e(y\mathpunct{\upharpoonright}I_{x,i}):i\in\omega\rangle.

Note that
f(x,y)=e(y\mathpunct{\upharpoonright}I_{x,i})\geq|I_{x,i}|=x(i)+1
. To see this
f
works, let
F\in M_{\mathcal{Z}}
be arbitrary and take
\phi\colon\omega^{<\omega}\to\mathcal{Z}
such that
F\subseteq M_{\phi}
. Since
\mathcal{Z}
is a P-ideal, there is
S\colon\omega\to\mathcal{Z}
such that
\phi(t)\subseteq^{*}S(i)
for any
i\in\omega
and
t\in\omega^{i}
, and take
h\colon\omega^{<\omega}\to\omega
such that
\phi(t)\subseteq S(i)\cup h(t)
for such
i
and
t
.
Choose
z\in{\omega}^{\omega}
such that for any
i\in\omega
and
k\geq z(i)
,

\frac{|S(i)\cap\left[k,k+2^{k}\right)|}{2^{k}}\leq\frac{1}{2}.

Let
m\colon\omega^{<\omega}\to\omega
be such that for any
x\in{\omega}^{\omega}
and
y\in 2^{\omega}
,

m(x\mathpunct{\upharpoonright}i)=\max\{h(f(x,y)\mathpunct{\upharpoonright}i):y\in 2^{\omega}\},

which is possible since only
x\mathpunct{\upharpoonright}i
and finitely many
y
are relevant here.

Define
g\colon\omega^{<\omega}\to\omega
by
g(t)\coloneqq\max\{z(i),m(t)\}
for
i\in\omega
and
t\in\omega^{i}
. Assume
x\in{\omega}^{\omega}
is not adaptively dominated by
g
, that is, there is
D\in[{\omega}]^{\omega}
such that for any
i\in D
,
x(i)>g(x\mathpunct{\upharpoonright}i)
. For
i\in\omega
put

S^{*}_{x,i}\coloneqq\{\sigma\in 2^{I_{x,i}}:e(\sigma)\in S(i)\}.

Since
e
on
2^{I_{x,i}}
is bijective to
\left[x(i)+1,x(i)+1+2^{x(i)+1}\right)
,

(4.2)

\text{ if }i\in D,~\frac{|S^{*}_{x,i}|}{2^{|I_{x,i}|}}\leq\frac{|S(i)\cap\left[x(i)+1,x(i)+1+2^{x(i)+1}\right)|}{2^{x(i)+1}}\leq\frac{1}{2},

since
x(i)+1>x(i)>g(x\mathpunct{\upharpoonright}i)\geq z(i)
.
Thus, it suffices to show that:

(4.3)

\{y\in 2^{\omega}:f(x,y)\in M_{\phi}\}\subseteq\bigcup_{j\in\omega}\bigcap_{j\leq i\in D}[S_{x,i}^{*}],

since the latter set is
F_{\sigma}
null in
2^{\omega}
by (
4.2
).
To prove (
4.3
), let
y\in 2^{\omega}
be such that
f(x,y)\in M_{\phi}
. Take
j\in\omega
such that for
i\geq j
,

f(x,y)(i)\in\phi(f(x,y)\mathpunct{\upharpoonright}i)\subseteq S(i)\cup h(f(x,y)\mathpunct{\upharpoonright}i).

By the choice of
m
, for such
i
, either
f(x,y)(i)\in S(i)
or
f(x,y)(i)\in m(x\mathpunct{\upharpoonright}i)
. If
j\leq i\in D
, then

f(x,y)(i)\geq x(i)+1>g(x\mathpunct{\upharpoonright}i)\geq m(x\mathpunct{\upharpoonright}i),

so the former case
f(x,y)(i)\in S(i)
holds, which means
f(x,y)(i)=e(y\mathpunct{\upharpoonright}I_{x,i})\in S(i)
. Thus,
y\mathpunct{\upharpoonright}I_{x,i}\in S^{*}_{x,i}
and hence
y\in[S_{x,i}^{*}]
.
Therefore, we obtain (
4.3
).
∎

Corollary 4.52
.

\non(M_{\mathcal{Z}})\leq\max\{\mathfrak{b},\non(\mathcal{E})\}
and
\cov(M_{\mathcal{Z}})\geq\min\{\mathfrak{d},\cov(\mathcal{E})\}
.

Proof.

We will only prove the first inequality. Let
B\subseteq{\omega}^{\omega}
and
E\subseteq 2^{\omega}
witness
\mathfrak{b}
and
\non(\mathcal{E})
, respectively. We shall show
F\coloneqq f(B\times E)\notin M_{\mathcal{Z}}
where
f
is as above. If not, let
g\colon\omega^{<\omega}\to\omega
as in Lemma
4.51
. By Lemma
4.7
, some
x\in B
is not adaptively dominated by
g
. However, it follows
\mathcal{E}\ni\left\{{y\in 2^{\omega}}:{f(x,y)\in F=f(B\times E)}\right\}\supseteq E\notin\mathcal{E}
, a contradiction.
∎

In the following lemma, we consider
\mathcal{Z}
as an ideal on
2^{<\omega}
, so for any
A\subseteq 2^{<\omega}
,

A\in\mathcal{Z}\iff\lim_{k\to\infty}\frac{|A\cap 2^{k}|}{2^{k}}=0.

Recall the concatenating function
\mathsf{concat}
in
4.32
.

Lemma 4.53
.

Let
\Vdash_{\mathbb{M}_{\mathcal{Z}}}\dot{r}\coloneqq\bigcup\{\stem(T):T\in\dot{G}\}\in(2^{<\omega})^{\omega}
be (the canonical
\mathbb{M}_{\mathcal{Z}}
-name of) an
\mathbb{M}_{\mathcal{Z}}
-generic real. Then
\Vdash_{\mathbb{M}_{\mathcal{Z}}}\mathsf{concat}(\dot{r})\in 2^{\omega}
is
\mathcal{E}
-quasi-generic.

Proof.

We prove that for any tree
T\subseteq 2^{<\omega}
such that
[T]\subseteq 2^{\omega}
is null,
\Vdash\mathsf{concat}(\dot{r})\notin[T]
. To see this, let
p\in\mathbb{M}_{\mathcal{Z}}
be arbitrary and we may assume that every node of
p
is either non-splitting or
\mathcal{Z}^{+}
-splitting. Let
\sigma
be the stem of
p
and
A\coloneqq\suc_{p}(\sigma)\subseteq 2^{<\omega}
. Since
A\in\mathcal{Z}^{+}
, there is
\varepsilon\in(0,1)\cap\mathbb{Q}
such that for infinitely many
k<\omega
:

(4.4)

\frac{|A\cap 2^{k}|}{2^{k}}\geq\varepsilon.

Put
s\coloneqq\mathsf{concat}(\sigma)\in 2^{<\omega}
. Assume on the contrary that for infinitely many
k<\omega
:

(4.5)

\frac{|T\cap[s]\cap 2^{|s|+k}|}{2^{k}}\geq\varepsilon.

Since the left-hand side is non-increasing with respect to
k
, (
4.5
) holds for all
k<\omega
. However, it implies
\frac{\mu([T])}{\mu([s])}\geq\varepsilon>0
where
\mu
denotes the Lebesgue measure on
2^{\omega}
, which contradicts that
[T]
is null. Hence, there exists
k_{0}<\omega
such that for all
k\geq k_{0}
,

(4.6)

\frac{|T\cap[s]\cap 2^{|s|+k}|}{2^{k}}<\varepsilon.

Take
k\geq k_{0}
satisfying (
4.4
) and put
\Sigma\coloneqq\{\sigma^{\frown}a:a\in A\cap 2^{k}\}
. Note that
\mathsf{concat}
is one-to-one on
\Sigma
and
\mathsf{concat}[\Sigma]\subseteq[s]\cap 2^{|s|+k}
. By (
4.4
) and (
4.6
),

|T\cap[s]\cap 2^{|s|+k}|<\varepsilon\cdot 2^{k}\leq|A\cap 2^{k}|=|\mathsf{concat}[\Sigma]|,

so we have
\mathsf{concat}[\Sigma]\nsubseteq T\cap[s]\cap 2^{|s|+k}
.
Take
a\in A\cap 2^{k}
such that

\mathsf{concat}(\sigma^{\frown}a)\notin T\cap[s]\cap 2^{|s|+k}

and let
p^{\prime}\coloneqq p\cap[\sigma^{\frown}a]
. Then, we have
p^{\prime}\Vdash\mathsf{concat}(\dot{r})\notin[T]
, since
p^{\prime}\Vdash\mathsf{concat}(\dot{r})\mathpunct{\upharpoonright}(|s|+k)=\mathsf{concat}(\sigma^{\frown}a)\notin T
.
∎

Theorem 4.54
.

\non(M_{\mathcal{Z}})\geq\non(\mathcal{E})
and
\cov(M_{\mathcal{Z}})\leq\cov(\mathcal{E})
. Therefore,

\non(M_{\mathcal{Z}})=\max\{\mathfrak{b},\non(\mathcal{E})\}\text{ and }\cov(M_{\mathcal{Z}})=\min\{\mathfrak{d},\cov(\mathcal{E})\}.

Proof.

It follows from
4.52
,
4.53
and
2.10
.
∎

As a corollary of
4.54
, we obtain new bounds of
\non^{*}(\mathcal{Z})
and
\cov^{*}(\mathcal{Z})
. We quickly recall previous results about these cardinal invariants.

Theorem 4.55

(Hernández-Hernández–Hrušák
[
HH07
]
)
.

\displaystyle\min\{\mathfrak{d},\cov(\mathcal{N})\}\leq

\displaystyle\non^{*}(\mathcal{Z})\leq\max\{\mathfrak{d},\non(\mathcal{N})\},

\displaystyle\min\{\mathfrak{b},\cov(\mathcal{N})\}\leq

\displaystyle\cov^{*}(\mathcal{Z})\leq\max\{\mathfrak{b},\non(\mathcal{N})\}.

Theorem 4.56

(Raghavan–Shelah
[
RS17
]
)
.

\mathfrak{b}\leq\non^{*}(\mathcal{Z})
and
\cov^{*}(\mathcal{Z})\leq\mathfrak{d}
.

The lower bounds for
\non^{*}(\mathcal{Z})
and the upper bounds for
\cov^{*}(\mathcal{Z})
above were improved by Raghavan:

Theorem 4.57

(Raghavan
[
Rag20
]
)
.

\min\{\mathfrak{d},\mathfrak{u}\}\leq\non^{*}(\mathcal{Z})\text{ and }\cov^{*}(\mathcal{Z})\leq\max\{\mathfrak{b},\mathfrak{s}(\mathfrak{pr})\},

where
\mathfrak{u}
denotes the ultrafilter number and
\mathfrak{s}(\mathfrak{pr})
is a variant of the splitting number
\mathfrak{s}
introduced in
[
Rag20
]
.
6
6

6

By definition,
\mathfrak{s}\leq\mathfrak{s}(\mathfrak{pr})
but it is unknown to be distinguishable from
\mathfrak{s}
.

Theorem 4.58
.

\non(\mathcal{E})\leq\non^{*}(\mathcal{Z})
and
\cov^{*}(\mathcal{Z})\leq\cov(\mathcal{E})
. Also,
\cov^{*}(\mathcal{Z})\leq\non^{*}(\mathcal{Z})
.

Proof.

Since
\mathcal{Z}
is a P-ideal,
\non^{*}_{\omega}(\mathcal{Z})=\non^{*}(\mathcal{Z})
. By
4.54
,
4.14
and
4.56
, we have

\non(\mathcal{E})\leq\non(M_{\mathcal{Z}})\leq\max\{\mathfrak{b},\non^{*}_{\omega}(\mathcal{Z})\}=\max\{\mathfrak{b},\non^{*}(\mathcal{Z})\}=\non^{*}(\mathcal{Z}).

Similarly,

\cov(\mathcal{E})\geq\cov(M_{\mathcal{Z}})\geq\min\{\mathfrak{d},\cov^{*}(\mathcal{Z})\}=\cov^{*}(\mathcal{Z}).

Since
\cov(\mathcal{E})\leq\mathfrak{r}\leq\mathfrak{u}
, using
4.56
as well, we have

\cov^{*}(\mathcal{Z})\leq\min\{\mathfrak{d},\cov(\mathcal{E})\}\leq\min\{\mathfrak{d},\mathfrak{u}\}\leq\non^{*}(\mathcal{Z}).

∎

We finish this section by remarking that the cardinal invariants
\min\{\mathfrak{d},\mathfrak{u}\}
and
\non(\mathcal{E})
are independent, so are
\max\{\mathfrak{b},\mathfrak{s}(\mathfrak{pr})\}
and
\cov(\mathcal{E})
:

•

\min\{\mathfrak{d},\mathfrak{u}\}>\non(\mathcal{E})
holds in the Cohen model.

•

\min\{\mathfrak{d},\mathfrak{u}\}\leq\mathfrak{u}<\mathfrak{s}\leq\non(\mathcal{E})
holds in the Blass-Shelah model(
[
BS87
]
). (Or:
\min\{\mathfrak{d},\mathfrak{u}\}\leq\mathfrak{d}<\non(\mathcal{E})
holds in the model constructed in
[
She92
]
(see also
[
Bre95
, Section 3.4, Theorem 1]
, it is easy to see
\mathfrak{e}_{ubd}\leq\mathfrak{e}_{fin}=\mathfrak{e}_{2}\leq\non(\mathcal{E})
).)

•

\max\{\mathfrak{b},\mathfrak{s}(\mathfrak{pr})\}\geq\mathfrak{b}>\cov(\mathcal{E})
holds in the Laver model.

•

\max\{\mathfrak{b},\mathfrak{s}(\mathfrak{pr})\}\leq\mathfrak{d}<\cov(\mathcal{N})\leq\cov(\mathcal{E})
holds in the random model.

5.
Consistency results

In this section, we study consistency results for cardinal invariants associated with the
\sigma
-ideals
M_{\mathscr{I}}
and
K_{\mathscr{I}}
. We conclude by constructing a model of Cichoń’s maximum in
D
.

5.1.
Forcing theory

We list the necessary facts on relational systems, and some preservation theorems that will be used to prove our consistency results.

Definition 5.1
.

A triple
\mathbf{R}=\langle X,Y,\sqsubset\rangle
is called a
relational system
if
X
and
Y
are non-empty sets and
\sqsubset
is a relation from
X
to
Y
. Elements of
X
are called
challenges
, and elements of
Y
are
responses
. We say that
x
is met by
y
if
x\sqsubset y
.

•

A subset
F\subseteq X
is
\mathbf{R}
-unbounded
if no response meets all challenges in
F
, i.e.,
\neg\exists y\in Y~\forall x\in F~(x\sqsubset y)
.

•

A subset
F\subseteq Y
is
\mathbf{R}
-dominating
if every challenge is met by some response in
F
, i.e.,
\forall x\in X~\exists y\in F~(x\sqsubset y)
.

•

We say that
\mathbf{R}
is
non-trivial
if
X
is
\mathbf{R}
-unbounded and
Y
is
\mathbf{R}
-dominating.

•

Two cardinal invariants associated to
\mathbf{R}
are introduced as follows:

\displaystyle\mathfrak{b}(\mathbf{R})

\displaystyle=\min\{|F|\colon F\subseteq X\text{ is }\mathbf{R}\text{-unbounded}\},

\displaystyle\mathfrak{d}(\mathbf{R})

\displaystyle=\min\{|F|\colon F\subseteq Y\text{ is }\mathbf{R}\text{-dominating}\}.

Hereafter, we assume that a relational system is always non-trivial.

Definition 5.2
.

Let
\mathbf{R}=\langle X,Y,\sqsubset\rangle
be a relational system. The
dual of
\mathbf{R}
is the relational system
\mathbf{R}^{\bot}\coloneq\langle Y,X,\sqsubset^{\bot}\rangle
, where
y\sqsubset^{\bot}x
if and only if
\lnot(x\sqsubset y)
.

Definition 5.3
.

Let
\mathbf{R}=\langle X,Y,\sqsubset\rangle,\mathbf{R}^{\prime}=\langle X^{\prime},Y^{\prime},\sqsubset^{\prime}~\rangle
be two relational systems. We say that
(\Phi_{-},\Phi_{+})\colon\mathbf{R}\rightarrow\mathbf{R}^{\prime}
is a
Tukey connection from
\mathbf{R}
into
\mathbf{R}^{\prime}
if
\Phi_{-}\colon X\rightarrow X^{\prime}
and
\Phi_{+}\colon Y^{\prime}\rightarrow Y
are functions such that

\forall x\in X~\forall y^{\prime}\in Y^{\prime}~\Phi_{-}(x)\sqsubset^{\prime}y^{\prime}\Rightarrow x\sqsubset\Phi_{+}(y^{\prime}).

We write
\mathbf{R}\preceq_{T}\mathbf{R}^{\prime}
if there is a Tukey connection from
\mathbf{R}
into
\mathbf{R}^{\prime}
and call
\preceq_{T}
the Tukey order. Tukey equivalence is defined by
\mathbf{R}\cong_{T}\mathbf{R}^{\prime}
if and only if
\mathbf{R}\preceq_{T}\mathbf{R}^{\prime}\land\mathbf{R}^{\prime}\preceq_{T}\mathbf{R}
.

Fact 5.4
.

Let
\mathbf{R},\mathbf{R}^{\prime}
be relational systems.

(1)

\mathbf{R}\preceq_{T}\mathbf{R}^{\prime}
implies
(\mathbf{R}^{\prime})^{\bot}\preceq_{T}\mathbf{R}^{\bot}
.

(2)

\mathbf{R}\preceq_{T}\mathbf{R}^{\prime}
implies
\mathfrak{b}(\mathbf{R}^{\prime})\leq\mathfrak{b}(\mathbf{R})
and
\mathfrak{d}(\mathbf{R})\leq\mathfrak{d}(\mathbf{R}^{\prime})
.

(3)

\mathfrak{b}(\mathbf{R}^{\bot})=\mathfrak{d}(\mathbf{R})
and
\mathfrak{d}(\mathbf{R}^{\bot})=\mathfrak{b}(\mathbf{R})
.

We will list several basic lemmas from
[
CM22
]
, where the role of Tukey connections in forcing Cichoń’s maximum is clarified.

Definition 5.5
.

For an ideal
I
on a set
X
, we define two relational systems:

\displaystyle\bar{I}

\displaystyle=\langle I,I,\subseteq\rangle

\displaystyle C_{I}

\displaystyle=\langle X,I,\in\rangle.

We write
\mathbf{R}\preceq_{T}I
to mean
\mathbf{R}\preceq_{T}\bar{I}
(we do the same for
\succeq_{T}
and
\cong_{T}
).
Note that we have
\mathfrak{b}(\bar{I})=\add(I),\mathfrak{d}(\bar{I})=\cof(I)
and
\mathfrak{b}(C_{I})=\non(I),~\mathfrak{d}(C_{I})=\cov(I)
.

In this section,
\theta
will always be a regular uncountable cardinal.

Fact 5.6
.

Let
A
be a set of size
\geq\theta
. Also, let
\mathbf{R}
be a relational system.

(1)

If
